# RocksDB — technique cards (190)

Each card is exactly 7 lines. Fetch one with: grep -A 6 "^### rocksdb-NNN " rocksdb.md

### rocksdb-001 — Exploit sorted order: binary search, and carry the position forward between levels
Lens: algorithms · Source: RocksDB
Principle: If a collection is already sorted, never scan it linearly. Binary-search it, and when you search several related sorted lists in turn, use what one search found to narrow the next one (fractional cascading).
Why it is faster: A linear scan of n items costs O(n) comparisons per query. Binary search costs O(log n), so a loop that queried a sorted list once per input item goes from O(N·S) to O(N log S). In a layered structure each element overlaps only a few elements of the next layer, so a hint from layer L shrinks layer L+1's search to a tiny window. The sort itself should happen once, when the data changes, not on every read. For very small lists (a handful of items) a linear scan can still win on branch prediction.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: look up a timestamp, price tier or CSS breakpoint in a sorted array with binary search instead of `Array.find`; resolve layered config by narrowing from the previous layer. • Data / databases: MVCC visibility checks against a sorted snapshot list; partition pruning over time-sorted files. • Native / systems: tile pyramids and multi-level page indexes where a parent position bounds the child search.
Watch out: keep the data actually sorted, and sort at change time, not query time. Off-by-one termination bugs in hand-rolled searches can silently make them linear.
Seen in RocksDB: cb7a00227f 2012-11-04 `GetOverlappingInputs` switched from a linear scan to binary search: 16000 to 4600 us per 1000 iterations. • 9b87a2bae8 2012-11-06 Remembered the hint index between calls: 4500 to 3900 us. d7d43ae21a later fixed a backward-search termination bug that was "too slow for large databases". • 009034cf12 2013-01-29 Histogram bucket lookup used a precomputed value-to-bucket map instead of a linear search over bucket limits. 97b30dee5b later dropped the map and used `std::lower_bound` on the bucket array. • bc5dd19b14 2013-12-09 Presorted L0 files once when a version was built, instead of on every Get. • 0f2d768191 2014-04-21 `FileIndexer`: each comparison at one level narrows the candidate range at the next, and non-overlapping L0 files are skipped. QPS +7% with light writes, +13% with heavy writes. b278ae8e50 did the same for `ForwardIterator::Seek`, cutting comparisons from 2B to 1.3B. • d4540654e9 2015-04-29 `ApproximateSize` binary-searched the start file in sorted levels and stopped early, after profiling showed it as a bottleneck. 9aa011fa36 optimized range lookups per level the same way. • 75ca13875c 2018-09-27 `FindFile`/`FindFileInRange` rewritten on `std::lower_bound`. a1f6142f38 reduced the L0 expansion path in `GetOverlappingInputs` to expected O(n). • 32b4d4ad47 2018-10-15 `findEarliestVisibleSnapshot`, called for every key during flush and compaction, used `std::lower_bound` on the sorted snapshot list instead of a scan. Big win with many snapshots.

### rocksdb-002 — Start the next search where the last one ended (fingers, hints, cursors)
Lens: algorithms · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Successive operations usually touch nearby keys. Remember the last position (a finger, insert hint or cursor) and start the next search from there instead of from the root, falling back to a full search only when the target is far away.
Why it is faster: A search from the root of a tree or skip list costs O(log n) dependent memory hops. Starting from the previous position costs O(log d), where d is the distance between successive keys, often O(1) for sequential inserts, tailing reads and sorted batches. The remembered path must be kept valid under concurrent modification, and a mispredicted hint should fall back cheaply. When accesses are random the hint check is pure overhead (RocksDB measured a slowdown at batch size 1).
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: the "rightmost leaf" fast path for auto-increment B-tree inserts; per-series append position in time-series ingestion; merge-join cursors. • Web / backend: a cursor hint in a text-editor rope or timeline scrubber; sorted batch lookups that reuse the last B-tree leaf. • Native / systems: `std::set::insert(hint, v)`; finger trees; sweep-line algorithms in geometry.
Watch out: the cached path is shared mutable state; invalidate it correctly under concurrency. Check the hint cheaply and bail out fast when access is random.
Seen in RocksDB: 90b2924fb2 2012-05-04 Skip list kept `prev_[kMaxHeight]`, the last insert path, and checked it first; sequential inserts had been CPU bound. 08740b15a4 fixed it to update all levels. • 88edfd90ae 2014-09-23 `LookaheadIterator`: Seek first takes up to `lookahead` linear steps from the previous node. fillseekseq 0.389 to 0.153 us/op. • df5eeb85ca 2016-11-13 `InlineSkipList::InsertWithHint`, with one hint per key prefix (1ea79a78c9) so several interleaved sequential streams each get their own finger. • dfb6fe6755 2016-11-22 Unified the sequential cache, concurrent insertion and external hints into one `Splice`-based insert. 2c1db5ea51 fixed invalidation of the cached path during concurrent multi-level inserts. • b104b87814 2017-01-05 Range-tombstone lookups kept forward, backward, binary-search and full-scan positioning modes, stepping from the last position when queries arrive in order. • 4479dff208 2019-05-01 Reseek shortcut: if the new target lies inside the current file's key range, skip `FindFile`; if it lies between the current key and the block's index key, skip the index seek and block reload. 9fad3e21eb tried the same for `MergingIterator` children and was reverted (846e05005d) after a prefix-seek correctness bug. • 1a928c22a0 2019-09-12 Per-write-batch insert hints for concurrent memtable writes. fillseq with 8 threads and batch 100: 2,681,039 to 3,086,426 qps; batch 1000 with 4 threads: 1,839,877 to 3,231,048. Slower at batch size 1. • c66c14258f 2026-03-16 Finger search for sorted MultiGet batches in the memtable (sort, search with finger, unscramble): +0.2% to +4.1% at small batches on a 2M-key memtable.

### rocksdb-003 — Give the common operation its own direct path instead of reusing generic machinery
Lens: algorithms · Source: RocksDB
Principle: A general abstraction (build a full iterator stack, then seek; a merge over N inputs) does far more work than the special case needs. Write a dedicated path for the hot, simple case, and collapse layers that have degenerated to a single input.
Why it is faster: The generic path pays setup costs (allocating iterators, building heaps), per-step indirection (virtual calls, heap operations) and work beyond the answer (positioning every child). A point lookup only needs "search newest to oldest, stop at the first hit". A merge of one input needs no merge. The cost is more code paths to keep correct and consistent.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: a dedicated `getById` that bypasses the ORM query builder; skip a middleware chain when only one handler is registered. • Data / databases: single-shard queries that skip the distributed merge step; single-key commands next to scan commands. • Native / systems: unwrap `Promise.all([x])`-style wrappers; specialize a k-way merge for k = 1 and k = 2.
Watch out: two paths must return identical results; keep shared semantics (snapshots, deletes, merges) in one place and test both paths.
Seen in RocksDB: ccf0fcd5c2 2011-06-22 Implemented `Get()` directly instead of through a merged iterator: readrandom +15-30%. It also stopped reading a file per level when none overlapped. • 33042669f6 2014-02-11 Memtable Get used a callback inside the rep instead of allocating an iterator: +3%. 2faf49d5f1 replaced the callback function pointers with a `GetContext` object and a fast path in `SaveValue`. • 749b849a34 2022-09-21 With one memtable and no SST files, `DBIter` wraps the memtable iterator directly instead of a single-child merging iterator. seek+30 nexts: 2.41M to 3.01M ops/s; seek+1000: 102.6K to 128.9K.

### rocksdb-004 — Append to a contiguous array and sort once instead of maintaining an ordered tree
Lens: algorithms · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When all inserts happen before the reads, or the set is small and rarely changes, collect items in a contiguous vector and sort (and deduplicate) once. Search it with binary search instead of a node-based ordered container.
Why it is faster: Red-black trees and multisets pay one allocation and several pointer hops per insert, and every lookup chases pointers across cache lines. A sorted array is built with one bulk sort, is searched with cache-friendly binary search, and is smaller. The trade-off: inserting into a sorted array after it is built costs O(n), so this fits build-then-read phases.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: collect events, then sort once before render instead of a sorted-set library; static lookup tables as sorted arrays. • Embedded: sorted const arrays for routing or config tables, searched in place from flash. • Data / databases: bulk-load a B-tree from sorted input; flat maps (Boost/Abseil `flat_map`) for small read-mostly maps.
Watch out: interleaved inserts and reads after the build make this O(n) per insert; switch back to a tree or a log-structured buffer if writes continue.
Seen in RocksDB: 74781a0c49 2013-08-22 `VectorRep` memtable for bulk loads: append, then sort once when read. • e0abec1580 2015-12-22 Subcompaction boundaries computed with `std::sort` + `std::unique` on a vector instead of a `std::set` with a `std::function` comparator. • 7dd1641048 2018-10-11 Uncollapsed range-tombstone map changed from `std::multiset` to a vector sorted on first iterator creation: `AddTombstones` 1822.61 to 199.26 us, first `ShouldDelete` 94.5 to 38.9 us with 5000 tombstones. • 320d9a8e8a 2022-02-09 Blob file metadata moved from `std::map` to a preallocated sorted vector: a fillseq run went from 2986 to 2611 s, with less DB-mutex hold time.

### rocksdb-005 — Pick the container for the access pattern you actually have
Lens: algorithms · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Re-check container choices when access patterns change: a priority queue fed in order is just a FIFO, a list searched by key should be a hash map, and a node-based hash map can be replaced by a flat, probed one.
Why it is faster: A container's cost model only matches reality if its assumptions hold. Heap operations are O(log n) wasted when items arrive sorted (a deque is O(1)). Scanning a list to find one entry is O(n) and turns pathological at scale. Node-based hash maps allocate per entry and chase pointers, while open-addressing maps probe contiguous memory.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: a `Set` for "is this id already queued" instead of `array.includes`; ring buffers for time-ordered event queues. • Native / systems: `absl::flat_hash_map` / hashbrown instead of chained maps. • Data / databases: job schedulers and lock managers keyed by id rather than scanning wait lists.
Watch out: the cheaper container usually relies on an invariant (in-order arrival, unique keys); assert it, or a later change silently breaks correctness.
Seen in RocksDB: 773f914a40 2019-06-11 `PreparedHeap` moved from `priority_queue` to a deque because prepared sequence numbers had become monotonically increasing. • a68d814570 2019-09-17 The file purge queue changed from a list to an `unordered_map` keyed by file number. Before, with 140,000 SST files and about 1,000 pending purges, a full scan blocked for about 2 minutes. • efd035164b 2022-04-13 Swapped folly's SIMD-probed `F14FastMap` in for the heaviest `std::unordered_map` users behind a compile-time alias.

### rocksdb-006 — Merge many sorted streams with a purpose-built heap: lazy build, replace-top, cached child comparison, one K-way pass
Lens: algorithms · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Merge N sorted inputs with a binary heap rather than a linear min-scan, build the heap only when it is first needed, and update the top in place. Exploit the fact that the root is replaced repeatedly, and merge all K inputs in one pass rather than K-1 pairwise merges.
Why it is faster: A linear min-scan is O(N) per output item; a heap is O(log N). Generic priority queues only offer pop and push, two sift operations, while replacing the top needs only one sift-down. When the same source wins repeatedly, the comparison between the root's two children rarely changes and can be cached. Building lazily saves the whole setup when an iterator is discarded right after Seek. Pairwise merging re-touches every item log K or K times; one K-way pass touches each once.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: external merge sort; merging posting lists or shard results in a search engine; LSM merges in any store. • Web / backend: top-K feed assembly from many shards; merging sorted log files. • Native / systems: timer heaps that re-arm the same timer (replace-top); event-loop schedulers.
Watch out: custom heaps need careful invariants (RocksDB reverted the first version once). Lazy building adds a branch on every operation.
Seen in RocksDB: 3f7af03a2d 2012-12-26 Priority queue in the merging iterator instead of scanning all children. • 15b31b57df 2013-11-18 `MergingIterator::Seek` builds its min-heap lazily, which pays off for prefix-filter workloads where most iterators are dropped early. • e1c99e10c1 2015-07-06 Custom `BinaryHeap` with in-place top update replaced `std::priority_queue` (re-landing b6655a679d after revert b7a2369fb2). • 4a21b1402c 2016-12-01 `root_cmp_cache_` remembers which child of the root won: key comparisons for a full DB scan fell from 2.0M to 1.0M, readseq 2.96M to 3.24M ops/s. • 56715350d9 2022-10-27 Range-tombstone keys in the iterator heap use `replace_top` instead of pop + push: readseq 1.43M to 1.94M ops/s (+36%). • d34712e0ac 2024-04-16 The coalescing (multi-column-family) iterator gathers every same-key item from all K sources and merges them in one K-way pass instead of K-1 two-way merges, using non-owning views of the columns.

### rocksdb-007 — Skip comparisons the search, or the data's metadata, already settled
Lens: algorithms · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Each comparison in a search either learns something new or repeats what is already known. Track what earlier steps proved (an upper bound, a node already rejected, "this is the oldest version") and skip comparisons whose outcome is implied.
Why it is faster: Key comparisons are often the dominant cost: they decode variable-length keys, run user comparators and miss the cache. In a skip list, descending a level often reaches the same node just rejected. In a block search, the next restart key is already known to be larger than the target. In a versioned store, a key with sequence number 0 cannot have an older duplicate. Each skipped comparison removes a call and a likely cache miss.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: B-tree descents with expensive collations; MVCC scans that skip dedup checks when a run is known unique. • Web / backend: sorting with locale-aware comparators (`Intl.Collator`) where each compare is costly. • Native / systems: galloping search in merge routines; sentinel-bounded sorted-array lookups.
Watch out: every skipped comparison relies on an invariant; if metadata can lie (for example seqno 0 assigned too early), results become wrong rather than slow.
Seen in RocksDB: 1ae27113c7 2015-08-11 Skip list search tracks `last_bigger` and skips the compare when the next node is the one just rejected, and returns at once on equality. About 1/4 of comparisons skipped at branching factor 4; CPU-bound fillrandom +7%. • 6bee36a786 2018-11-14 Fragmented range tombstones are stored as distinct key ranges with index ranges into a separate seqno array, so binary search compares only distinct keys when tombstones overlap. • 25d81e4577 2019-05-09 `DBIter` skips the user-key equality check after a key with sequence number 0. Comparisons per key in readseq 2.981 to 2.165, and zero on a fully compacted DB. 59244447e3 zeroes the sequence number of even the last key at the bottom level so more keys qualify. • e6be168aa5 2020-06-10 `FindKeyAfterBinarySeek` stops the linear scan at the next restart point without comparing, and skips it entirely after a hash-index hit: `user_key_comparison_count` 19,399,529 to 18,431,498. • 82611ee25a 2020-07-09 `BinarySeek` brought down to log2(N+1) comparisons by widening the range by one: 28,881,965 to 27,823,245 comparisons.

### rocksdb-008 — Push query bounds down and check them per container, not per item
Lens: algorithms · Source: RocksDB
Principle: If the caller gave a range limit, test it against coarse summaries (a block's index key, a file's key range or properties) and stop or skip whole containers. Check every input of a merge against the bound, including auxiliary streams.
Why it is faster: A container whose maximum is inside the bound cannot hold an out-of-bound item, so per-item checks inside it are wasted. One whose minimum is past the bound need not be read at all, which saves I/O as well as CPU. Doing the check once per block or file and carrying a "may be out of bound" flag replaces millions of comparisons with a few.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Parquet row-group min/max to skip predicate evaluation; push LIMIT and range predicates into every branch of a UNION. • Web / backend: keyset pagination that checks limits per page; time-bucketed log queries that skip whole buckets. • Embedded: sensor logs stored in chunks with min/max headers, scanned by header first.
Watch out: container summaries must be exact or conservative; the RocksDB history shows several reverts from bound checks that were subtly wrong.
Seen in RocksDB: c6c770a1ac 2016-04-28 Iteration ends as soon as `prefix_same_as_start` leaves the prefix, avoiding long tombstone walks. • a30a696034 2017-05-05 `KeyReachedUpperBound` compares the index separator with `iterate_upper_bound`, so the next data block is never read when it cannot contain in-range keys. • 7891af8b53 2017-10-17 `ReadOptions::table_filter` skips whole SST files based on table properties; a CockroachDB incremental backup went from hours to seconds. • 92ee3350e0 2018-06-19 The block iterator is kept after hitting the bound, so repeated seeks into the same block don't re-fetch it. • f3a7847598 2019-05-17 `DBIter` bound checks moved to per-file and per-block granularity via `may_be_out_of_upper_bound` (reverted by ba64a4cf52, re-landed as 662ce62044; an earlier attempt f1239d5f10 was reverted by 0f48e56f96). • 41c328fe57 2020-08-04 Fixed a regression that had brought back per-key bound checks, using an enum for the block's bound state. • 534fb06dd3 2022-11-23 Range-tombstone streams in the merging iterator also stop at `iterate_upper_bound`; a production team saw lower CPU.

### rocksdb-009 — Step linearly for a bounded number of items, then jump with one seek
Lens: algorithms · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When a scan must skip a run of irrelevant entries (obsolete versions, deleted items), step one by one only up to a threshold, then issue one targeted seek past the run.
Why it is faster: Stepping costs O(1) per item but O(n) for a long run; a seek costs O(log n) regardless of run length. A small linear budget keeps the cheap path for short runs and caps the damage of long ones. The threshold is a tunable trade-off.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: skipping tombstones or dead MVCC versions in scans; skipping deleted rows in columnar scans. • Native / systems: galloping search in TimSort merges (linear first, then exponential jumps). • Web / backend: cursor-based feeds that jump past large hidden or muted runs.
Watch out: too small a threshold reseeks on short runs and loses the cache locality of stepping.
Seen in RocksDB: 197034e4c3 2013-07-28 `max_sequential_skip_in_iterations`: after that many skipped versions of the same key, the iterator reseeks. Made dynamic in 574028679b. • 236d4c67e9 2016-11-28 The skip-count reseek also applies inside `Seek()`, not only after a delete; production had shown high CPU in `MergingIterator::Next` under `DBIter::Seek` for heavily overwritten keys.

### rocksdb-010 — Check only the candidates, not the whole universe
Lens: algorithms · Source: RocksDB
Principle: To decide whether K candidates are garbage, check each one against the live set directly. Don't build the full live set or scan every directory entry, and don't track "everything" when a refcount reaching zero already tells you what died.
Why it is faster: Materializing the full live set costs O(total) every time, often under a lock. Checking candidates costs O(K × lookup). Driving deletion from refcounts removes the scan altogether. Hash sets and vectors are cheaper than tree sets for the remaining bookkeeping.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: blob or chunk GC in object stores; "is this blob referenced" checks in content-addressed storage. • Web / backend: cache invalidation by key lookup instead of rebuilding a full index; cleaning build caches incrementally. • Native / systems: refcount-driven resource release instead of periodic full sweeps.
Watch out: candidate tracking must not miss anything, or files leak; keep an occasional full sweep as a safety net.
Seen in RocksDB: 013e9ebbf1 2013-04-11 Obsolete-file collection moved from `std::set` to a vector with one allocation plus an `unordered_set`, with directory listing outside the mutex (a p99 fix). • 0e422308aa 2013-07-16 Deleted a WAL file right after its memtable flushed, instead of finding it later by scan. • 29c931f70b 2013-11-14 Avoided populating the live-file set when not needed; 1510339e52 further reduced the work. • d5de22dc09 2014-03-10 Purged only when `HaveSomethingToDelete`. • 0acc738810 2014-12-22 Excluded moved files from obsolete candidates, so the live-file set was no longer needed at all. • 356f8c5d81 2022-05-25 `FindObsoleteFiles()` checks each candidate file against live versions directly (O(m·k)) instead of building the set of all live files (O(m·n)) under the DB mutex.

### rocksdb-011 — Sort only the top K you will use
Lens: algorithms · Source: RocksDB
Principle: If only the first few results of an ordering are consumed, use a partial sort or heap selection instead of a full sort.
Why it is faster: A full sort is O(n log n); `partial_sort` is O(n log k). When a picker looks at the best handful of candidates out of thousands, almost all sorting work is wasted.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: leaderboards and "top 10" widgets (`heapq.nsmallest`, quickselect). • Data / databases: ORDER BY ... LIMIT via a bounded heap. • Embedded: picking the N strongest signals from a large sample buffer.
Watch out: if K later grows toward n, or the consumer starts reading past K, a full sort becomes cheaper and simpler.
Seen in RocksDB: 95dda37858 2012-10-31 Only the first few files per level were sorted by size for compaction picking (and the sort moved out of the mutex): 1500 to 1700 writes/s. • 246d469750 2022-06-24 `SortFileByOverlappingRatio()` uses `std::partial_sort` for the top 50 files: fillseq +11% with many files.

### rocksdb-012 — Approximate when exactness isn't needed, using the structure's own shape
Lens: algorithms · Source: RocksDB
Principle: For estimates (sizes, counts), sum cheap coarse units first and skip refinement when the possible error is negligible. Sample along the data structure's own levels rather than walking it, which can be both faster and more accurate than a crude method.
Why it is faster: Exact answers often require reading or walking data proportional to the range. Whole-file sums come from metadata at no I/O cost; partial "dives" into edge files can be skipped if they are small relative to the total. A skip list's upper levels are a ready-made sample: counting at the level where the range spans enough nodes gives a logarithmic-cost estimate with low variance.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: "about 1,200 results" pagination counts; HyperLogLog for distinct visitors. • Data / databases: query-planner row estimates from B-tree inner-node fan-out or histograms. • Native / systems: progress bars and capacity planning from metadata sums.
Watch out: document the error bound; callers that use an estimate for correctness decisions (quota enforcement) need exact numbers.
Seen in RocksDB: 6b7fcc0d5f 2019-07-23 Calls `ApproximateOffsetOf` without creating an iterator, keeping the index iterator on the stack. c2404d9928 builds the index iterator once for both endpoints. • 4834dab578 2019-07-31 `ApproximateSize` sums full files first and skips partial estimation of small edge files within an error margin (`SizeApproximationOptions`, 9625a2bc2b). • 239d17a19c 2020-12-18 Replaced an exact `ApproximateNumEntries` computation with faster implementation-specific estimates. • dd23e84cad 2024-10-02 `GetApproximateMemTableStats` reimplemented by sampling skip-list level fan-out: for an expected count of 1000 the standard deviation fell from 26,587 to 198, and the call got faster (3.711 to 2.694 us/op), about the cost of two point lookups.

### rocksdb-013 — Choose the in-memory index by workload: hash buckets that adapt, or hash with a lazily sorted view
Lens: algorithms · Source: RocksDB
Principle: An ordered structure is not always required for the hot path. When access is by key or prefix, use hash buckets, upgrade a bucket to an ordered structure only when it grows, and build sorted order only when an ordered scan actually asks for it.
Why it is faster: A global skip list pays O(log n) dependent hops for every insert and point read. Hash buckets give O(1) access. Small buckets stay as linked lists (least memory); converting a bucket to a skip list past a threshold bounds the worst case. Sorting on demand moves O(n log n) work off the write path and into a background job. The cost is slower or unavailable ordered iteration and a bucket count that must scale with data.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: Java 8 `HashMap` turning large bins into trees; adaptive containers in compilers. • Web / backend: a session store or leaderboard kept as a hash map with an on-demand sorted snapshot. • Data / databases: write buffers in time-series or LSM engines whose reads are mostly by key.
Watch out: ordered scans get slower or lose features; fixed bucket counts must be sized to the data or chains grow long.
Seen in RocksDB: 74781a0c49 2013-08-22 Added prefix-hash and unsorted memtable reps alongside the vector rep. be96f2498e used an array instead of `unordered_map`, removing locks. • 424a524ac9 2013-12-27 HashLinkList memtable for memory efficiency; 4e6463ea44 made skip-list height and branching configurable to cut per-bucket memory. • 9c332aa11a 2014-07-01 A bucket switches from a linked list to a skip list past a size threshold, to cut outliers. • 315281a167 2026-11-09 `sorted_hash` memtable: hash table for point reads and writes, sorted view built only when an ordered iterator is requested, cached until new inserts, with the sort moved to flush. Point reads 53-65% faster, MultiGet 46-51%, single-thread inserts 55-58%; a 64 MiB flush takes 470 ms instead of 210 ms, and tailing iterators are not supported.

### rocksdb-014 — Interpolation search, chosen per block from statistics gathered at write time
Lens: algorithms · Source: RocksDB
Principle: For roughly uniform keys, guess the target position from key values rather than always halving, falling back to binary search when the guess stops shrinking the range. Decide at write time, per block, whether the keys are uniform enough, and store a one-bit flag for readers.
Why it is faster: Interpolation search takes O(log log n) probes on uniform data versus O(log n) for binary search, but degrades badly on skewed data. Measuring the gap distribution while building (an online variance gives the coefficient of variation) costs almost nothing, and lets each block use the right algorithm, so skewed blocks don't pay.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: sorted timestamp or ID arrays; learned-index style position guesses. • Native / systems: per-page encoding or search strategy flags chosen at build time. • Web / backend: searching large sorted numeric arrays (time series in dashboards).
Watch out: forced interpolation on skewed keys can be much slower (-40% measured); always keep a fallback and a per-block or per-dataset choice.
Seen in RocksDB: 9f47518676 2026-02-13 Index-block seek interprets the first 8 bytes after the shared prefix as a number and interpolates, with binary-search fallback for small ranges, equal endpoints or a step that fails to halve the range: readrandom 347K to 383K ops/s. Bug fix in 98002215d0. • 3ad23b2d94 2026-03-06 `BlockSearchType::kAuto`: the builder runs Welford's online variance over key gaps and sets a footer bit. readrandom +9.2%; with heavy key shortening, kAuto gave +2.6% where forced interpolation gave -40.1%.

### rocksdb-015 — Cuckoo hashing with cache-local neighborhoods for fixed lookup tables
Lens: algorithms · Source: RocksDB
Principle: For read-mostly tables, cuckoo hashing gives worst-case O(1) lookups. Probe nearby slots first so most probes stay in one cache line, use power-of-two bucket counts, and store only what the iterator needs.
Why it is faster: Each key has a few fixed candidate locations, so a lookup checks at most a handful of slots, with no chains. Neighborhood probing keeps those checks within a cache line. Inlining the hash instead of calling through a function pointer and masking instead of dividing remove per-probe overhead. Inserts can be expensive (relocation paths), so this suits build-once tables.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: read-only feature-store lookup tables; hash-join build sides. • Native / systems: cuckoo filters and flow tables in network switches and packet processors. • Embedded: fixed-size lookup tables built offline and searched with bounded latency.
Watch out: insert cost and failure at high load factors; plan for rebuilds and keep the load factor moderate.
Seen in RocksDB: 9d9d2965cb 2014-04-29 `HashCuckooRep` memtable using BFS to find cuckoo paths. • 7f71448388 2014-08-28 Cache-friendly cuckoo SST format with neighborhood probing. • d20b8cfaa1 2014-08-29 Inlined hash instead of a function pointer and power-of-two buckets: about 50% faster, roughly 0.1 us/op batched. • c6275956e2 2014-09-25 Iterator stores only the bucket id: 20 to 4 bytes per entry. • fbd2dafc9f 2014-09-25 Read-only `CompactedDBImpl::MultiGet` over cuckoo tables with prefetching: 3M to 4.9M lookups/s.

### rocksdb-016 — Apply cancelling operations as they arrive when replaying a log
Lens: algorithms · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When replaying an add/delete log, apply deletes immediately instead of collecting every add and resolving at the end; most early adds are later cancelled.
Why it is faster: Accumulating everything makes the working set (and the final sort) proportional to the whole history. Cancelling eagerly keeps it proportional to the live state.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: event-sourced replay; Redis AOF rewrite; applying CRDT tombstones eagerly. • Web / backend: rebuilding client state from an offline mutation queue.
Watch out: only valid when a delete can be matched to its add without later context (no resurrection semantics).
Seen in RocksDB: 4d16a9a633 2015-01-07 `VersionBuilder::Apply` applies "delete file" edits as they arrive: reading a 50MB manifest with 9K live files went from 1.6 s to 0.7 s.

### rocksdb-017 — Let the merge policy match the workload: leveled, tiered or FIFO
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: No single merge strategy minimizes write, read and space cost at once. Offer leveled merging (low read and space amplification), size-tiered merging (low write amplification) and FIFO dropping (write amplification of 1 for expiring data), each with tunable triggers.
Why it is faster: Leveled compaction rewrites each byte roughly once per level times the fanout, which keeps reads and space tight but costs write bandwidth. Tiered compaction merges similar-sized runs, rewriting each byte far fewer times at the cost of more runs to search and temporary space. FIFO never rewrites: old files are simply deleted. Matching the policy to the workload (ingest-heavy, read-heavy, time-to-live data) can change throughput by large factors.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Cassandra STCS/LCS/TWCS; Lucene merge policies; Kafka retention versus compaction. • Web / backend: log or metrics retention that drops whole time buckets instead of deleting rows. • Embedded: flash logs that rotate whole segments (FIFO) rather than compacting.
Watch out: tiered merging needs headroom for temporary space; FIFO only works when data truly expires by age.
Seen in RocksDB: 469724be7f 2013-03-08 Bulk-load settings: two levels and large files, so data is written as few times as possible. • 47c4191fe8 2013-06-13 "Hybrid" L0-to-L0 compaction to reduce write amplification (554c06dd18), later renamed Universal compaction (116ec527f2). • 4012ca1c7b 2013-09-09 `max_size_amplification_percent` triggers a full compaction when space amplification gets too high; 9edda37027 added a size-ratio threshold and 5e3aeb5f8e a similar-size stop style. • 6de6a06631 2014-05-21 FIFO compaction for TTL-style data, "write amplification of 1".

### rocksdb-018 — Move or copy data by metadata instead of rewriting it
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: If an input segment does not overlap anything in the destination, relocate it by editing metadata, with no read or write of the data. When only placement changes, copy raw bytes rather than decoding and re-encoding.
Why it is faster: A rewrite costs a full read, decode, merge, encode and write, plus syncs. A metadata move costs one manifest record. Sequential or partitioned keys often produce non-overlapping files, so a large fraction of compaction work can disappear. The more files a single move can take (left and right, multiple files, at open time), the larger the saving.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: metadata-only partition moves in data lakes (Iceberg/Delta "add files"); Kafka segment promotion. • Native / systems: `rename`, `reflink` or `copy_file_range` instead of copy loops; server-side copy in object stores. • Web / backend: re-tiering media between storage classes without re-encoding.
Watch out: moved files keep their original layout (compression, size, tombstones); a moved file that is too big or full of garbage may need rewriting later anyway.
Seen in RocksDB: ccf0fcd5c2 2011-06-22 Trivial move of a non-overlapping file to the next level (with other changes) removed 2 of 5 syncs in fillseq. • 3ce3bb3da2 2015-06-04 L0-to-L1 trivial move for any number of non-overlapping files; c0b23dd5b0 for universal compaction. • 2f4fc539c6 2016-12-07 `IsTrivialMove` relaxed using grandparent overlap up to `max_compaction_bytes`. • 4428c76181 2022-06-30 Multi-file L0-to-L1 trivial move: fillseq throughput about +50%. a9565ccb26 extended it to up to 4 files at other levels, which removed stalls in the benchmark. • 601320164b 2023-03-27 Files are trivially moved down at DB open when dynamic level sizing is enabled. • ba16e8eee7 2023-04-14 `TryExtendNonL0TrivialMove` also extends to the left: fillrandom 61,159 to 63,298 ops/s. 43e9a60bb2 always allows L0-to-L1 trivial moves in manual compaction, avoiding large readahead memory with many L0 files. • 13d865f6f1 2025-05-08 FIFO temperature change copies the file bytes ("trivial copy") instead of rewriting keys.

### rocksdb-019 — Drop obsolete versions and deletion markers as early as it is safe
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Overwritten values and tombstones that no snapshot can see are garbage. Remove them in memory before flush, during every merge, and at intermediate levels whenever nothing below could be resurrected, instead of carrying them to the bottom.
Why it is faster: Every obsolete entry that survives a step is written again at every later step, and slows reads that must skip it. Dropping it at flush saves all downstream write amplification and space. Tombstones can only go when no older version of the key can exist below; checking overlap with lower levels lets them go before the bottom. Snapshots split history into stripes, and only the newest version per stripe must be kept.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: coalesce duplicate updates in a client outbox before upload; collapse redundant writes in a write-back cache. • Data / databases: Cassandra tombstone GC when no older SSTables overlap; MVCC vacuum below the oldest snapshot; Kafka compacted topics. • Embedded: write combining in flash drivers so overwritten sectors never reach NAND.
Watch out: dropping a tombstone too early resurrects old data; the overlap and snapshot checks must be exact.
Seen in RocksDB: 9a357847eb 2012-11-26 Drops versions that lie entirely between two snapshots. • 806e264350 2013-02-28 Deduplicates keys while flushing a memtable; 6acbe0fc45 merges several memtables in memory before flush (`min_write_buffer_number_to_merge`). • fe25070242 2013-08-19 In-place update when the new value for an equal key is no larger, avoiding a new entry. • a09ee1069d 2014-01-10 `max_successive_merges` folds merge operands in the memtable so reads don't re-apply long chains. • 4ab26c5ad1 2015-08-24 Snapshot-aware purging of overwritten and deleted keys during flush. • 6a9335dbbb 2017-10-27 Always drops tombstones in a bottommost L0-to-L0 compaction. • 4848bd0c4e 2018-08-24 Drops a deletion marker at the bottom level when all other occurrences of the key are in the same snapshot stripe. • 4aa52d89cf 2023-06-05 Drops range tombstones in non-bottommost compactions when the range doesn't exist in lower levels. • 9c94559de7 2024-10-25 Standalone range-deletion files from bulk loads are marked for compaction, and input files they fully cover are filtered out without being read; snapshot releases trigger scheduling.

### rocksdb-020 — Pick what to merge by cost-benefit: least overlap, most garbage
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When choosing the next segment to merge, prefer the one that rewrites the fewest bytes per byte it pushes down (smallest overlap with the next level relative to its size), and inflate the apparent size of segments full of deletions so space is reclaimed sooner.
Why it is faster: The cost of merging a file is proportional to the bytes it overlaps below; its benefit is proportional to its own size. Ranking by that ratio directly lowers write amplification. Tombstones look small but represent large amounts of reclaimable data below them, so a "compensated size" lets the picker see their real value.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: cost-benefit segment cleaning in log-structured filesystems (LFS, F2FS); SSD FTL victim selection. • Data / databases: Lucene/Elasticsearch merge selection; vacuum ordering by dead-tuple ratio. • Web / backend: GC region selection by garbage ratio (G1 "garbage first") in managed runtimes.
Watch out: pure greedy picking can starve some ranges; add randomization or age so every region is eventually compacted.
Seen in RocksDB: 5b0fe6c73b 2012-10-25 Greedy largest-file-first picking via a per-level files-by-size list. • e813f5b6d9 2014-06-24 Deletion entries count as twice the average value size, so files full of tombstones get compacted and space is reclaimed. • 7413306d94 2015-05-15 Random first file for `SuggestCompactRange`, avoiding left-skewed, conflicting picks. • d6e1035a1f 2015-12-04 Oldest-smallest-seqno-first for random-update workloads. • 92a9ccf1a6 2016-02-11 `kMinOverlappingRatio`: about 5% less compaction than oldest-smallest-seqno-first under a bounded pending-bytes limit. • d94aa2f7db 2019-01-23 `kMinOverlappingRatio` became the default ("much better write amplification"); 5bf941966b uses compensated file size in it. • cc6f323705 2022-12-29 Range tombstones add an estimate of the bytes they delete below (`compensated_range_deletion_size`, persisted in the manifest): total compaction writes 92.3 to 87.2 GB, and a smaller final DB.

### rocksdb-021 — Bound the size and fanout of each unit of background work
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Cap how much one merge may take in, and how much it may overlap with the level after next, so no single job becomes huge and no level becomes a write-amplification hotspot.
Why it is faster: An unbounded compaction holds resources for a long time, bursts I/O and makes the next compaction from its outputs expensive (large grandparent overlap). Bounded units keep latency predictable and keep each future step cheap. Limiting fanout from L0 into the base level keeps that step's write amplification in check under bursts.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Lucene's max merged segment size; incremental vacuum batches. • Web / backend: batch jobs processed in bounded chunks so one job never monopolizes workers. • Native / systems: F2FS segment selection limits; bounded GC slices.
Watch out: limits that are too small create many tiny jobs and files, which raises per-job overhead and metadata cost.
Seen in RocksDB: 239ac9d2de 2012-02-02 "Avoid very large compactions". • 85584d497e 2012-04-17 LevelDB foundation (`version_set`): `kMaxGrandParentOverlapBytes` = 10 × target file size, and a cap on expanded compaction size. • 3d9d77d900 2019-05-22 Intra-L0 compaction bounded by `max_compaction_bytes`. • 177f8bd063 2020-09-01 Bounded L0-to-Lbase fanout in dynamic leveling: burst write amplification 20.2 to 12.6, total 21.5 to 14.1, fillrandom 4.7 to 7.2 MB/s.

### rocksdb-022 — Size levels from the actual data so ratios stay regular
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Derive each level's target size from the size of the last level, so every level is about the fanout times the one above. Don't artificially boost one level's priority, since that distorts the shape and makes later merges expensive.
Why it is faster: Write amplification per level is proportional to the size ratio between adjacent levels. Fixed targets leave irregular ratios as data grows: some levels are nearly empty, others oversized. Dynamic targets keep ratios near the multiplier, which minimizes total write and space amplification. Inflating L0's score made L1 grow, which made every later L0-to-L1 merge more expensive.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: leveled compaction tuning in Cassandra/ScyllaDB; tier sizing in tiered storage. • Native / systems: sizing GC generations by measured survival rates. • Web / backend: multi-tier caches sized as ratios of the backing store.
Watch out: when the last level shrinks (mass deletes), dynamic targets shift and can trigger extra compactions while the shape settles.
Seen in RocksDB: db03739340 2015-02-05 `level_compaction_dynamic_level_bytes`. • 7a3577519f 2015-05-21 Stopped inflating the L0 score: a heavy-write benchmark went from 47 minutes to 2 minutes. • 235b162be1 2016-01-07 No L1-to-L2 compaction is scheduled while a higher-priority L0-to-L1 compaction is pending.

### rocksdb-023 — Apply smooth backpressure from background debt, and don't create thrash
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Slow writers gradually as background debt (unmerged files) approaches a limit instead of switching between full speed and a hard stall. Avoid control actions that create more debt, such as flushing buffers that are already waiting.
Why it is faster: On/off stalls produce latency cliffs and bursty throughput. A delay that grows with pressure keeps the system near its sustainable rate. Flushing a nearly empty buffer when others are already queued creates tiny files and hits the queue limit, causing the very stall it tried to avoid.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: token-bucket API limits with soft thresholds; adaptive concurrency limits. • Native / systems: TCP congestion control; debounced flushes in batching producers. • Data / databases: ingestion pipelines that throttle on consumer lag rather than rejecting at a hard cap.
Watch out: soft limits that kick in too early waste throughput; tune them against the background system's real drain rate.
Seen in RocksDB: 9700677a2b 2013-07-31 Writes slowed by a quadratic function as the L0 file count approaches the trigger. • 1036537c94 2013-08-05 Soft and hard rate limits. • 6115254416 2022-06-29 Concurrent compaction completions are no longer counted as reasons to slow down further. • 91166012c8 2022-08-17 The write buffer manager no longer flushes a column family that already has immutable memtables waiting: write stalls 175 to 0.

### rocksdb-024 — Make range deletions first-class: fragment once, cache, and skip covered keys with one seek
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Represent range deletes as non-overlapping fragments sorted by key, built once per immutable unit and cached. Readers carry only the strongest covering deletion from upper levels, and iterators seek past an entire covered range in one step.
Why it is faster: Overlapping intervals force a linear check per key; non-overlapping fragments make coverage a binary search. Rebuilding fragments per read is O(T log T) per Get; caching them makes it amortized zero. Carrying a single "max covering seqno" avoids accumulating every level's tombstones. A tombstone [start, end) in a newer level means every older level can seek straight to end, which skips a whole swath instead of testing each key. Long runs of point deletes can be converted into a range tombstone so later scans also skip.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: deleted-range skipping in MVCC scans (Pebble, CockroachDB); "gap" markers over soft-deleted rows. • Web / backend: IP allow/deny lists and calendar availability as normalized interval maps. • Native / systems: free-extent maps in allocators kept coalesced.
Watch out: the intermediate design (local computation without caching) was 2.5x slower; changes like this often need the whole sequence to pay off. Invalidate caches on every new range delete.
Seen in RocksDB: 50e305de98 2016-12-19 Collapsed mode: overlapping range tombstones normalized into non-overlapping segments so checks become `upper_bound` searches, used on scan paths where many checks hit the same tombstones. • 8c78348c77 2018-10-24 Get carries only the max covering seqno from upper levels and checks the current file's tombstones (initially 220,090 to 89,707 ops/s, enabling the next step). • 7528130e38 2018-10-25 The fragmented tombstone list is cached at table open: readrandom with range tombstones 89,707 to 1,017,076 ops/s. • 85394a96ca 2018-11-26 `ForwardRangeDelIterator` keeps heaps of active (by end key) and inactive (by start key) tombstone iterators, advancing only those passed, instead of seeking every iterator on every check. • 9d77bf8f7b 2022-08-05 Immutable memtables fragment their tombstones once, outside the mutex: readrandom with 1000 tombstones 320.33 to 5.21 us/op; 32 threads with 100 tombstones 2308 to 6.57 us/op. • 30bc495c03 2022-09-02 Merging iterator skips swaths covered by range tombstones from newer levels with one seek: full scan with 10k tombstones 22,384 to 227,919 ops/s (+918%), short scans +1997%, about -1.5% without tombstones. • f291eefb02 2022-09-13 Mutable memtables rebuild the fragment cache lazily on read (double-checked locking), invalidated by DeleteRange: readwhilewriting 48.28 to 1.52 us/op. • 3f51c0a185 2026-04-03 `min_tombstones_for_range_conversion`: an iterator that skips a long run of point deletes inserts a logically redundant range tombstone covering it, so later scans jump over it.

### rocksdb-025 — Cut output segments at boundaries that keep future merges cheap
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When writing merge output, end files at the boundaries of the next level's files (within a size tolerance), estimate the final file size including its index and filter, and check partition boundaries cheaply.
Why it is faster: An output file that straddles next-level files drags extra overlap into every future merge that includes it. Aligning the cut removes that wasted rewrite. Files that overshoot or undershoot their target (because the tail size was ignored or cut state was stale) increase file count or merge cost. Checking a cached boundary prefix inline is cheaper than a per-key copy and virtual call.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: align time-series chunks with rollup windows; shard split points aligned with secondary-index ranges. • Web / backend: tile boundaries aligned across zoom levels; chunked uploads that reserve space for the footer. • Native / systems: log rotation decided by comparing a cached date prefix.
Watch out: aligning cuts produces variable file sizes; keep a tolerance band so files don't become tiny.
Seen in RocksDB: f053851af6 2021-10-19 Looks past non-overlapping levels when choosing grandparents for cutting. • f3cc66632b 2022-09-29 `level_compaction_dynamic_file_size`, on by default: cut at grandparent boundaries within 0.5x to 2x of target. Cumulative compaction writes 285.9 to 250.0 GB (-12.6%); -14% in a simulator. 17a1d65e3a also cuts at round-robin compaction cursors. • 4d0f9a995c 2023-01-17 TTL cut state is updated even on early return, preventing unnecessarily small output files. • 7603712a88 2025-11-05 `EstimatedTailSize()` adds the index and filter that will be appended at finish to the cut decision, updated per data block rather than per key. • d3a82f28ce 2026-08-19 `SstPartitioner::ShouldPartitionByPrefix()` caches the next boundary prefix and compares it inline: instructions -2.6%, compaction wall time -2.4%.

### rocksdb-026 — Merge small fresh segments among themselves before merging into a big level
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When the newest level is small, or the next level is busy, merge new files with each other (intra-L0) instead of rewriting a large base level each time.
Why it is faster: An L0-to-base merge rewrites base-level data in proportion to its size, even if L0 holds little. Merging L0 files together costs only their own size and keeps the file count (read amplification) down until a base merge is worth it. Using compensated sizes keeps deletes moving down.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: merge small Parquet files before merging into large ones; coalesce small Kafka or Lucene segments first. • Web / backend: batch small uploads into medium objects before final consolidation.
Watch out: intra-level merges re-write the same data more than once if the level keeps growing; bound them by size.
Seen in RocksDB: d659faad54 2017-04-04 L0-to-L0 compaction when the base level is busy, reducing L0 file count. • ef3e289b2d 2023-09-12 Excludes some L0 files from size-amp compaction to avoid write stops. • 4b684e96b7 2024-01-22 `intra_l0_compaction_size`: fillrandom 4,264 to 7,782 ops/s, compaction read bytes 1.49 to 0.81 GB. 8be824e316 uses compensated file size for this decision.

### rocksdb-027 — Reclaim space incrementally instead of with occasional full rewrites
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When space amplification must be reduced, compact a bounded slice of files at a time rather than waiting for a full-database merge.
Why it is faster: Full compactions rewrite everything at once: huge I/O bursts, temporary double space and latency spikes. Bounded steps spread the same work out, and often avoid it, because only the most expensive part needs merging.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: incremental or region-based GC instead of stop-the-world. • Data / databases: rolling reindexing and re-sharding.
Watch out: incremental steps can lag behind heavy ingest; keep a full-compaction fallback for when debt keeps growing.
Seen in RocksDB: c66b4429ff 2021-10-20 Incremental space-amp compactions in universal style: full compactions mostly eliminated in rate-limited ingestion; cutting files at 1.5x target improved write amplification from 9.4 to 8.9.

### rocksdb-028 — Skip merges and searches whose result changes nothing
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Don't rewrite data that was just written, don't touch levels with nothing in range, don't run a search whose outcome is predetermined, and delay rewrites that are likely to be redone soon.
Why it is faster: Re-compacting bottom-level files without a compaction filter only rewrites identical data. Starting a manual compaction at L0 when the range lives deeper wastes passes. Searching the tree for an ingestion target level when overlap forces L0 anyway burns CPU. Each is pure overhead with no change in the result.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: incremental builds that skip unchanged targets; debounce re-indexing of frequently edited documents. • Data / databases: skip VACUUM FULL on tables without dead tuples; skip planning for fixed-path queries.
Watch out: users sometimes run a "redundant" compaction for a side effect (applying a new compression setting); provide an explicit force option.
Seen in RocksDB: 4eabbdb7ec 2015-06-18 Manual compaction skips the bottommost level unless a compaction filter exists or `force_bottommost_level_compaction` is set. • baa5302447 2019-04-16 Manual compaction skips the final Lmax-to-Lmax recompaction of files just produced by Lmax-1-to-Lmax. • afb97094ae 2020-03-04 `CompactRange` starts at the highest level that overlaps the range instead of L0. • 2021392e25 2020-12-15 A "busy" result from the concurrency limiter no longer triggers a full scan for obsolete files. • d1ff401472 2023-08-16 `bottommost_file_compaction_delay` defers snapshot-driven bottommost rewrites that are likely to be repeated. • 928aca835f 2024-01-24 Ingestion skips the LSM search for a target level when ingested files overlap each other; 2cb00c6921 splits overlapping ingests into batches so they can still land in lower levels.

### rocksdb-029 — Scale background effort to measured pressure, and de-synchronize periodic work
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Increase compaction parallelism only on real signals (pending bytes relative to data size, files marked for compaction, a minimum debt), auto-tune limits from database size, and spread periodic jobs across instances with stable randomized phases.
Why it is faster: Fixed parallelism either wastes resources when idle or falls behind under load. Signals relative to data size avoid over-reacting in tiny column families. Fleets of identically configured instances run periodic compactions in lockstep, producing synchronized I/O spikes; stable per-instance phases spread the load.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: autoscale workers on queue lag relative to throughput; jittered cron jobs and cache TTLs. • Native / systems: GC pacing on heap growth rate. • Data / databases: cost-based vacuum throttling; staggered certificate or index rebuilds across a fleet.
Watch out: multiple signals can interact and oscillate; add hysteresis and minimum thresholds.
Seen in RocksDB: d8e47620d7 2023-12-13 Compaction speedup triggered by pending compaction bytes relative to bottommost data size; aacf60dda2 by the number of files marked for compaction; bceb2dfe6a requires a minimum debt. • fecb10c2fa 2024-05-24 `CompactionOptionsUniversal::max_read_amp`, with 0 meaning auto-tune from DB size. • e4a7136d3e 2026-08-26 Preferred-phase periodic compaction; 7e0c4d8e11 computes how far to pull work early so catch-up is spread evenly.

### rocksdb-030 — Reorganize data based on observed read cost, and retire the heuristic when it stops paying
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Use read-side signals (wasted file probes, hidden entries skipped, read frequency) to trigger merges, not just write volume. Re-evaluate when other changes (such as filters) remove the cost the heuristic was fighting.
Why it is faster: Write-driven triggers ignore data that is rarely written but read often through many obsolete entries. Merging it removes repeated read amplification. But a trigger costs CPU and I/O itself; once Bloom filters removed most wasted probes, seek-triggered compaction cost more than it saved and was removed.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: auto-vacuum triggered by dead-tuple scans; adaptive indexing (database cracking). • Native / systems: JIT recompilation thresholds; promote-on-read in tiered storage. • Web / backend: rebuild a denormalized view when read latency, not write count, crosses a threshold.
Watch out: adaptive triggers become dead weight once another mechanism solves the problem; measure and remove them.
Seen in RocksDB: ccf0fcd5c2 2011-06-22 Compacts a file after enough overlapping seeks (foundation: allowed seeks = file size / 16384, minimum 100). • c1bb32e1ba 2012-09-27 Charges a seek only after a Bloom filter miss or a table open, when real I/O happened. • d4a8423334 2014-06-20 Removed seek compaction because it made code "slower in some cases". • 56359da691 2025-04-10 `memtable_op_scan_flush_trigger` flushes a memtable whose scans skip many hidden entries: seekrandomwhilewriting 18.5 to 4.9 us/op (54K to 203K ops/s). • da2c3c0ee6 2026-02-28 Quiet DBs react sooner to time-based triggers. • 5db0603613 2026-03-27 Read-triggered compaction (`read_triggered_compaction_threshold`).

### rocksdb-031 — Strip per-record metadata that no reader can distinguish any more
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Once data is older than every snapshot and sits where no older version can exist, its version numbers carry no information. Zero them so the data compresses better, special compact encodings apply, and readers can take shortcuts.
Why it is faster: Unique 7-byte sequence numbers per key are incompressible noise. Replacing them with 0 makes blocks smaller after compression, enables one-byte encodings, and gives readers a cheap invariant ("seqno 0 means no older duplicate") that skips comparisons.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Postgres-style tuple freezing; truncating event timestamps after compaction in event stores. • Web / backend: stripping version vectors from converged CRDT data before archiving.
Watch out: features that later need history (time-travel reads, user timestamps) must opt out.
Seen in RocksDB: 4564915446 2013-02-15 Zeroes sequence numbers in the last level that are older than the earliest snapshot. • 56bea9f80d 2014-01-27 The same for universal compaction; d169b67680 lets PlainTable encode seq=0 plus value type in 1 byte instead of 8. • 59244447e3 2019-02-01 Removed the exception for the last key, so even it gets seqno 0 and its tombstone dropped at the bottom; 25d81e4577 uses seqno 0 to skip comparisons during iteration.

### rocksdb-032 — Split a large merge into balanced parallel ranges using sampled key anchors
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Parallelize one big merge by key range, choosing split points from sampled index keys so each range holds about the same data, and let each worker open only the input files that overlap its range.
Why it is faster: Naive splits at file boundaries give uneven ranges, so the slowest worker sets the job time. Sampling anchors from indexes approximates equal data per range at almost no cost. Trimming non-overlapping inputs per worker avoids opening files and wasting readahead.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: range partitioning for parallel sort (TeraSort sampling); parallel table scans split by histogram. • Web / backend: load-balanced batch jobs split by sampled key ranges.
Watch out: more parallel ranges produce more, smaller output files; balance parallelism against file count.
Seen in RocksDB: e03f8a0c12 2022-04-06 Each L0 subcompaction trims input files that don't overlap its range. • 252bea405e 2022-07-23 Subcompaction partitioning via `ApproximateKeyAnchors()`. c7ccbb33a6 runs manual compactions in parallel by default.

### rocksdb-033 — Link a large pre-indexed batch in as its own immutable segment instead of re-inserting it
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: A large transaction already holds its writes in a sorted, indexed buffer. At commit, wrap that buffer as a read-only memtable and link it into the read path in O(1), rather than inserting every key into the shared write buffer.
Why it is faster: Key-by-key insertion of a large batch costs O(n log n) in the shared structure and blocks other writers for the duration. Publishing the existing index is constant time; the merge happens later in the background flush.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: Lucene `addIndexes` segment swaps; atomic table swap after bulk load; COPY versus row INSERTs. • Web / backend: publishing a prebuilt immutable config map with a pointer swap.
Watch out: each linked segment adds a source to every read until it is flushed; only use it above a size threshold.
Seen in RocksDB: 1c7652fcef 2024-11-12 `WBWIMemTable`, a read-only memtable (2ce6902cf5) backed by the transaction's indexed batch. • d5345a8ff7 2024-12-05 `commit_bypass_memtable`: for a 10k-key transaction write_memtable_time fell more than 100x (116,950,422 to 740,784), and fillrandom rose from 251,119 to 380,559 ops/s. • 925c63a96b 2025-04-17 `IngestWriteBatchWithIndex()`; e3b7dd7b56 per-transaction option; a00391c729 trigger by write-batch byte size.

### rocksdb-034 — Separate large values from the sorted structure at write time
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Write large values to separate blob files as soon as they arrive and keep only small references in the sorted tree, so merges move references instead of payloads.
Why it is faster: Compaction cost scales with bytes rewritten; keeping payloads out of the tree makes every merge cheaper. Separating at Put time (not at flush) also avoids holding values in memory twice and lets blob writes run in parallel across partitions.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: store uploads in object storage and keep pointers in the database. • Data / databases: WiscKey-style key-value separation; TOAST-like out-of-line columns.
Watch out: range scans now need extra reads per value, and blob files need their own garbage collection.
Seen in RocksDB: af4e32945b 2026-04-02 Blob direct write with partitioned blob files. • 3070f73e97 2026-04-08 Extended to wide columns with lazy resolution of values.

### rocksdb-035 — Reserve the minimum while a background job waits, and re-pick when it runs
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: A queued or long-running job should not lock all of its inputs, or hold back unrelated cleanup, for its whole lifetime. Reserve only what is essential, choose the rest when it actually starts, and track exact protection needs.
Why it is faster: Over-reservation blocks other jobs from those files and can let garbage pile up: a long remote compaction pinned deletion of every newer obsolete file, letting obsolete data grow to many times the live data. Late binding keeps choices fresh and resources free.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: late binding of work items in job queues; lease-based reservations. • Data / databases: MVCC GC horizons that are not pinned by idle transactions.
Watch out: re-picking at run time can find the inputs already taken; handle "nothing to do" gracefully.
Seen in RocksDB: 02bce9b1af 2025-06-12 Universal compaction forwards only the last input and re-picks on the bottom-priority thread; c33a4989ad made `reduce_file_locking` the default. • a813464e4b 2026-11-17 Long-running (remote) compactions no longer hold back deletion of all newer obsolete SSTs.

### rocksdb-036 — Put a membership filter in front of every expensive lookup that usually misses
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Before probing a costly structure (a file on disk, an in-memory index, a remote service), check a compact filter with no false negatives. A "no" skips the probe entirely; a "maybe" falls through to the real lookup.
Why it is faster: A Bloom filter at about 10 bits per key gives roughly 1% false positives, so 99% of absent-key probes avoid the I/O or search. In an LSM tree a point read may otherwise check every level; with filters it typically touches one. The filter also lets writes skip work: a delete of a key that cannot exist need not be written. The payoff grows with the miss rate and the cost of a miss.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: a Bloom filter before the username-availability query or a cache lookup for known-missing keys. • Data / databases: per-SST or per-partition filters (Cassandra, HBase, Parquet); hash-join build-side filters. • Embedded: blocklists on devices that would otherwise query a server.
Watch out: filters cost memory and build CPU, and help only on misses; for hit-heavy lookups they are pure overhead.
Seen in RocksDB: 85584d497e 2012-04-17 LevelDB `FilterPolicy` and Bloom filters per data block: k probes from one hash, k rounded down "to reduce probing cost", at least 64 bits for small n. • c1bb32e1ba 2012-09-27 Seek-compaction accounting counts a probe only when the Bloom check fails, i.e. when real I/O happened. • 2a986919d6 2013-07-05 Uses the filter to drop deletes of keys that cannot exist: no write, no space, no compaction work. • 3c02c363b3 2013-11-27 `DynamicBloom` filters the memtable for non-existent keys. • ed995c6a69 2019-02-19 Whole-key Bloom filter for memtables, motivated by MyRocks `GetForUpdate` during INSERT, which almost always finds nothing. 58c4aee42e also skips older memtables once the current one's earliest seqno covers the key's history. • ee79a28963 2021-02-05 Memtable Bloom filter (about 1.5% of memtable memory) on by default.

### rocksdb-037 — Skip filters, and filter checks, where they cannot pay off
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Don't build, cache or probe a filter where the answer is almost always "present" (the deepest level of a hit-heavy store), and don't consult a second filter or index when the first already decided.
Why it is faster: The last level holds most of the data, so its filters are the largest, and for workloads that look up existing keys they almost never say "no". Dropping them saves the bulk of filter memory and disk with little read cost. A prefix filter adds nothing to a Get when a whole-key filter exists. When a filter says "maybe", an extra index check before the data read is redundant work.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: skip a negative cache when the miss rate is low. • Data / databases: build partition-level filters only for selective columns (Parquet/Iceberg). • Native / systems: CDN "probably exists" checks only on tiers where misses are common.
Watch out: if the workload shifts toward misses, the removed filters are sorely missed; make it a per-workload option.
Seen in RocksDB: e63140d52b 2015-02-02 Get consults the prefix Bloom filter for full filters. • e7c434c364 2015-02-17 `optimize_filters_for_hits`: no filters in the last level, saving memory and disk. • e089db40f9 2015-12-23 Skips loading and caching bottom-level filter blocks. • fdd70d1495 2016-02-01 Skips filters for the last L0 file when L0 is the deepest level. • dff4c48ede 2016-04-12 Simplified `PrefixMayMatch`: no data-block lookup after a full-filter "maybe"; 535af525d6 no index search when no filter exists. • 5009b5326b 2016-06-09 Skips the prefix Bloom check when a whole-key Bloom filter exists.

### rocksdb-038 — Confine all probes for a key to one cache line
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Choose one 64-byte block per key from the hash, then set or test all k bits inside that block. Use sizes that make the block choice cheap, and keep all probe arithmetic inlined.
Why it is faster: A standard Bloom filter's k probes land on k random cache lines: k cache misses per query. A blocked filter costs one miss. The price is a slightly higher false-positive rate for the same bits (because bits within a block are less independent), which a few extra bits per key recover. With all bits in one line, the probe test can also become branch-free.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: split-block Bloom filters in Parquet and Impala; dedup filters in stream processors. • Native / systems: packet classifiers and CDN admission filters. • Web / backend: client-side URL blocklists (WASM) where every probe counts.
Watch out: blocked filters need slightly more bits per key for the same false-positive rate; the data must be cache-line aligned or a probe can straddle two lines.
Seen in RocksDB: cd535c2280 2014-01-14 Inlined `MayContainHash` and made constants `const`: 9.127 s to 7.891 s for 2^30 probes, where it had been 5-7% of production CPU. • 0d755fff14 2014-03-28 "Cache friendly blocked bloomfilter" (`bloom_locality`). • 462796697c 2014-06-02 Block size equal to the cache line so divides become shifts, an odd block count, and a byte rotation after each probe to use all bits (better false-positive rate). • 0af157f9bf 2014-09-08 Full (whole-file) filter format built on the cache-local layout: read QPS 2,230,002 to 2,991,411 (about +30%). • f059c7d9b9 2019-11-13 `FastLocalBloomImpl`: 8 probes inside one cache line tested together with AVX2, removing the "nastily unpredictable branch". Hot query 63 to 26 ns, cold 188 to 150 ns.

### rocksdb-039 — Tune the space / false-positive / probe-cost trade-off precisely
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Configure filters by target false-positive rate or fractional bits per key, and pick the probe count from measurements of the actual filter design rather than the textbook formula. Accept a slightly worse false-positive rate when a miss is cheap.
Why it is faster: Integer bits per key wastes up to a bit per key. The optimal number of probes for a cache-local Bloom filter is closer to bits/2 than to the textbook bits × ln 2, and every extra probe costs CPU. Where a false positive costs little (an in-memory skip list search), fewer memory operations per add or query win even at a higher false-positive rate.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: size dedup or join filters by target FP rate and expected miss cost. • Web / backend: expose "bytes vs accuracy" as a continuous knob in a sketch or filter library. • Embedded: fit a filter to an exact memory budget instead of rounding to whole bits per key.
Watch out: optimal parameters depend on the filter layout and hash quality; re-measure when either changes.
Seen in RocksDB: b55b2f45d0 2019-09-05 Memtable filter sets two bits per 64-bit word: false-positive rate about 1.12x higher, judged fine because a memtable filter miss costs only about 50x a filter query; 3% more bits recovers the accuracy. Average add latency 65.6 to 44.4. • 57f3032285 2019-11-26 Bits per key became a double, rounded to "millibits per key"; at 16 bits per key it picks 9 probes (FP 0.0843%) rather than 11 (0.0884%), and 9.55 new-format bits per key equal 10 old ones. • 60af964372 2020-11-12 Ribbon filters configured by `one_in_fp_rate`.

### rocksdb-040 — Derive all probes from one good hash, and keep persisted hashes separate from in-memory ones
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Compute one high-quality hash per key and generate the k probe positions from it with cheap arithmetic (double hashing, multiply and rotate, multiply-shift range reduction). Use a stable, versioned hash for anything stored on disk and a fast replaceable one for in-memory structures.
Why it is faster: Hashing the key k times dominates filter cost for long keys; one hash plus a few multiplies is nearly free. Multiply-shift (fastrange) maps a hash into [0, n) without integer division. Poor hashes collide on similar inputs, which raises false positives and, for sharded structures, lock contention. Decoupling persisted hashes lets in-memory code adopt faster functions without breaking file compatibility.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: xxHash/wyhash for in-process maps, a stable versioned hash for persisted sharding keys. • Data / databases: Count-Min and HyperLogLog sketches using one hash with derived indices. • Embedded: bitmap indexes where one hash computation must serve several lookups.
Watch out: derived probes are only as good as the base hash; never change a persisted hash without a format version.
Seen in RocksDB: 85584d497e 2012-04-17 LevelDB Bloom filter generates k probes from one hash by double hashing (`h += delta` with `delta` a rotation of `h`). • 0bb555630f 2019-04-08 Added a non-persistent 64-bit hash (`NPHash64`); the old hash produced many collisions on similar inputs, causing extra lock contention for reads of the same file. • b55b2f45d0 2019-09-05 Memtable filter expands one 32-bit hash with a golden-ratio multiply, derives bit positions from slices of it, rotates for the next word, and replaces modulo with fastrange. • ca7ccbe2ea 2019-10-24 `NPHash64` switched to XXH3, with `fastrange64`; 18f57f5ef8 added a persistent 64-bit hash (XXH3p) for new filters and made the empty string not hash to 0. • 8b8a2e9f05 2020-11-07 Ribbon derives its start position, coefficient row and result row cheaply from one stock hash, and pre-mixes ordinal seeds once at load instead of per query.

### rocksdb-041 — Choose filter granularity: one filter per file, partitioned with a pinned top level when large
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Check one whole-file filter before consulting the index, instead of per-block filters after it. When per-file filters get too big to cache whole, partition them and keep only a small, pinned top-level index of partitions in memory.
Why it is faster: A per-block filter requires an index lookup to find the block before the filter can say "no"; a whole-file filter rejects first and saves the index search and the filter-block fetch. Monolithic filters for big files must load entirely to answer anything and thrash the cache; partitions load only what is needed, and pinning the tiny top level removes cache lookups and misses on it. Feeding each key only once avoids redundant hashing.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: per-segment filters in search indexes; sharded Bloom filters for very large datasets. • Web / backend: per-shard membership filters at a router, checked before any shard lookup. • Native / systems: multi-level page-table style metadata with a pinned root.
Watch out: partitioning adds an indirection per lookup; for small files a single filter is simpler and faster.
Seen in RocksDB: 0af157f9bf 2014-09-08 Full (whole-file) filter blocks: read QPS about +30%. • 54b434110e 2017-03-07 Partitioned filter builder and reader (8b0097b49b), alongside the two-level index (69d5262c81). • 1dfcdb15f9 2017-08-23 Preload and pin L0 filter partitions. • bc0da4b512 2018-04-24 Skips duplicate whole-key and prefix additions when both are interleaved. • 80ade9ad83 2018-06-22 `pin_top_level_index_and_filter` keeps the top level in cache yet pinned, avoiding misses and lookup overhead. • 6e938c904f 2018-11-30 `NewBloomFilterPolicy()` defaults to full filters ("clear CPU saving benefits").

### rocksdb-042 — Filter on key prefixes so range scans can skip too
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: If queries stay within a key prefix (tenant, user, time bucket), build filters over the prefix. Then whole files or blocks lacking the prefix can be skipped even for scans, not only for exact-key lookups.
Why it is faster: Whole-key filters only help point lookups. A prefix filter answers "does any key with this prefix exist here?", which prunes seeks and short scans. Copying the filter and extractor pointers into the iterator removes pointer chasing on every seek. A scan bounded within one prefix can use the filter automatically.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: tenant-prefixed keys in multi-tenant KV stores; per-user activity feeds. • Data / databases: time-bucket prefixes in metrics stores; prefix-bucketed autocomplete.
Watch out: a prefix extractor that doesn't match the real query shape gives no pruning, and changing it invalidates existing filters.
Seen in RocksDB: f5f1842282 2013-08-13 Prefix filters for scans, with faster filter creation. • fd2044883a 2013-11-03 Prefix-aware iterator for multiple seeks. • 2d3468c293 2014-03-28 Copied the Bloom filter and extractor pointers into the iterator; 10-25% of Seek CPU had gone to pointer chasing. • d642c60bdc 2014-04-25 Checks `PrefixMayMatch` on Seek. • 408205a36b 2018-06-26 Uses the Bloom filter when `[user_key, iterate_upper_bound)` shares a prefix under the file's extractor. • 280b9f371a 2022-05-19 Applied the `auto_prefix_mode` range check to partitioned filters too, with a large seekrandom gain there.

### rocksdb-043 — Use near-optimal static filters (Ribbon) where filters live long, and Bloom where they are short-lived
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: For immutable key sets, encode membership as the solution of a banded linear system over GF(2) (Ribbon), which comes within a few percent of the information-theoretic space bound. Lay out the solution so a query touches a few contiguous words. Pay its higher build cost only for filters that stay in memory long.
Why it is faster: Cache-local Bloom filters use about 50% more space than optimal; Ribbon reaches the same false-positive rate with about 30% less memory, so more of the filter set stays cached. Building costs more CPU, which is wasted on short-lived filters (flush output, upper levels) that are soon replaced. An interleaved (column-blocked) layout makes query cost scale with cache lines touched, not result width.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: static sets shipped to clients (revoked certificates, ad-block lists) as xor/Ribbon filters. • Embedded: read-only membership tables in firmware where every byte counts. • Data / databases: choose filter type by object lifetime in a cache or LSM; static retrieval maps for feature hashing.
Watch out: Ribbon cannot be updated incrementally and is slower to build and somewhat slower to query than Bloom; use it only for immutable, long-lived sets.
Seen in RocksDB: 25d54c799c 2020-10-25 Generic Ribbon algorithms: construction "within 2.5% space overhead vs. information theoretic lower bound". • 746909ceda 2020-11-03 Interleaved solution storage: outside-query time 117.8 ns (simple layout) to 42.3 ns (Bloom: 24.0 ns), with prefetching of rows during construction. • 60af964372 2020-11-12 SST Ribbon filter policy: same FP as Bloom at about 30% less space; single-filter query 18.75 ns net. • 10196d7edc 2021-04-16 Long-term SST support and a Bloom/Ribbon hybrid by starting level; 865a25101d declared Ribbon production-ready. • 2a383f21f4 2021-08-20 Hybrid API in which flush counts as level -1; the author estimates Ribbon "only pays off if the structure lives in memory for more than an hour".

### rocksdb-044 — Treat filter readability across versions as a performance requirement
Lens: storage-engine · Source: RocksDB
Principle: If a format or name change makes existing filters unreadable, every lookup silently falls back to reading data. Test cross-version filter use as you would test correctness.
Why it is faster: Filters fail open: a reader that cannot use one still returns correct results, only many times slower. Nothing breaks except throughput, so the regression is easy to ship.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: index formats kept readable across rolling upgrades. • Web / backend: cache-key schema and compiled-asset hash stability across deploys, where a mismatch shows up only as misses.
Watch out: fail-open accelerators need explicit metrics (filter usage rate) so a silent fallback is noticed.
Seen in RocksDB: 91687d70ea 2022-03-23 Fixed a filter compatibility bug: 6.29 reading a 7.0 DB ran at 5,249 ops/s versus 29,085; about 24,907 with the fix.

### rocksdb-045 — Construct rarely used state on first use, not up front
Lens: laziness · Source: RocksDB
