Principle: An object created on every request should not build maps, heaps, arenas, scratch buffers or helper objects that only rare paths use. Create them on first use, and return "nothing" instead of allocating empty placeholders.
Why it is faster: Per-request objects multiply any fixed construction cost by the request rate. Even an empty container can allocate (MSVC's `std::map` allocates a sentinel node). Lazy construction moves that cost onto only the requests that need it and shrinks objects and frames, which helps caches. The cost is a branch on each access and care with thread safety if the lazy member can be reached concurrently.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: request contexts with nullable optional sub-objects; lazy DI singletons; lazy imports in Python and JS bundles. • Native / systems: `std::optional` / `Option` members; keep large buffers out of async state machines (Rust futures, C# async). • Data / databases: lazily parsed headers and deferred index construction in dataframes.
Watch out: lazy members shared across threads need safe publication; a lazy path hit by most requests only adds a branch.
Seen in RocksDB: 58e1956d50 2013-11-20 Memtable keys for prefix-hashed memtables are encoded lazily; d439451fab delays cuckoo iterator initialization. • a8029fdc75 2013-12-02 `MergeContext` allocates its operand list in Get only when a merge operand appears (with ef2211a9ca). • 6c66918645 2014-10-17 DB open loads only the first 20 uninitialized file metadata entries instead of reading every file. • 1369f015ee 2015-06-17 Thread status data is initialized only for RocksDB-managed threads. • 3f62215210 2016-11-18 `RangeDelAggregator` builds its map and pinning manager lazily; it had cost about 3% of total CPU. fe349db57b removed its arena, and fd43ee09da returns `nullptr` instead of an empty tombstone iterator. • be723a8d8c 2017-03-22 `MemTableInserter`'s post-processing map lives in `std::aligned_storage` and is placement-new'd on first use; on Windows its construction had cost about 40% of fillrandom throughput. • 6f7b7f91b5 2018-03-14 `DuplicateDetector` is created only when needed. • 45f213b558 2018-10-17 Range-tombstone stripe map entries are created lazily, so flush and compaction skip a binary search per key when no range deletions exist. • 7a95938899 2022-10-25 `seq_set_` in tombstone fragmenting is built only when `ContainsRange()` (used by compaction) asks: fragmenting 270 to 150 us, readwhilewriting 1.31M to 1.44M ops/s. • 7bf6d4c9d5 2024-05-03 The block-based table iterator's `block_handles_` deque is constructed lazily: seekrandom 47,811 to 51,013 ops/s (+6.7%), fixing a regression. • c2b86f3ec0 2026-10-02 The 8 KiB scratch buffer is no longer embedded in the MultiGet coroutine frame and is heap-allocated only when I/O happens: frame size 21,048 to 12,864 bytes (-38.9%).

### rocksdb-046 — Defer loading data until it is used, and fetch only the part asked for
Lens: laziness · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Keep enough metadata (first key, handles, sizes) to order or filter items without loading their payload. Load a payload only when the caller actually reads it, read only the requested byte range, and skip it entirely when only metadata is needed.
Why it is faster: Many reads touch an item without needing its value: merging sources by key, skipping tombstones, key-only scans, conflict checks that need only a version. Eager loading pays I/O, decompression and copying for nothing. With the first key of each block in the index, a short range scan can merge files by their index entries and never read most data blocks.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `HEAD` before `GET`; GraphQL resolvers that fetch only selected fields; HTTP Range requests for media. • Data / databases: covering indexes and key-only scans; lazy-loaded ORM columns and BLOBs. • Native / systems: virtualized lists rendering from headers; merging remote sorted streams by their heads only.
Watch out: a lazy handle must keep its source alive (snapshots, files); if almost every item is eventually read, laziness adds a second round trip.
Seen in RocksDB: da032495d3 2015-12-21 `GetLatestSequenceForKey` skips loading the value, avoiding memcpys in transaction conflict checks. • b4d7209428 2019-06-24 `kBinarySearchWithFirstKey`: the index stores each block's first key, so reading a data block is deferred until its file reaches the top of the merging heap; for short scans most files never do. • ea85148b78 2023-02-21 `FindNextUserEntryInternal` stops calling `PrepareValue` for Delete and SingleDelete entries. • ecc084d301 2024-10-16 `ReadOptions::allow_unprepared_value` lets iterators skip eager blob loads until the application calls `PrepareValue()`; extended to multi-CF iterators (c0be6a4b90) and indexed write batches (1006eddd63). • 8e1569b351 2026-08-07 Byte-range blob reads: a sub-range no longer reads the whole record. • 34d5074bfa 2026-09-16 `MultiGetEntityLazy` resolves large values only when accessed and coalesces them across keys into one read per file.

### rocksdb-047 — Skip work whose result has no consumer
Lens: laziness · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Some steps exist only to feed one consumer: a log, a compressor, a verifier, a queued job. When that consumer is disabled, opted out or gone, skip the step entirely.
Why it is faster: Work done "just in case" still costs full price. Flattening write groups only matters if a WAL is written. Training a compression dictionary only matters if output is compressed. Verifying checksums only matters if the caller asked. Queued background jobs need not run when the database is closing.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: skip serializing a request body when no logger or audit sink is attached; don't compute ETags when caching is off; cancel pending timers on unmount. • Data / databases: skip index builds on temporary tables; propagate "skip validation" flags through every layer. • Native / systems: skip checksum generation when replication or verification is off.
Watch out: make the consumer check explicit and cheap; options that silently stop propagating (as RocksDB found with verify flags) bring the cost back.
Seen in RocksDB: b2b3086524 2015-03-16 On close, the thread pool unschedules this DB's pending flush and compaction tasks instead of waiting for them. • 6ce42dd075 2015-11-06 Merging a write group into one batch moved into the WAL step and is skipped without a WAL: about 5% on multi-threaded no-WAL writes. • f6d7ec1d02 2022-01-31 `total_order_seek` is ignored in `DB::Get`, where it no longer mattered: Get with it set went from 1,222 to 25,012 ops/s (about 20x). • f9cfc6a808 2022-07-06 Index blocks no longer fetch the uncompression dictionary, which only data blocks use; it had been 1-2% CPU with partitioned indexes. readrandom 143,950 to 146,176 ops/s. • b45738622a 2023-04-04 Callers' `verify_checksums=false` now reaches index and filter reads: checksum computations 384,353 to 22, fully cached readrandom 274,848 to 277,904 ops/s. • 4ce1dc930c 2024-03-20 Dictionary buffering and training happen only if `max_dict_bytes > 0` and output is compressed, fixing `SstFileWriter::Finish` throughput with bottommost compression off.

### rocksdb-048 — Run the cheap or selective check first, and do expensive preparation only after it passes
Lens: laziness · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Order conditions so the cheapest or most selective one runs first, and move setup work (copies, string building, value collection) to after the filter that usually rejects the operation. Once an index has located the answer, don't redo the general search.
Why it is faster: Short-circuit evaluation only helps if the decisive check is first. A copy made before a filter that rejects 94% of operations is wasted 94% of the time. A merge operator that can say "enough operands" lets a lookup stop collecting early.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: parse request bodies only after auth and rate limiting pass; order guard clauses by cost. • Data / databases: SQL predicate ordering by cost and selectivity; a covering index that skips the table lookup. • Native / systems: check a Bloom filter before building a query; lazy log-message formatting.
Watch out: reordering is only safe for side-effect-free checks; selectivity can shift with the workload.

### rocksdb-049 — Stop at the first hit, or once a threshold is reached
Lens: laziness · Source: RocksDB
Principle: If the question is "is there any overlap?" or "are there at least N?", test candidates one at a time and stop as soon as the answer is known, instead of building a full combined view or counting everything.
Why it is faster: Building a merged iterator over all candidates costs setup for every one before the first answer. Counting a list to compare against a limit is O(length) when O(limit) suffices. When a check against one structure opens files, reuse them for the related check.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `Array.some` instead of `filter().length > 0`; calendar conflict checks with early exit. • Data / databases: `EXISTS` / `LIMIT 1` instead of `COUNT(*) > 0`. • Native / systems: collision detection that stops at the first contact.
Watch out: order candidates so likely hits come first; otherwise the early exit rarely triggers.
Seen in RocksDB: 1bdb44de95 2017-11-28 The range-deletion overlap check reuses files already opened by the point-key overlap check. • ecfca1ff59 2018-03-16 External file ingestion checks L0 files individually and stops at the first overlap. • 7027265417 2024-04-17 `CountSuccessiveMergeEntries(key, limit)` stops at `max_successive_merges`; after merge chains became unbounded, counting had scanned the whole chain on every write.

### rocksdb-050 — Don't create empty or no-op artifacts
Lens: laziness · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Before creating a file, switching a buffer or flushing, check that there is something to put in it. Empty artifacts cost I/O and metadata operations and can trigger stalls further down.
Why it is faster: An empty output file still costs create, sync and manifest updates. Switching an empty memtable creates an immutable memtable that counts toward stall limits. Flushing a memtable that doesn't overlap a manual compaction's range adds L0 files for nothing.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: skip uploading empty telemetry batches; skip re-render when a diff is empty. • Data / databases: don't create zero-row partitions or empty segment files.
Watch out: some consumers expect a marker even when empty (a checkpoint, an "end of batch" record); keep those semantics explicit.
Seen in RocksDB: c1b375e96a 2017-06-29 Compaction skips generating empty SST files. • 3ae0047278 2018-02-27 `CompactRange` flushes only if the memtable overlaps the range: fewer L0 files, fewer stalls. • 17f9a181d5 2018-08-24 Checks that range deletions exist before opening a dedicated tombstone SST (1a88c43751 also fixed a size underflow). • 92b4401566 2018-10-29 Doesn't switch an empty active memtable; 671a7eb36f extended this, avoiding extra immutable memtables and write stalls.

### rocksdb-051 — Update long-lived derived state by diff, not by full rebuild
Lens: amortization · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When the inputs to a long-lived structure change slightly (a few files added or removed), add and remove only the affected parts instead of rebuilding the whole structure.
Why it is faster: A full rebuild costs proportional to the whole input set and throws away warm state (cached blocks, positioned iterators). A diff costs proportional to the change and keeps the rest warm.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: keyed virtual-DOM diffing; change-feed consumers that subscribe only to new partitions. • Data / databases: incremental view maintenance instead of full refresh.
Watch out: diff logic is easy to get subtly wrong; a missed removal leaks resources, a missed addition returns stale data.

### rocksdb-052 — Keep running aggregates instead of recomputing per query
Lens: amortization · Source: RocksDB
Principle: If a value is queried on every insert (an estimated size, a bucket count), maintain it incrementally as items are added, with one cheap update per step, rather than recomputing or dividing on each query.
Why it is faster: Recomputing an estimate two or three times per key multiplies its cost by the key count. An incremental add is O(1) and can replace a division with an addition (accumulate 1/ratio rather than divide by ratio). Values constant per configuration should be computed once.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: running averages in telemetry and progress estimators; maintain `size` in custom collections. • Embedded: per-packet rate computations as running sums. • Data / databases: incrementally maintained statistics for query planning.
Watch out: floating-point running sums drift; re-anchor periodically if exactness matters.
Seen in RocksDB: efd013d6d8 2016-06-13 `BlockBuilder` keeps a running `CurrentSizeEstimate` (called 2-3 times per key), `FlushBlockBySizePolicy` caches its deviation threshold, and consecutive varint appends share one buffer append. • 6d37fdb365 2018-08-20 The data-block hash index estimate stores `1/util_ratio` and adds it per key instead of dividing `num_keys / util_ratio` on every `Add`.

### rocksdb-053 — Recompute derived state once per batch of mutations
Lens: amortization · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When applying many mutations, recompute expensive derived state (scores, layouts, indexes) once at the end rather than after each one.
Why it is faster: Per-mutation recomputation turns O(N + cost) into O(N × cost). Readers rarely need intermediate states, so recomputing once at the end gives the same result.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: batch DOM writes before layout; rebuild a search index after a bulk import, not per document. • Data / databases: recompute aggregates and statistics once after a bulk insert.
Watch out: readers that observe state mid-batch see stale derived values; hold the batch under one critical section or publish atomically.
Seen in RocksDB: d2b0652b32 2023-08-07 `DeleteFilesInRanges` computes the compaction score once per batch: in production (TiKV, 30,000 ranges, about 80,000 files) the call went from more than 10 minutes to over 100 ms.

### rocksdb-054 — Pack the fields the hot path reads together, and move the rest away
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Group the data a hot loop actually touches into as few cache lines as possible: hot fields first, statistics and init-only fields last. Where a lookup only needs a small subset of a big record, copy that subset into its own dense array.
Why it is faster: A cache miss costs about 100 ns, and a miss loads a whole 64-byte line. If the three fields a search needs sit in three different lines (or three different heap objects), every probe pays three misses. A dense side array of just the search keys turns a pointer-chasing walk into a sequential scan that the hardware prefetcher handles well. The cost is a second copy to keep in sync, so this suits data that is built once and read many times. Measure first: RocksDB used a field-access profiler to find which members mattered.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: keep a compact array of IDs and sort keys for an in-memory index, separate from the full objects; in JS, initialize hot properties first and consistently so the hidden class stays small. • Native / systems: split hot and cold members (`struct Hot { ... }; Cold* cold;`); keep a contiguous key array next to a payload array for binary search. • Embedded: place the fields an interrupt handler reads in the first line of a control block. • Data / databases: keep hot columns of a row together; keep a min/max summary array per segment apart from the segment data.
Watch out: reordering by guesswork can make things worse. Profile field access, and re-check after other changes grow the struct. A duplicated dense array must be rebuilt whenever the source changes.
Seen in RocksDB: 8898a0a0d1 2014-06-24 Reordered `FileMetaData` so the fields used on lookup come first and stats sit at the bottom. • f697cad15c 2014-07-09 Built a compact arena-allocated array of {fd, smallest, largest} for `Version::Get`/`FindFile` instead of walking full metadata objects: random read 1.8363 to 1.7587 ms/op. • c11d604ab3 2014-07-16 Put `FileIndexer` arrays in sequential arena memory: `Version::Get` CPU 7.98% to 7.42%. • 80f409ea37 2014-06-09 `PlainTableReader` inlines its bloom filter and moves init-only fields to the end. c90d446ee7 stores a hash-link-list node's key right after the node so one prefetch covers both. • aed7abbcca 2018-05-17 A field-access profiler (esan) showed two fields of `InlineSkipList` (comparator and head node) took most accesses, and the struct itself accounted for 10%+ of all memory accesses. Making them adjacent put both in one line.

### rocksdb-055 — Shrink hot objects: remove padding, drop derivable fields, share instead of copy
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Order fields so the compiler inserts no padding, remove members that can be derived or held once elsewhere, and keep a pointer to shared configuration instead of a copy in every object. Where padding is unavoidable, put it to work.
Why it is faster: A bool between two 8-byte fields wastes 7 bytes. An iterator tree where each node copies a 100-byte options struct multiplies that by the tree size. Smaller objects mean more per cache line and per arena block, fewer allocations, less stack and fewer bytes copied. Savings can double under aligned allocators, which round object sizes up to 16 bytes. The trade-off is that grouping by size can split fields that are used together, so weigh it against hot/cold grouping.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Go: order struct fields largest first (the `fieldalignment` linter finds waste); pass a pointer to request-wide config rather than copying it into each handler object. • Native / systems: check `sizeof` and `pahole` output of structs stored in large arrays; use the flyweight pattern for per-node settings in ASTs and scene graphs. • Embedded: pack protocol and control structs by hand; use leftover padding for flags or a small inline buffer. • Data / databases: trim per-row and per-entry headers in in-memory stores, where millions of copies multiply every byte.
Watch out: a struct that gains one field later can silently undo the work, so keep size assertions. Packing by size can separate fields read together. Holding references instead of copies requires the shared object to outlive every holder.
Seen in RocksDB: c2c62ad4e6 2017-05-26 `ReadOptions` reordered from 64 to 48 bytes. • b82e57d425 2018-11-13 Removed the compression-type and cachable flags from `BlockContents` (8 bytes per cached block because of padding), and stopped wrapping compressed blocks in `Block`, saving tens of bytes per compressed-cache entry. • 9ea9007b50 2018-09-12 Removed a redundant `active_comparator_` from `IndexBlockIter` to fix a P50 regression caused by the object growing. • c84fad7a19 2019-03-21 Reordered `DBIter` fields so 1-byte members pack together (the commit notes the cache-locality trade-off). • 643c863b72 2020-07-20 Moved comparator objects out of `BlockIter` onto the stack at the use site; the bloated iterator had caused extra arena heap allocations and slowed `NewIterator()`. a4a4a2dabd stores `ReadOptions` once in the top iterator and has sub-iterators hold a const reference: arena use for a test DB 4,792 to 4,160 bytes. • 4c277ab201 2021-10-14 `MergingIterator` repacked; 02974c9437 `PerfStepTimer` 8 bytes smaller on the stack; 490fcac078 `WriteBatch` 16 bytes smaller. • 0c7e5bd2f0 2025-03-03 Swapping two members shrank `SkipList` from 56 to 48 bytes, and by 16 bytes per object under 16-byte aligned arena allocation (hash-skiplist buckets). • ddfcbea3e1 2023-06-06 Grew `IterKey`'s inline buffer from 32 to 39 bytes "to utilize padding space": more keys avoid a heap allocation at zero size cost.

### rocksdb-056 — Prefer flat, contiguous containers over node-based ones
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Store elements inline in one array (sorted vector, open-addressing table, packed record buffer) instead of one heap node per element (tree maps, chained hash maps, vectors of strings).
Why it is faster: Node-based containers pay an allocation per insert, a header per node and a dependent pointer load per step. A flat layout gives one allocation, no per-element overhead and sequential access the prefetcher can follow. Sorted vectors win when data is built once and searched many times. Open addressing wins for hash lookups and is a prerequisite for lock-free slots. The trade-off is costlier inserts in the middle and rehash or resort costs.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: for small static lookups, a sorted array with binary search often beats a `Map`; use `absl::flat_hash_map`/hashbrown-style maps in native services. • Native / systems: `boost::flat_map` for read-mostly maps; one string arena plus offsets instead of `vector<string>`. • Embedded: fixed-capacity arrays of inline buckets avoid a heap entirely. • Data / databases: pack variable-length records into a single buffer with offsets (FlatBuffers/Arrow-style) for ingestion and sort.
Watch out: flat containers move elements on growth, so pointers into them are invalidated. Frequent middle inserts into a sorted vector are O(n). Open-addressing tables degrade sharply when too full.
Seen in RocksDB: e63350e726 2016-11-19 Replaced `std::unordered_map` in the transaction deadlock wait maps with a fixed array of 128 inline small vectors (`std::array<autovector<pair>,128>`), so inserts and erases under the global lock allocate nothing: deadlock stress 39k to 49k txns/s. • 320d9a8e8a 2022-02-09 Blob file metadata moved from `std::map` to a preallocated sorted vector: fillseq run 2986 to 2611 s, with less DB-mutex hold time. 97b30dee5b dropped a redundant histogram map in favor of `std::lower_bound` on the bucket array. • efd035164b 2022-04-13 An `UnorderedMap` alias swaps in folly's SIMD-probed flat `F14FastMap` for the largest CPU users of `std::unordered_map`. • 3afed7408c 2022-06-21 The cache's per-shard chained hash tables were replaced by an open-addressing table with all handles preallocated inline. • 9abd6e7f6c 2026-11-16 `sst_dump recompress` packs millions of key/value pairs back to back as `[u32 klen][u32 vlen][key][value]` in one lazily zeroed mapping, sized from table properties, instead of a vector of `std::string` pairs.

### rocksdb-057 — Allocate the payload in the same block as the node that indexes it
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Put the key bytes (or other payload) directly after the index node in one allocation instead of pointing from the node to a separately allocated payload.
Why it is faster: Every comparison during a search must read the key. With a separate allocation that is a second dependent cache miss per node visited. Co-locating removes one pointer per node, one allocator call per insert, and the extra miss, because the key sits in the line already being loaded. The node becomes variable-sized, so it needs a custom allocator (an arena) and careful offset arithmetic.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: intrusive lists and trees (Linux `list_head`); flexible array members (`struct node { ...; char key[]; }`) allocated in one shot. • Data / databases: B-tree or trie nodes that keep short keys inline rather than pointing to them. • Web / backend: in JS/Java, prefer storing small primitive fields directly in the object over wrapping them in nested objects.
Watch out: variable-sized nodes complicate freeing and reuse, which is why this pairs with arena allocation. Very large payloads inline push node headers apart and can hurt traversal locality.
Seen in RocksDB: c90d446ee7 2014-04-04 Hash-link-list memtable nodes store the key immediately after the node, which also helps prefetch. • 9a9d4759b2 2015-11-19 `InlineSkipList` (with parts 78812ec6bf and 5201729545): `AllocateNode(key_size, height)` places the tower of `next_[]` pointers at negative offsets before the node and the key straight after it, and stashes the height in `next_[0]` until insert. Single-threaded fillrandom with 16-byte keys went from about 310k to 410k ops/s, and 5-10% faster with large values.

### rocksdb-058 — Separate what you search from what you return (struct of arrays)
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Lay out the search keys contiguously and the payloads in a separate region, and deduplicate repeated keys, so a search touches only key bytes.
Why it is faster: Interleaving keys with values means a binary or linear search drags value bytes through the cache that it never reads. Keeping keys together raises the useful fraction of every loaded line. It also groups similar data, which compresses better. When records repeat the same key with different payloads, storing each distinct key once also cuts comparisons. The cost is an extra offset per entry or per group to find the value.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: struct-of-arrays in physics and ECS game engines; separate metadata or key arrays in hash tables (SwissTable-style). • Data / databases: columnar layouts; a term dictionary plus a postings region in search indexes. • Web / backend: for large in-memory datasets in JS, parallel typed arrays (ids, scores) instead of arrays of objects.
Watch out: fetching a single whole record now touches two regions. It pays when searches dominate or when it improves compression, not when every access reads both key and value.
Seen in RocksDB: 6bee36a786 2018-11-14 `FragmentedRangeTombstoneList` stores a vector of distinct (start, end, index range) stacks and a separate vector of sequence numbers, instead of one record per (fragment, seqno). Binary search then compares only distinct keys. • 901c88e37b 2026-02-23 Option `separate_key_value_in_data_block`: a data block becomes keys section, values section, restart array, values offset and footer, at about 1 bit per entry overhead. SST size fell 18.6% with 1-byte values, 8.5% with 16-byte and 3.2% with 100-byte values; readrandom +6.9% with 1000-byte values; readseq +4.4% with 16-byte values.

### rocksdb-059 — Prefix-compress sorted keys, with restart points only where you need to seek
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: In a sorted run, store each key as (shared length, suffix) relative to the previous key, keep a full key every K entries so binary search still works, and store a prefix shared by the whole block only once.
Why it is faster: Sorted neighbors share long prefixes (table ids, tenant ids, timestamps). Delta encoding often halves block size, so more blocks fit in cache and fewer bytes are read. Restart points bound how far a seek must decode linearly: a small interval gives faster seeks, a large one gives smaller blocks. Data that is only ever read front to back needs no restart points at all. Comparing only stripped suffixes after removing a block-wide prefix also saves CPU per comparison.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: front coding in term dictionaries; prefix-compressed B-tree pages; delta-encoded sorted id lists. • Web / backend: compress sorted URL or path lists and sync payloads by sending shared-prefix lengths. • Embedded: compact sorted lookup tables in flash with a full entry every K rows for binary search.
Watch out: random access within a restart interval and reverse iteration both need forward decoding from the restart point. Random keys with no shared prefixes gain little and still pay the length fields.
Seen in RocksDB: 85584d497e 2012-04-17 LevelDB foundation `block_builder.cc`: varint shared/unshared/value lengths, a `restarts[]` trailer, and restart interval 1 for index blocks. • edd47c5104 2014-06-18 PlainTable prefix encoding avoids rewriting the same prefix. • 8e6172bc57 2016-02-05 A separate `index_block_restart_interval`, so index blocks can be denser or faster to seek than data blocks. • 3593275357 2018-06-12 The properties block, always read sequentially, is written with an infinite restart interval (an earlier attempt for all meta blocks, 593d3de371, was reverted). • 8f492b2f47 2026-08-20 format_version 8 stores the common user-key prefix once at the head of each data and index block. A nonzero `restarts[0]` signals it, so no flag bit is needed, and seeks compare only suffixes. Motivated by MyRocks, ZippyDB and time-series keys and by index-seek CPU rivaling data-seek CPU (fix in ea5db0c142).

### rocksdb-060 — Index with the shortest key that separates two blocks, and nothing more
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: An index entry only needs some key between block A's last key and block B's first key. Store the shortest such separator, and omit suffix fields (versions, sequence numbers) that do not help distinguish neighbors.
Why it is faster: The index is consulted on every lookup and should stay cached. Shorter separators shrink it, so more of it fits in cache and fewer index blocks are fetched. Dropping an 8-byte sequence number from every index key is free whenever no user key spans two blocks. The trade-off: a separator that is too short (a shortened successor for the last block) can steer seeks into the wrong block and cost an extra read.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: suffix truncation in B+tree inner pages (as in Postgres 12); minimal split keys for range partitioning. • Web / backend: shortest opaque pagination cursors that still sort correctly. • Native / systems: sparse indexes over sorted log segments that store truncated boundary keys.
Watch out: a separator is not a real key, so code must never treat it as one. Over-shortening at the end of a range can cost extra I/O on seeks.
Seen in RocksDB: 85584d497e 2012-04-17 LevelDB foundation `table_builder.cc`: `FindShortestSeparator` on a deferred pending index entry, `FindShortSuccessor` for the last block. • e23e73e67c 2014-04-10 Went back to shortened keys for the hash index because they are more space-efficient. • 7c14abf2c7 2016-04-25 Better `FindShortestSeparator` (bump the first non-0xFF byte after the divergence point): index blocks about 8.5% smaller on LogDevice files. • 402b7aa07f 2018-05-25 Index keys exclude the 8-byte seqno/type when no block boundary splits a user key, recorded in the properties block; b73652169e extends it to partitioned index and filters. • df38c1ce66 2019-04-22 Default `index_shortening` changed to separators only: shortened successor keys made seeks land in the previous block and load an unnecessary block.

### rocksdb-061 — Don't store what the reader can predict
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When a field follows from the previous entry (the offset of a block that starts where the last one ended), store only the unpredictable part (its size), with a full value at each restart point.
Why it is faster: Block handles are (offset, size) pairs for consecutive blocks, so the offset is redundant. Delta encoding cuts the index by a large fraction. The decoder spends a little CPU reconstructing values, which is paid back by a higher cache hit rate for the smaller index.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: delta-encoded posting lists and offset tables in columnar or search formats; delta-of-delta timestamps in time-series chunks. • Web / backend: send deltas of monotonically increasing ids or timestamps in API payloads. • Embedded: store sensor samples as deltas with periodic absolute values.
Watch out: random access needs a restart point to decode from. One corrupted entry can break decoding until the next restart.
Seen in RocksDB: caf0f53a74 2018-08-09 format_version 4: index values are delta-encoded, so after the first entry of each restart interval only the block size is stored (`IndexBlockIter::DecodeCurrentValue`). The commit notes "a bit of cpu overhead ... paid back by higher cache hits". • b73652169e 2018-06-06 Carried the new index format to partitioned index and filter blocks.

### rocksdb-062 — Erase metadata no reader can tell apart, then exploit the constant
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When a field no longer affects any reader (a version older than every snapshot), overwrite it with a constant. The data compresses better, special short encodings apply, and code can use the constant as a shortcut.
Why it is faster: Unique per-entry numbers are incompressible. Zeroing them makes runs of identical bytes for the compressor, allows a 1-byte encoding instead of 8 bytes, and gives the reader a fact ("seqno 0 means the oldest version, so no duplicate follows") that lets it skip a key comparison. It is only safe once you can prove no reader can observe the difference.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: freeze old MVCC tuples (Postgres freeze) so transaction ids no longer need storing; strip version vectors from fully converged CRDT data. • Web / backend: drop per-event sequence metadata from archived event-store segments once no consumer depends on it. • Embedded: clear unused timestamp fields in logged records so they compress well before upload.
Watch out: you must prove no snapshot, replica or tool still needs the original value. Getting it wrong causes silent data-visibility bugs.
Seen in RocksDB: 4564915446 2013-02-15 Zero sequence numbers at the bottom level for entries older than the earliest snapshot; 56bea9f80d does the same for universal compaction. • d169b67680 2014-01-27 PlainTable encodes seq=0 plus a value type in 1 byte instead of 8 (saves 7 bytes per key before compression). • 59244447e3 2019-02-01 Removed the exception for the final key, so even it gets seqno 0 at the bottom level. • 25d81e4577 2019-05-09 The payoff on reads: `DBIter` skips the user-key comparison after a seqno-0 key. Comparisons per key in readseq fell from 2.981 to 2.165, and to zero on a fully compacted DB.

### rocksdb-063 — Compress only where the gain beats the CPU, and learn when it doesn't
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Spend compression CPU only on data that is cold, long-lived and actually compressible: store raw when the ratio is poor, skip short-lived or latency-critical data, and track the observed payoff to stop trying on incompressible streams.
Why it is faster: Compression costs CPU on write and on every uncached read. Data rewritten soon (upper LSM levels) or read constantly (filters) gives little back for that CPU. Incompressible data costs a full compression attempt for nothing. A cheap ratio check per block, or a running estimate with occasional sampling, removes most of the wasted work while keeping the space savings where they exist.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: gzip only above a size threshold and never for already-compressed MIME types; learn per endpoint whether compression pays. • Data / databases: leave hot recent time-series chunks uncompressed and compress cold ones harder. • Embedded: sample compressibility of telemetry before spending battery on compressing every packet.
Watch out: an adaptive skip needs ongoing sampling or it never notices compressible data returning. Thresholds tuned for one codec and CPU may be wrong on another.
Seen in RocksDB: 85584d497e 2012-04-17 LevelDB foundation: store a block raw if Snappy saves less than 12.5%. • 321dfdc3ae 2012-10-27 Per-level compression algorithms; 50994bf699 doesn't always compress L0 flush output; f146cab261 centralizes the per-level decision in the compaction picker; f43c8262c2 skips blocks over 2 GB. • 64a1f7670f 2023-02-16 `do_not_compress_roles`: filter blocks are not compressed by default. • 4ce1dc930c 2024-03-20 Skip buffering data and training a compression dictionary when the output won't be compressed, fixing `SstFileWriter::Finish` throughput. • ee6b0def55 2025-07-25 The compressed secondary cache stores incompressible entries uncompressed, with 1-byte headers instead of varints. • fcf382c95c 2026-08-11 `CompressionOptions::auto_skip` keeps a payoff-weighted EWMA of the compression ratio on its own cache line, skips compression when not worth it, and keeps sampling after randomized gaps to notice compressible data returning. On incompressible data CPU went from 2.33 to 0.82 s/GB (-65%) with no size change (replacing the experimental 504ff4ed55).

### rocksdb-064 — Treat the codec, its speed level and its API as tunable costs
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Pick the compressor and its speed/ratio setting per workload, expose acceleration knobs, call the cheapest entry point for fast codecs, and give the compressor a fixed-size output buffer so poor results are rejected early.
Why it is faster: Decompression runs on every uncached read, so a codec that decompresses faster at a similar ratio cuts read CPU across the board. Fast codecs finish so quickly that per-call setup (stream objects, growable output strings, zero-filling) becomes a large share of the cost. A bounded output buffer sized to the largest acceptable result lets the codec abort on poor compression with no extra copy, and reused working state removes per-call initialization.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: pick Brotli or zstd quality per content type; reuse deflate contexts rather than creating one per response. • Data / databases: LZ4 for hot data, zstd for cold; use `ZSTD_compressBound`-sized or capped buffers; benchmark the codec in isolation, not only end to end. • Embedded: LZ4 fast mode for telemetry where CPU and energy dominate.
Watch out: defaults change the on-disk format of new data and the CPU profile of every user, so measure across hardware. Full-system benchmarks are often too noisy to rank codec entry points.
Seen in RocksDB: df2f92214a 2014-02-07 Added LZ4 as a fast codec option. • 4196ad81e3 2023-09-18 LZ4 acceleration via negative `compression_level`: compress throughput about 181k ops/s at level 1, 663k at -10, 2.6M at -100 and 3.0M at -1000. • 0a169cea0e 2025-07-31 `Compressor::CompressBlock` writes into a fixed buffer of the maximum acceptable size, the growable buffer is reused, and LZ4HC reuses its working area: LZ4HC +218-222% throughput, lz4 +7.6-7.9%, snappy +4.4-6.6%. • 9d3afcf543 2025-10-02 Benchmarked LZ4 variants and went back to the basic one-shot API, faster than per-call streams, reused streams or extState; e231c1826c revamped `sst_dump --recompress` into a low-noise benchmark for such choices. a6af317476 fixed a lookup bug that picked the ZSTD digested-dictionary decompressor for LZ4. • 8053b9414f 2026-06-04 Default codec switched from Snappy to LZ4: about 25-30% less read CPU on AMD, Intel and ARM, and decompression throughput +45-50% at 160 threads.

### rocksdb-065 — Prime small-block compression with a dictionary sampled from the data
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Small blocks compress poorly on their own because the compressor starts with no history. Sample the data, build one dictionary per file (or narrower scope), and prime every block with it.
Why it is faster: Blocks must stay small (a few KB) for random reads, but compressors need context to find repetition. A shared dictionary supplies that context, so each block compresses as if it had seen the rest of the file. A dictionary trained on a narrower sample (one output file) fits better than one shared across a whole job. Building it costs buffering memory and training CPU. Finalizing a raw sample dictionary is a cheap middle ground.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: zstd or Brotli dictionaries for small JSON API responses (HTTP Compression Dictionary Transport). • Data / databases: per-tenant or per-column dictionaries for log and row storage. • Embedded: a fixed dictionary for compressing short telemetry messages.
Watch out: the reader needs the same dictionary, so store or cache it with the data. Buffering a whole file for sampling costs memory, so cap it.
Seen in RocksDB: 843d2e3137 2016-04-27 A dictionary meta block sampled from the first output file of each subcompaction, used for zlib/LZ4/LZ4HC on write and read. • 62f70f6d14 2019-02-11 Dictionary scoped to a single SST: buffer the uncompressed data up to the target file size, sample, train, then compress. c8c8104d7e extends it to `SstFileWriter`. • d904233d2f 2021-02-19 `max_dict_buffer_bytes` caps the buffering, and `SwapAndReset()` avoids an allocation and memcpy; daca92c17a picks samples with a prime stride in one allocation-free code path. • cc23b46da1 2022-05-20 Run zstd's `ZDICT_finalizeDictionary` on raw samples: on synthetic data 251 MB to 236 MB and compaction 16.6 to 12.6 s; on real SSTs close to a trained dictionary with less CPU.

### rocksdb-066 — Write down the sizes a reader would otherwise have to guess
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Record lengths that the consumer needs (decompressed size, size of trailing metadata) at write time, so it can allocate once and read once instead of guessing and growing.
Why it is faster: Without the output size, decompression must guess a buffer and grow it in steps, copying each time. With a varint header the reader allocates exactly once. The same applies on the write side: estimating the metadata that will be appended at the end keeps output files from overshooting their target size. It costs a few header bytes per block.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: send `Content-Length` instead of chunked encoding when known; length-prefix protobuf messages. • Data / databases: store footer and index lengths so a reader fetches them in one request; reserve footer space when cutting segments. • Embedded: length-prefixed frames so the receiver can allocate from a fixed pool once.
Watch out: a size field is untrusted input on read and must be bounds-checked before allocating. Adding it changes the format, so it needs versioning.
Seen in RocksDB: 9ab5adfc59 2015-01-14 format_version 2 stores the decompressed size in compressed zlib/bzip2/LZ4 blocks, replacing a guess-and-grow loop (input length times 5, then +20% steps) and a non-portable memcpy of `size_t`. Made default in 12fd9b1868. • 7603712a88 2025-11-05 `BlockBasedTableBuilder::EstimatedTailSize()` includes the index and filter blocks that will be appended at finish in the file-cutting decision, updated per data block rather than per key.

### rocksdb-067 — Partition large metadata behind a small top level
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Split a large per-file index into fixed-size partitions plus a tiny top-level index, keep the top level pinned, and cut each kind of metadata at its own target size.
Why it is faster: A monolithic index must be loaded and cached whole, even if a workload touches a small key range. Partitions let the cache hold only the hot parts, which also keeps cache entries a uniform, cache-friendly size. Pinning the top level removes a cache lookup and a possible miss from every read. If two kinds of partitions are cut together, the smaller kind ends up undersized and is evicted unfairly, so cut each at its own size.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: multi-level page tables; pin the root of a B+tree in the buffer pool. • Data / databases: per-segment indexes behind a small segment directory in search engines and data lakes. • Web / backend: a small manifest that points to sharded asset or route tables loaded on demand.
Watch out: a point lookup can now cost two cache lookups (or two I/Os) instead of one. Partitioning pays off for large indexes under a tight memory budget, not for small files.
Seen in RocksDB: 69d5262c81 2017-02-06 Two-level index (`kTwoLevelIndexSearch`). • 1efc600ddf 2017-08-18 Preload and pin L0 index partitions. • 80ade9ad83 2018-06-22 `pin_top_level_index_and_filter` keeps the top level in the cache but pinned, avoiding misses and lookup overhead. • 4d3518951a 2024-08-16 `decouple_partitioned_filters`: index and filter partitions are cut independently at their own target sizes; SST write CPU neutral. Made default in 5c7162da27.

### rocksdb-068 — Add a tiny hash directory to sorted blocks for point lookups
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Append a compact hash table that maps keys straight to a position in a sorted block, falling back to binary search on collision. Sorted order is kept for scans, and point lookups get O(1).
Why it is faster: Binary search over restart points costs about log2(n) comparisons and cache misses per lookup. A one-byte-per-bucket table locates the right restart interval in one probe. Sentinels for "no entry" and "collision" keep it tiny (about 1 byte per key) and correct. It costs space and build time, and helps only point reads.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: a secondary hash directory inside B-tree leaves; slot hashes in page formats. • Web / backend: a static asset manifest with a small hash table over a sorted list. • Embedded: a byte-wide bucket table in front of a sorted flash table.
Watch out: it adds space to every block and does nothing for range scans. Collision handling must fall back correctly, or lookups return wrong answers.
Seen in RocksDB: 906f3dca72 2014-02-18 Hash index from prefix to block for block-based tables (with 75b59d5146). 0b3d03d026 persists it so it isn't rebuilt at open; 0f0076ed5a uses a bucket array that doesn't store prefixes and tolerates collisions; 4a8f0c957c checks the previous boundary key to reject collision false hits (about 80% better in the favoured setting, under 3% penalty otherwise). • f040e536e4 2013-11-21 PlainTable index uses 32-bit buckets with no keys, and a high bit chooses between a direct offset and a second-level index. • 19ec44fd39 2018-08-15 In-block hash index (`kDataBlockBinaryAndHash`): uint8 buckets mapping to restart indexes, flagged by the top bit of the restart count for backward compatibility. • da40d45267 2018-08-23 On a "no entry" result, search only the last restart interval instead of stepping into the next block: readmissing 4.10 to 3.35 µs/op (binary search: 3.61). • f631138e1c 2023-04-07 When the hash index finds a merge-type key, the block seek is not redone: readrandom with merges +5%.

### rocksdb-069 — Store a per-block summary in the index so data can be read later, or never
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Keep enough about each data block in the index (its first key) that merges and range checks can be decided without loading the block.
Why it is faster: A merge over many sorted files only needs each file's current smallest key to order them. If the index already holds a block's first key, the block is fetched only when its file reaches the top of the merge heap. For short scans most files never do, so their data blocks are never read. The index grows by one key per block.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: min/max per chunk (zone maps) to skip or defer chunk reads. • Web / backend: merge sorted remote streams using only their head records; render placeholder rows from headers in virtualized lists.
Watch out: long keys inflate the index, which competes for the same cache. It pays most for short range scans over many files.
Seen in RocksDB: b4d7209428 2019-06-24 `IndexType::kBinarySearchWithFirstKey` stores each block's first key in the index and defers reading the data block until the file reaches the top of the merging heap.

### rocksdb-070 — Pad blocks so no logical block straddles a physical unit
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: If the storage device reads in fixed units (4 KB pages, larger "super blocks"), pad the file so each logical block lies within one unit.
Why it is faster: A 4 KB block that crosses a 4 KB page boundary needs two device pages for one logical read, doubling IOPS for that read. A little padding guarantees one unit per read. The cost is wasted space and, for small-unit padding, a requirement that blocks be fixed-size (uncompressed, power-of-two).
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: align records to sector size for direct I/O; page-aligned slabs in mmapped files. • Data / databases: align pages to SSD erase or object-store chunk boundaries. • Embedded: keep records within one flash page so a read or program touches one page.
Watch out: padding wastes space. Cap the overhead ratio, and check the real unit size of the storage underneath, which may differ from 4 KB.
Seen in RocksDB: f9f4d40f93 2018-03-26 `block_align` pads data blocks to min(block size, 4 KB); requires a power-of-two block size and no compression. • 742741b175 2025-10-01 `super_block_alignment_size` with a space-overhead ratio: power-of-two padding stops an SST block splitting across the storage layer's super blocks, with no fillseq regression (202 vs 204 MB/s).

### rocksdb-071 — Encode a sorted key set as a succinct trie
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Represent index keys as a level-ordered bitmap trie with rank/select support, dense bitmaps near the root and sparse label arrays below, so seeks work directly on the compact encoding.
Why it is faster: A full-key index repeats shared prefixes and stores each key whole. A succinct trie stores each branch once, in a few bits per node, so far more of the index fits in cache, and it supports seek without decompressing. Rank is O(1) with a small sampled lookup table plus popcount. Small details matter: linear scan over sparse nodes beats a mispredicted binary search, and the hottest popcount loop is worth unrolling.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: FST-style term dictionaries; compact range-filter indexes. • Web / backend: autocomplete or routing tables shipped to clients in compact form. • Embedded: on-device lexicons and lookup tables in limited flash.
Watch out: succinct structures are complex to build and debug, and each step costs more CPU than a plain array lookup. They pay off when memory, not CPU, is the constraint.
Seen in RocksDB: ce0fff9f41 2026-02-26 SuRF-inspired trie index: rank LUT sampled every 256 bits with uint32 entries (half the memory of uint64), a single-pass streaming builder, zero-copy handle arrays, a fanout-1 fast path, linear scan for nodes with 16 or fewer labels, and rank reuse during descent. • 41e5eb1a4d 2026-03-31 Bit-scan helpers moved into the header, the `Rank1` popcount loop unrolled, and the path-stack top replaced in place instead of pop+push. • 5f39d7b8ea 2026-10-30 A two-phase prepare/finish entry API so the trie builder works with parallel compression.

### rocksdb-072 — Use a separate, flat format for data that lives entirely in memory
Lens: memory-layout · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Formats built for disk (compressed blocks, restart points, a block cache) waste CPU when the data is fully RAM-resident. Offer a flat format with an in-memory index for that case, and persist the index so open doesn't rebuild it.
Why it is faster: For mmapped, RAM-resident data, every block decode, cache lookup and copy is pure overhead. A flat layout addressed through a compact hash index and a bloom filter goes straight to the key. Building the index at open costs CPU and I/O, so storing it in the file trades space for faster startup. The format gives up compression and suits read-mostly data.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: memory-mapped read-only lookup files (CDB, sparkey) for configuration and feature data. • Native / systems: flat asset packs in games loaded with mmap. • Data / databases: static search indexes for static sites.
Watch out: a second format doubles maintenance and testing, and assumes the data really stays resident. Under memory pressure, an mmap format without its own cache becomes page-fault bound.
Seen in RocksDB: b59d4d5a50 2013-10-28 "A Simple Plain Table": a flat, mmap-friendly SST format. • f040e536e4 2013-11-21 Compact PlainTable hash index (32-bit buckets, no keys). • d169b67680 2014-01-27 1-byte internal-key suffix when seqno is 0. • 9d70cce047 2014-07-18 Saves the index and bloom filter in the file to avoid rebuilding them at open. • 3c68006109 2014-09-25 `CompactedDBImpl`: a read-only, single-level DB with a simplified Get; fbd2dafc9f adds MultiGet with prefetch, 3M to 4.9M lookups/s.

### rocksdb-073 — Keep the common small case on the stack or inline
Lens: allocation · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Give objects that are usually small a fixed inline buffer (on the stack or inside the owner) and fall back to the heap only when they overflow it. Make the "nothing to report" case allocate nothing.
Why it is faster: Most keys, child lists and error results are small. An inline buffer turns their allocation into a pointer bump on the stack that is already in cache, and removes the free. Small-size optimization keeps one code path with a rare heap fallback. Inline storage should be raw bytes, constructed on insert, so unused slots cost nothing. The trade-off is larger objects, which matters if many sit in arrays, and the inline capacity must match the real size distribution.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `absl::InlinedVector`, LLVM `SmallVector`, Rust `SmallVec`/`arrayvec`; error types whose common variant is a plain code. • Web / backend: in Go, keep small scratch arrays on the stack (escape-analysis friendly); in Java, avoid allocating exception messages on hot "not found" paths. • Embedded: fixed-capacity buffers with an explicit overflow path instead of a general heap. • Data / databases: inline short keys in lookup structs; heap only for long ones.
Watch out: big inline buffers bloat objects held in large numbers and can overflow small thread stacks. Pick the capacity from the measured size distribution.
Seen in RocksDB: 18df47b79a 2013-12-26 `Status` stores its code inline, and NotFound without a message allocates nothing; malloc+free had been about 8% of a miss-heavy readrandom. • c01676e46d 2013-12-12 `autovector`: a stack array that overflows to a vector, adopted in 774ed89c24. 959f86e5f8 changed its inline buffer to raw bytes with placement new on insert, so unused slots are never constructed. • ddd41146c4 2014-05-08 `MergingIterator` keeps up to 4 children inline: "reduces CPU of MergingIterator::Seek() by half". • e0a87c4cf1 2014-04-01 `DBIter` keeps short saved keys in an inline buffer (like `LookupKey`'s 200-byte buffer in the foundation); 5e2db3b434 PlainTable iterator key buffer instead of `std::string`, 1100 to 750 ns; b0999011e2 stack buffer for compressed block reads; 0c9dc9f8e0 file-name formatting without malloc. • da9274574f 2014-07-23 `IterKey` inside `Block::Iter`: malloc 4.26% to 2.91% of CPU. • d9bca1e14c 2016-01-11 Single-element fast path: the current iterator is destroyed directly unless pinned, instead of always inserted into a `std::set`: newiterator 1.0 to 0.75 µs/op. • e92a0ed040 2021-12-13 `FooterBuilder` owns its serialized bytes inline so it lives on the stack: fillseq +1.7%, recovering a 4.2% regression. • b12d3a6da6 2026-04-28 `SstFileReader::Get` uses `LookupKey` instead of appending to a heap string; bc11adb4ba adds a `char*` overload of internal-key encoding so callers write into preallocated storage.

### rocksdb-074 — Reuse containers and scratch objects across calls instead of rebuilding them
Lens: allocation · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Keep a container, buffer or helper object as a long-lived member, clear it (keeping capacity) between uses, and swap instead of reallocating. In steady state, the loop allocates nothing.
Why it is faster: Constructing a `std::string`, deque or iterator per call costs an allocation, initialization and a free, every time. A cleared member keeps its capacity, so after warm-up there are no allocations and the memory stays hot in cache. Pools extend this to objects handed between threads. The trade-off is memory held at peak size, and stale state if a reset is missed.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Go `sync.Pool` for request buffers; reuse a `StringBuilder` per worker in Java; reset typed arrays rather than reallocating in JS render loops. • Native / systems: per-thread scratch vectors in game loops; double buffers when the previous value must stay readable. • Embedded: statically allocated work buffers reused for every message. • Data / databases: reuse decode buffers and row objects across rows in a scan.
Watch out: reused buffers hold peak-size memory forever, so cap or shrink them occasionally. Forgetting to clear state leaks data between calls.
Seen in RocksDB: 8f09d53fd1 2014-07-30 Reuse the data and index block iterators in Get: malloc 3.30% to 1.42% and free 3.59% to 1.61% of CPU. • 8c71eb5afc 2016-01-07 A `merge_operands_` member in `DBIter` instead of a per-call deque: readreverse about 1.5M to 2.0M ops/s; 8a1a603fdb the same on the forward path; efd013d6d8 a reusable `InternalKey` member in `SstFileWriter::Add`. • edcbb36944 2017-09-14 `DBIter` reuses a member `ParsedInternalKey` (with two other tweaks, about 3% readseq). • 62829ff751 2019-08-26 Reuse a scratch string in the transaction-log iterator; 03a781a90c parallel compression keeps string capacity across clears and pools block objects. • fec4403ff1 2022-03-09 WAL compression keeps a streaming compressor in the writer and reuses one output buffer. • 9058fd037c 2024-07-29 A `Reset()` plus `std::swap` for a range-filter result object, because "vector allocation/sizing was substantial in a CPU profile". 32dd657bad builds keys from a reused array of slice pieces straight into `IterKey`, which gains a secondary buffer to alternate between.

### rocksdb-075 — Allocate same-lifetime objects from an arena and free them all at once
Lens: allocation · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When many small objects die together (a memtable, an iterator tree, a request), carve them out of large blocks with a bump pointer and release the blocks in one go.
Why it is faster: Bump allocation is a few instructions, has no per-object header and never takes a lock. Objects allocated together sit next to each other, which helps locality. Freeing is one call per block instead of one per object. Large requests (over a quarter of a block) get their own block to limit waste. The trade-off: nothing can be freed individually, so memory is held until the whole arena dies.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: per-request arenas (Apache pools, Rust `bumpalo`, protobuf arenas) freed when the response is sent. • Native / systems: per-frame allocators in games; AST arenas in compilers. • Embedded: a static arena per session reset on disconnect, with no general heap. • Data / databases: per-query or per-memtable arenas.
Watch out: a long-lived arena that keeps growing is a leak. Destructors are not run automatically, so objects needing cleanup must be tracked separately.
Seen in RocksDB: 85584d497e 2012-04-17 LevelDB foundation `util/arena.cc`: 4 KB blocks, a fallback path for objects over a quarter block, used for skiplist nodes. Never freeing until the memtable dies is also what lets readers traverse the skiplist without locks. • 12b6d2b839 2014-01-08 Aligned allocations come from one end of each block and unaligned ones from the other, cutting alignment waste. • 5656367416 2014-06-30 Memtable bloom filter and hash-skiplist buckets come from the memtable arena; 11da8bc5df uses the last block's fullness to decide when a memtable is full. • df9069d23f 2014-06-02 The whole DB iterator tree is allocated in one arena (`ArenaWrappedDBIter`); 05e2831966 adds level-file iterator helpers to it.

### rocksdb-076 — Size arena blocks to the real workload: inline first block, capped blocks, per-core shards
Lens: allocation · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Serve tiny arenas from an inline first block, cap block size so mostly empty instances don't reserve megabytes, and give concurrent writers small per-core shards that fall back to the main arena for large requests.
Why it is faster: A fixed large block is wasteful when there are thousands of mostly empty arenas, and it causes page-fault and RSS spikes when it is touched. Blocks that are too small make more allocator calls. An inline first block lets small arenas live inside their owner. Per-core shards let threads allocate in parallel without a shared lock, but shard blocks that are too large multiply memory by the core count.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: start request arenas small and grow geometrically; cap buffer sizes held in Go pools. • Native / systems: per-CPU slab caches (SLUB, jemalloc tcache); JVM TLAB sizing. • Embedded: size arenas from the actual worst-case session, not a round number.
Watch out: per-core memory multiplies by the core count, which grows from one machine generation to the next. Re-check defaults set years ago.
Seen in RocksDB: 0f0a24e298 2013-07-31 Configurable arena block size. • 3e4a9ec241 2014-05-09 A 2 KB inline first block, so small arenas live on the stack or inside the owning object. • 7d87f02799 2015-08-14 `ConcurrentArena`: cache-line-padded shards indexed by a thread-local CPU id, guarded by `try_lock` spinlocks, falling back to the main arena for big requests, for concurrent memtable inserts (default in 972e3ff295). • b5b2b75e52 2015-09-01 Default arena block size set to write_buffer/8 or /16, aligned to 4 KB to avoid allocator slack. • e85f2c64cb 2017-07-28 `ConcurrentArena` serves from the inline block while it lasts: empty memtables stopped taking 4 MB each (OOM with about 40k column families). 4bb1e239b5 caps per-core shard blocks at 128 KB (64 cores times 1 MB could pre-reserve 64 MB and trigger flushes). • 0454f781c2 2018-02-15 The arena's block list no longer calls `reserve(size()+1)` per block, which was O(N) reallocation. • a4919d6b62 2021-05-07 Automatic arena block size capped at 1 MB to avoid page-fault storms.

### rocksdb-077 — Reserve when you know the size; grow in chunks when you don't
Lens: allocation · Source: RocksDB
Principle: If the final size is known or cheaply estimated, allocate it once. If it is unknown and possibly large, grow geometrically from small, or use a chunked container that never copies.
Why it is faster: Growth by doubling copies every element about twice over and briefly holds two buffers. One upfront reserve removes that. Allocating the maximum up front wastes memory for small cases, and a doubling vector can be up to 2x oversized at the end. A chunked deque grows with almost no slack, no copying and no need for one huge contiguous region. Reserving for a single element is itself waste.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `new StringBuilder(capacity)`, `new ArrayList(n)`, Go `make([]T, 0, n)`, preallocated typed arrays in JS. • Native / systems: `Vec::with_capacity`; rope or chunk lists for log buffers of unknown size. • Embedded: compute buffer sizes at compile time where possible. • Data / databases: size hash tables from cardinality estimates before a bulk build.
Watch out: overestimates waste memory, and estimates from untrusted input can be abused. Lint rules help find missing reserves at scale.
Seen in RocksDB: 9ea8bf90f1 2014-01-14 `DB::Put` estimates and reserves the `WriteBatch` size (`string::reserve` showed in CPU profiles; with 51dd21926c). 013e9ebbf1 precomputes the total for one allocation; 8478f380a0 removed reserves where only one element is expected. • 0175d58c3c 2017-06-13 The direct-I/O write buffer doubles up to a maximum, copying existing data, instead of always allocating the maximum or flushing early. • a92bd0a183 2019-12-15 The filter builder's hash accumulator changed from `std::vector` to `std::deque`: build 52.2 to 39.1 ns/key for 6M keys, with lower peak RSS. • 9d66d6d13e 2021-10-18 `reserve(block_size)` for block contents: fillseq +3.3-4.4%; resetting restarts with `resize(1)` gave another +1.4-2.3%. 0cc0543893 preallocates a hash map to avoid rehashing (fillseq ingest 241 to 263 MB/s). • 02ea0d6367 2024-04-17 Reserve in `GetLiveFilesMetaData`. • 0f15da1c2d 2026-07-06 `MultiGet` reserves its per-key contexts; a21f416a9b added `reserve` to 125 `push_back` loops across 97 files, found by clang-tidy.

### rocksdb-078 — Hand out pinned views of cached data instead of copies
Lens: allocation · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Return a slice that points into the source buffer (cache block, mmap, blob) and keeps it alive with a reference, released when the caller is done, instead of copying the bytes into a caller-owned string.
Why it is faster: A copy costs time and bandwidth proportional to the value size, plus an allocation. Pinning costs a refcount and a cleanup callback, a fixed cost, so the gain grows with value size. It needs a lifetime protocol: the source must stay valid while pinned, and pins must be released promptly or they hold cache memory. Pinning memory that is immortal anyway gains nothing and just accumulates objects.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Node `Buffer.subarray` instead of `Buffer.from`; Netty ref-counted `ByteBuf` slices; `memoryview` in Python. • Native / systems: `std::string_view`/`&[u8]` borrowing from mmap; `sendfile`/`splice`. • Data / databases: Arrow buffers shared across operators; return row views over buffer-pool pages. • Embedded: pass DMA buffer descriptors up the stack rather than copying payloads.
Watch out: long-held pins block eviction and inflate memory. A view used after release is a use-after-free. For tiny values, the pin bookkeeping can cost more than the copy.
Seen in RocksDB: aececc209e 2015-12-16 `ReadOptions::pin_data` keeps iterator keys and values pinned in blocks: up to 2x on a copy benchmark. d719b095dc adds a central `PinnedIteratorsManager`; 6e801b0bd1 pins in `Prev()`, "up to 1.5X". • 68a8e6b8fa 2016-07-20 `FullMergeV2` takes merge operands as slices instead of copied strings: readseq with 10 KB operands about 254K to 4.0M ops/s. d11c09d9e2 does the same for the forward iterator. • 11526252cc 2017-03-13 `PinnableSlice` for Get: +1.8% at 100 B values, +11.5% at 1 KB, +26% at 10 KB. 0655b58582 row-cache hits return pinned slices; ccf7f833e3 transactions API; 235ab9dd32 pins mmapped files for read-only DBs. • c987eb4712 2022-07-06 Blob reads served from the cache hand the caller a pinned view that owns the cache handle; fe56cb9aa0 reads blobs into objects built with the cache's allocator (two copies removed); b07217da04 pins the newly cached blob; 06b04127a8 releases the pin as soon as the iterator moves. • 32f66712c8 2025-10-27 C API Get uses `PinnableSlice` instead of an intermediate `std::string`, halving allocations and copies (follow-ups in 1bb704b6e0). • 81ea677072 2026-07-20 `PinnableWideColumns` holds columns as slices into several address-stable buffers, so resolved blob columns are spliced in instead of re-serializing the entity; 744f120b12 extends it to same-file blobs. • 9457606301 2026-10-28 Iterators over immortal mmap tables free blocks instead of pinning them, so memory no longer grows per seek.

### rocksdb-079 — Let many views share one owner, and move cleanups instead of objects
Lens: allocation · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When N results point into the same buffer, take one reference and wrap it in a small refcounted owner that all N share. When a stack object's resources must outlive it, transfer its cleanup callbacks to a longer-lived owner rather than moving the object to the heap.
Why it is faster: N Ref/Release pairs on a shared cache handle mean N trips to a contended shard. One handle plus a relaxed atomic counter local to the request is far cheaper, and a purpose-built owner avoids `shared_ptr`'s extra control-block allocation. Delegating cleanups lets the worker object stay on the stack. Work should also scale with what was produced, not with caller-supplied capacity.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: one refcounted network slab with many zero-copy views (Netty, Tokio `Bytes`). • Native / systems: one `Arc` for a buffer instead of a clone per slice; move `defer`/drop guards into a parent scope's collector. • Data / databases: Parquet/Arrow readers sharing one decompressed page across all values that reference it.
Watch out: one long-lived view keeps the whole buffer alive. Ownership transfer must be airtight, or cleanups run twice or never.
Seen in RocksDB: 0712d541d1 2016-12-29 `Cleanable::DelegateCleanupsTo`: the block iterator in Get stays on the stack and hands its cleanups to the pinned-iterators manager: +1.8% on the merge workload, +6.4% on the non-merge workload. • 9d0cae7104 2022-04-26 `SharedCleanablePtr`: MultiGet results that point into one cached block share a single handle instead of one `Ref()` each (merge operands were the worst case). • 504fe4de80 2022-08-04 `GetMergeOperands` pins one SuperVersion/MergeContext bundle shared by all returned slices. • 302eaa8b1f 2026-10-28 `GetMergeOperands` resets only the output slots it fills (previously all of them, for example 1000 slots to read 3 operands), and read-only modes pin results through the shared bundle instead of copying.

### rocksdb-080 — Produce data directly in its final place, and work on encoded bytes in place
Lens: allocation · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Decompress or read straight into the destination buffer, skip staging buffers when the transform is the identity, gather pieces instead of concatenating, and compare or extract fields from the encoded bytes without building a decoded copy.
Why it is faster: Each intermediate buffer costs an allocation, a full memcpy and its cache footprint. If the data is already readable where it lies (an aligned I/O buffer, an mmap, an uncompressed block), pointing at it is free. Decoding a key into an object just to compare it costs a construction and destruction per comparison. The trade-off is code that must handle several buffer origins and lifetimes.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `writev` and scatter/gather responses instead of string concatenation; avoid `Buffer.concat` for a single chunk; bypass the gzip stream for identity encoding. • Native / systems: decode images directly into GPU buffers; reserve header space so headers can be prepended without copying. • Data / databases: compare binary keys with `memcmp`; read FlatBuffers/Cap'n Proto fields without deserializing. • Embedded: parse protocol fields in place in the DMA buffer.
Watch out: pointing into a buffer ties your result's lifetime to it. Make sure the buffer is not reused while the view is live.
Seen in RocksDB: 60bd8015f2 2011-07-21 Snappy decompresses into a flat array, removing one copy: readrandom about +10%. • 8a46ecd357 2013-11-07 `WriteBatch::Put` with `SliceParts` gathers header and payload without the caller concatenating them. • e78715c29a 2018-02-07 Block reads skip the stack staging buffer when table properties say nothing is compressed. 05dab3aacd BlobDB reads into a plain `char[]` instead of misusing `string::reserve`. • f184bee77b 2019-01-25 PlainTable doesn't copy Get results from immortal mmapped files. • 0a0151fb99 2020-03-06 Direct-I/O reads return a slice into the internal aligned buffer instead of copying into scratch; 40497a875a decompresses straight from that buffer, one memcpy fewer per compressed block; 8458532d58 passes an inline stack buffer (under 5000 bytes) as mmap scratch. • ae152ee666 2019-07-25 `WriteBatch` can write per-key timestamps in place, and a `LookupKey` constructor avoids a copy: readrandom 15.53 to 16.70 MB/s. • 6cdb7af9f8 2022-11-28 A comparator between an encoded slice and a parsed key removes copying of range-tombstone keys: readseq 26.0M to 27.5M ops/s. bc0db33483 stops decoding into `InternalKey` (and destroying it) just to compare, after it showed in a flame graph.

### rocksdb-081 — Embed owned helpers by value and borrow shared ones by plain pointer
Lens: allocation · Source: RocksDB
Principle: A member owned 1:1 by its parent belongs inside the parent, not behind `unique_ptr`/`shared_ptr`. An object whose lifetime is guaranteed elsewhere should be passed as a raw pointer or reference, not a refcounted copy.
Why it is faster: Each separate heap member costs a malloc/free per parent and a pointer chase per use. Each `shared_ptr` copy is an atomic increment and decrement on a shared cache line, which becomes contention when many threads copy the same pointer. Embedding and borrowing remove both. Borrowing only works when ownership is clear and documented.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: borrow `&T` instead of cloning `Arc<T>` in Rust hot loops; inline structs instead of `Box`; avoid by-value `shared_ptr` parameters in C++. • Web / backend: Go struct embedding; plain references to singletons in Swift to avoid ARC traffic. • Embedded: compose structs by value; avoid heap-allocated strategy objects.
Watch out: a borrowed raw pointer is a dangling-pointer risk if the owner's lifetime assumption breaks. Embedding very large members makes the parent large to copy or move.
