### rocksdb-082 — Remove hidden copies: move, borrow, and point to static data
Lens: allocation · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Make frequently returned value types movable, take non-owning views at API boundaries, iterate by reference, and point at static strings for common messages, so allocations and memcpys don't hide in ordinary-looking code.
Why it is faster: A `const std::string&` parameter forces a heap string when callers pass a literal. A range-for by value copies every element. A return without move copies a container. Each is invisible in the source but shows up in profiles. Linters find them in bulk. Some cases are dramatic: a debug hook that built a `std::string` per call dominated an entire test.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `std::string_view`/`&str`/`ReadOnlySpan` parameters; turn on clang-tidy `performance-*` or clippy `needless_pass_by_value`. • Web / backend: package-level error values in Go instead of formatting per call; avoid object spread copies in JS reducers on hot paths. • Embedded: error codes and messages in flash, never formatted at runtime on the fast path.
Watch out: a moved-from object is valid but unspecified, so don't reuse it by accident. A borrowed view must not outlive the data it points at.
Seen in RocksDB: 77a28615ec 2015-08-14 Status subcodes point to static messages instead of allocating and copying. • dbb8260f7e 2015-12-22 Status move constructor and assignment: "avoids 99% of the copies". efd013d6d8 adds a Slice move constructor. • 9f246298e2 2017-01-11 Range-for loops by `const&` (also 141ef7f8d3); a2de8e52bb `autovector::emplace_back` perfect-forwards instead of building a temporary. • ef0fc1b461 2019-04-10 Move a value containing a vector into an `unordered_map` with `emplace`; 6dea7530b5 and 03dbd11ead stop copying pairs in loops. • 678ba5e41c 2021-10-15 `SyncPoint::Process` takes a slice and a `char[N]` template instead of `const std::string&`: a load-and-iterate test went from 32 min 17 s to 1 min 14 s, and live heap objects from 5.1M to 105K. • 4500a0d6ec 2023-08-06 A `std::map` copy that showed in a user's CPU profile became a move; ddde1e6af8 avoids a descriptor copy; 2f712235ab more `std::move`. • 780280b52e 2025-01-16 Removed needless `MutableCFOptions` copies in `LogAndApply`; e591f5f1ab fixed 372 clang-tidy findings (unnecessary value parameters, range-for copies, missing automatic move) across 146 files.

### rocksdb-083 — Allocate rarely used members on first use, not at construction
Lens: allocation · Source: RocksDB
Principle: An object created per request should not allocate maps, arenas, scratch buffers or helpers that only rare paths need. Defer them (in place, with `optional` or aligned storage) or return null instead of an empty placeholder.
Why it is faster: Default-constructing a container is not always free: some implementations allocate a sentinel node, and an embedded scratch buffer bloats every frame. When the object is created on every read, that cost is paid millions of times for features most calls never use. In-place deferred construction keeps the fast path allocation-free without adding a heap indirection. The cost is a check on each use.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: lazily create maps on request-context objects; React lazy state initializers. • Native / systems: `std::optional`/`Option<T>` members instead of boxed ones; keep big arrays out of async state machines (Rust futures, C# async). • Data / databases: allocate per-row scratch only on rows that need it (spills, merges).
Watch out: lazy init adds a branch and, with threads, a race. If the "rare" path turns out to be common, the deferred allocation just moves the cost.
Seen in RocksDB: a8029fdc75 2013-12-02 Get's merge context allocates its operand list only when merges appear (with ef2211a9ca). • be723a8d8c 2017-03-22 `MemTableInserter`'s post-processing map lives in `std::aligned_storage` and is constructed on first use; on Windows its eager construction had cost about 40% of fillrandom throughput. • 3f62215210 2016-11-18 `RangeDelAggregator` builds its map and pinning manager lazily (about 3% of total CPU); fe349db57b removed its arena, whose construction alone hurt reads; fd43ee09da returns `nullptr` instead of an empty iterator. • 7c6c632ea9 2024-05-28 A conditional read lock in `std::optional` instead of a heap object; 7bf6d4c9d5 constructs an iterator's block-handle deque lazily: seekrandom 47,811 to 51,013 ops/s (+6.7%). • 2d0eacf68a 2026-11-10 Seek copied a readahead-trim prefix before the prefix-filter check, which rejected about 94% of seeks; moving the copy after the check (reusing the buffer with `assign()`) cut instructions per probe by 5.1-5.4% for 32-byte prefixes. • c2b86f3ec0 2026-10-02 An 8 KiB scratch buffer is no longer embedded in the MultiGet coroutine frame and is allocated only when I/O happens: frame size 21,048 to 12,864 bytes (-38.9%).

### rocksdb-084 — Request exactly what the allocator will hand you
Lens: allocation · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Allocators round requests up to size classes. Either grow the object to fill the usable size it actually got, or split variable-size data into pieces that each land just under a size class.
Why it is faster: A 4.5 KB request may consume an 8 KB slot. Across millions of cache entries that slack is a large share of memory. Filling it (for a filter, more bits mean a lower false-positive rate for free) or avoiding it (chunks sized to bins) means more useful data per byte of RAM, so higher hit rates at the same budget. An aggregate balance can keep the average quality target while individual objects round up or down.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: grow a vector's capacity to the usable size (jemalloc `nallocx`, mimalloc); pick arena chunk sizes that match slab classes. • Web / backend: size pooled network buffers to the allocator's or slab's classes; memcached slab-class tuning. • Embedded: choose record sizes that exactly fill fixed pool blocks or flash pages.
Watch out: this depends on allocator internals that vary between allocators and versions. `malloc_usable_size` must be called on memory from the same allocator.
Seen in RocksDB: 5b2bbacb6f 2020-06-22 `optimize_filters_for_memory`: allocate, read `malloc_usable_size`, grow the filter to fill it, and keep an atomic rounding balance so gains from rounding up pay for later rounding down. About 10% less memory and block-cache charge for filters with jemalloc. 239d17a19c extends it to Ribbon filters. • 87b82f28a1 2022-08-02 The compressed secondary cache splits values into chunks that fall just under jemalloc bin sizes: resident memory about 0.15 GB lower in A/B tests (8956 to 8365 MB locally). • 45c105104b 2024-04-30 `optimize_filters_for_memory=true` became the default after years in production.

### rocksdb-085 — Give big, hot memory users their own allocator and spread them across arenas
Lens: allocation · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Route large cache buffers through a dedicated allocator: per-thread caches cut lock contention, several arenas chosen by a cheap thread-local random pick spread the load, and special page flags keep the memory out of core dumps.
Why it is faster: A general allocator arena is a lock shared by every thread. Per-thread caches serve most frees and reallocations without it, and N arenas divide the remaining contention by N. Choosing the arena with a thread-local PRNG and a multiply-shift range reduction avoids both division and a shared round-robin counter bouncing between cores. Marking cache pages "don't dump" keeps core files small. Costs: more RSS from per-thread and per-arena slack.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: run services with jemalloc or mimalloc and several arenas; separate a large in-process cache into its own arena. • Native / systems: dedicated allocators for large buffers in game engines; per-thread pools in native addons. • Embedded: multiple free lists in an RTOS heap, chosen per task.
Watch out: more arenas and caches mean more fragmentation and RSS, so measure memory alongside latency. Allocator tuning does not carry over between allocators.
Seen in RocksDB: 682db81385 2017-10-27 Cache-line-aligned allocation via jemalloc. • 1cf5deb8fd 2018-10-02 A pluggable allocator for cache memory, threaded through block reads and compression (renamed `MemoryAllocator` in f560c8f5c8). • 5f5fddabc7 2018-10-26 Port of folly's `JemallocNodumpAllocator`; 327097c952 thread-local tcache "to reduce mutex contention inside Jemalloc arena"; cf1df5d3cb limits tcache memory. • 925d8252e5 2023-05-01 `num_arenas`: the arena is chosen with a `thread_local` random number and `FastRange32`. In a MyRocks test with 8 arenas, query latency fell 73% (37.1 to 10.2 s) for +3% RSS.

### rocksdb-086 — Reserve address space up front and let the OS commit pages lazily
Lens: allocation · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Map a large anonymous region once. The OS supplies zero-filled physical pages only when touched, so an array can "grow" in place with no copy and a fixed address.
Why it is faster: Realloc-style growth copies everything and moves the address, which breaks pointers and lock-free readers. Reserved virtual memory costs nothing until touched, so a table can grow one slot at a time to an exact size with low latency and no rehash pause. Zero-filled pages also remove explicit initialization. It needs a 64-bit address space and an MMU, and committed pages are not easily returned.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `mmap(PROT_NONE)` or `VirtualAlloc(MEM_RESERVE)` for growable heaps, JIT code buffers and lock-free hash tables. • Data / databases: buffer-pool hash tables that must never stall on a full rehash. • Embedded: on MMU-equipped devices, growable tables without realloc copies.
Watch out: overcommit settings and container memory limits may count reservations or kill on touch. Pages once touched stay resident unless explicitly released.
Seen in RocksDB: 8367f0d2d7 2022-10-17 Refactored anonymous mmap helpers (with RAII and huge-page cleanup) in preparation for a dynamically growing cache table. • cdb11f5ce6 2023-08-07 A typed `MemMapping` helper. • fe3405e80f 2023-09-01 AutoHyperClockCache grows its table by linear hashing over an anonymous mapping, "with low latency and to a precise size"; 16ae3548a2 adds an atomic "next grow home" so concurrent growth is simpler. • 9abd6e7f6c 2026-11-16 `sst_dump recompress` packs records into one `MemMapping::AllocateLazyZeroed` region sized from table properties.

### rocksdb-087 — Don't free memory the OS is about to reclaim
Lens: allocation · Source: RocksDB
Principle: At process exit, skip walking and freeing large caches; just release ownership and let the OS reclaim everything.
Why it is faster: Freeing millions of cache entries one by one at shutdown touches every page and calls free millions of times, for no benefit, because exit releases the whole address space at once. Skipping it shortens shutdown and restarts. Leak checkers will report the memory, so make it an explicit opt-in.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: compilers that skip destructors at exit (clang `-disable-free`); CLI tools that call `_exit` after flushing output. • Data / databases: batch jobs that drop arenas at the end instead of freeing object by object.
Watch out: objects with external side effects (files, locks, network state) still need proper cleanup. It hides real leaks from leak detectors unless the skip is conditional.
Seen in RocksDB: b13bdfa500 2014-01-24 `Cache::DisownData`: shutdown 7.2 to 6.3 s with about 7 GB of cache.

### rocksdb-088 — Compute derived values when their inputs change, not on every read
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: If a value depends only on state that changes rarely, compute it at change time (construction, version install, insert) and store it. The hot path reads a field instead of recomputing.
Why it is faster: Reads usually outnumber state changes by orders of magnitude. Moving the computation to the change point divides its cost by that ratio. It can also remove a cache miss (reading a value from the far end of a buffer) or a costly call under a lock. Running aggregates replace repeated recomputation. The cost is keeping the cached value in sync on every mutation.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: evaluate feature flags or permission bits once per config version; store parsed field offsets alongside records. • Native / systems: cache string length and hash in immutable strings; store child counts in tree nodes. • Embedded: precompute derived limits when configuration is written, not in the control loop. • Data / databases: maintain running sizes and counts in builders instead of summing on demand.
Watch out: every mutation path must update the cached value, or reads become silently wrong. If writes dominate, eager recomputation costs more than it saves.
Seen in RocksDB: fbbf0d1456 2014-01-14 Cache "L0 file count is at the slowdown trigger" when a version is installed; a3594867ba caches four write-stall conditions per column family; b6fd7811eb read-only DBs record at open whether memtables are empty and skip them. • f38540b12a 2016-04-01 Write-batch index entries store the key's offset and length at creation, so comparators stop re-parsing records: transaction Put of 1M keys 3.68 to 2.73 s. • efd013d6d8 2016-06-13 `BlockBuilder` keeps a running size estimate (called 2-3 times per key), and the flush policy caches its size-deviation threshold. • 26da3676d9 2018-05-18 `Block` stores its restart count at construction instead of re-reading it from the block tail, which "sometimes cause[d] a CPU cache miss". • 37ec9d0c12 2022-01-27 Precompute a prefix extractor's ID string at construction (`AsString` about 10x faster). • c78a87cd71 2022-05-24 LRU handles store total charge (charge plus metadata) at insert, so code inside the shard mutex no longer calls `malloc_usable_size()`. • 8b74cea7fe 2022-05-03 Block iterators store a comparator object instead of constructing one per comparison: DBGet microbenchmark -4.8% mean, -7.1% median. • c3f6e4d625 2026-07-08 `BlockIter` stores its keys-end offset at init instead of evaluating a ternary in every `Next()`.

### rocksdb-089 — Build expensive derived structures once per immutable object
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When every read needs the same transformation of data that doesn't change (a sorted list, a fragmented interval list, a resolved metadata location, a hash index), build it once when the object is opened or sealed, store it with the object, and share it.
Why it is faster: An O(T log T) transformation repeated per read makes every read pay for all of history. Doing it once at open, or once when a memtable becomes immutable, moves the cost off the read path entirely. Persisting the structure in the file removes even the open-time cost. For mutable objects, build lazily on first read and invalidate on write. Here the gains reach orders of magnitude.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: compile regexes, templates and router tries once at startup; memoize compiled ACL rule sets and invalidate on change. • Data / databases: cache Parquet footers and row-group offsets per file; materialized views refreshed on write. • Native / systems: build lookup tables when an asset is loaded rather than per frame.
Watch out: structures built at open add memory per open object and lengthen open time. For mutable sources, invalidation must be correct under concurrency.
Seen in RocksDB: bc5dd19b14 2013-12-09 Presort L0 files when a version is built, not on each Get. • 0b3d03d026 2014-05-15 Persist the block hash index so it isn't rebuilt at open (CPU and flash spikes); 9d70cce047 the same for PlainTable index and bloom. • 76f286cc82 2015-09-02 Resolve the filter block's location once at table open, so a filter cache miss needs 1 I/O instead of 2. • 7528130e38 2018-10-25 Cache the fragmented range-tombstone list at table open: readrandom with range tombstones 89,707 to 1,017,076 ops/s. • 9d77bf8f7b 2022-08-05 Fragment an immutable memtable's range tombstones once, outside the mutex, before it becomes immutable: readrandom with 1000 tombstones 320.33 to 5.21 µs/op. f291eefb02 handles mutable memtables by rebuilding lazily on read under double-checked locking, invalidated by DeleteRange: readwhilewriting 48.28 to 1.52 µs/op.

### rocksdb-090 — Cache the ready-to-use form, not just the raw bytes
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Store decoded or "digested" objects (a parsed filter reader, a prepared compression dictionary, decoded entries) in the cache entry itself, as self-contained data with no back-pointers, so a hit needs no further parsing.
Why it is faster: If the cache holds only raw bytes, every hit pays to parse them again, which can double the cost of a hit. Caching the parsed form along with its bytes removes that. Keeping entries pure data (no pointers into other objects) keeps them safe to share and evict. Within one iterator, remembering decoded entries saves re-decoding when the access pattern revisits them (reverse iteration over delta-encoded data). The cost is more memory per entry.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: cache parsed JSON/AST or compiled templates keyed by content hash; prepared-statement caches. • Native / systems: cache decoded textures or glyphs, not compressed files. • Data / databases: keep decoded dictionary pages next to the raw page in a buffer pool.
Watch out: parsed forms are often much larger than raw bytes, so charge their true size. Entries that point into other objects make eviction unsafe.
Seen in RocksDB: 812dbfb483 2016-06-14 Reverse iteration over a prefix-compressed block saves the entries decoded on the first forward pass from the restart point, so consecutive `Prev()` calls are O(1): readreverse about 2.42M to 3.0M ops/s. • 6c40806e51 2018-08-23 Digest the ZSTD dictionary once per SST (later reverted in 2c14662213); 01013ae766 digests it once per SST write. • 8ec3e72551 2019-01-23 Cache the compression dictionary including its ZSTD digest, removing a copy; with 092f417037. • 1e35584251 2019-05-30 Moved index/filter readers out of the block cache so it holds only data, fixing dangling-pointer hacks (with 3bde41b5a3). That forced re-parsing on every access, so 29ccf2075c stores the parsed filter-bits reader alongside the block contents as one pure-data object: single filter query 83.2 back to 42.6 ns.

### rocksdb-091 — Look it up once and carry the result
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When several steps need the same cached object or map entry, do one lookup and pass the handle, iterator or result along instead of looking it up again by key.
Why it is faster: Each lookup costs hashing, probing, a lock or atomic refcount, and possibly a miss. Two consumers of the same object, a find-then-at, or a two-phase async operation that probes in both phases all pay twice for one answer. Keeping the first result is free. The held handle must stay valid for as long as it is used.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: pass an already-fetched entity down instead of re-querying; one `Map.get` instead of `has` then `get`; carry the resolved auth context through middleware. • Native / systems: Rust `entry()`, Java `computeIfAbsent`, C++ `try_emplace`. • Data / databases: reuse the page latch from the search phase in the update phase.
Watch out: carrying a handle extends the time it is pinned. Iterators into containers are invalidated by inserts, so reuse them only when no mutation happens in between.
Seen in RocksDB: 734e4acafb 2016-11-21 The range-deletion iterator comes from the same table-cache handle as the point lookup, removing an extra lookup that cost about 10% in a read-heavy benchmark. • 3b35134e4b 2017-01-26 The range-deletion meta-block iterator is built from a pinned cache handle using `Cache::Ref()` (added in fe395fb63d) with no cache lookup. • e9c31ab159 2018-02-20 The lock manager reuses the iterator from `find(key)` instead of calling `at(key)`. • 19e8c9b64f 2019-09-05 `try_emplace` so the key is hashed once; da3b2840cb removes repeated lookups in associative containers; 0b8b17a9d1 find-then-insert becomes one `insert`. • 5b5b011cdd 2023-09-18 With async I/O, Seek does the block-cache lookup only in the first phase and carries the result to the second.

### rocksdb-092 — Shard a locked cache by hash, and size the shard count to cores and capacity
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Split one mutex-protected cache into 2^k independent shards picked by hash bits, so concurrent lookups mostly hit different locks. Scale the shard count with core count and capacity, keeping shards large enough to evict well.
Why it is faster: One lock around an LRU list serializes every hit. With N shards, contention falls roughly N-fold. There are two limits: each shard evicts independently, so many tiny shards evict unevenly and waste capacity, and a default sized for 2011 machines is far too few shards for 100+ threads. A well-distributed hash also matters, because collisions concentrate load on a few shards.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: sharded in-process caches in Go (bigcache-style); `ConcurrentHashMap` concurrency level. • Native / systems: lock striping for any shared map; per-core slab caches. • Data / databases: partitioned buffer-pool hash tables and LRU lists.
Watch out: hot keys still land on one shard. Very small shards hurt hit rate, so tie shard count to capacity, not only to cores.
Seen in RocksDB: e3584f9c28 2011-08-22 Block and table caches sharded 16 ways, shard chosen by the top hash bits. • a2a0e358cb 2012-05-16 Configurable shard count; e5fe80e4e3 caps it at 2^20. • 1518b733eb 2016-04-07 Default cache shard bits raised to 6 for less lock contention. • 2d75cd40d3 2017-01-27 `GetDefaultCacheShardBits`: each shard at least 512 KB, at most 6 bits. • 0bb555630f 2019-04-08 The shard hash moved to a faster, better-mixing non-persistent hash; the old one collided on similar inputs and caused "extra lock contention for reads from the same file". • 3c17930ede 2023-04-04 Default block cache 8 MB to 32 MB, raising the default shard count from 16 to 64, citing mutex contention on machines with more than 100 threads.

### rocksdb-093 — Replace the LRU list with CLOCK and pack each slot's state into one atomic word
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Instead of moving an entry to the head of a list on every hit (which needs a lock), bump a small counter and let an eviction pointer sweep. Pack reference count, recency counter and slot state into one machine word, so lookup and release are each a single atomic add.
Why it is faster: Strict LRU writes shared list pointers on every hit, so even reads serialize. CLOCK makes hits nearly free, and the cost moves to the eviction sweep. With refcount and clock state in one word, the clock update folds into the refcount change. Allowing rare inconsistency (occasional duplicate entries) lets lookups, inserts and releases become essentially wait-free. With per-op cost low, fewer, larger shards then evict better. Every atomic access should state its memory order, because an accidental seq_cst costs a full fence.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: CLOCK or S3-FIFO caches in CDNs and in-process caches, avoiding list churn and GC pressure from LRU nodes. • Native / systems: pack version, refcount and flags into one 64-bit atomic in concurrent maps and object pools. • Data / databases: clock-sweep buffer pools (as in Postgres). • Embedded: GPU or MCU tables where only single-word atomics are cheap.
Watch out: lock-free state machines are hard to get right; RocksDB took several rounds. CLOCK approximates recency, so some workloads see slightly lower hit rates than true LRU.
Seen in RocksDB: 4cc37f59e5 2016-08-19 First CLOCK cache: in-cache bit, usage bit and refcount packed in one atomic, a concurrent hash map for lookup, and recycled handles. • efdb428edc 2022-07-15 Lock-free Lookup/Release; 6a160e1fec fully lock-free ClockCache; 9d7de6517c production-quality pass. • c277aeb42c 2022-07-06 New entries start at medium or high clock priority depending on cache priority, and fewer shards by default because larger eviction pools beat the extra atomic contention. • 5724348689 2022-09-16 Revamp: the word holds acquire and release counters (refcount = acquires - releases, countdown = min(3, acquires)) plus state bits. Lookup went from about 4 atomic updates to 1, Release from a read plus update to 1 update, and spins and yields were removed. Production-ready in 8c0f5b1fcf. • fe3405e80f 2023-09-01 AutoHyperClockCache: "essentially wait-free", about 1 yield per second across 16 threads, no regression on the fixed-size version (847k vs 851k ops/s). • 65cde19f40 2023-11-08 An atomic wrapper that forces every access to name its memory order, fixing places that silently used seq_cst; 387cb4aae7 adds a BitFields atomic API for this kind of low-lock code. • 6127a42f98 2025-09-18 The default block cache became AutoHyperClockCache.

### rocksdb-094 — Use a preallocated open-addressing table with bounded probing
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Size the cache's hash table from the expected entry size and store every handle inline in it. Probe linearly for a few slots, then jump with a golden-ratio stride, and try an optimistic fast pass before the careful one.
Why it is faster: Chained hashing allocates a node per insert and chases pointers on every lookup. Inline slots need no allocation and give good locality, and fixed slots are what allows lock-free updates. Linear probing is cache-friendly but collapses under skewed density, so bounding it and then jumping keeps both locality and spread. A naive first pass that almost always succeeds keeps the common case near the cost of an unsynchronized lookup. The table must be sized correctly: too sparse wastes TLB reach, too full wastes memory.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: open-addressing maps in game engines and compilers; packet flow tables in DPDK or eBPF. • Data / databases: hash-join tables; buffer-pool page tables. • Embedded: fixed-capacity caches on microcontrollers with no heap.
Watch out: deletion in open addressing needs tombstones or backward shifting. Probing behavior is subtle, so benchmark both steady state and growth.
Seen in RocksDB: f105e1a501 2022-06-13 Per-shard table preallocated from an estimated entry size; 3afed7408c replaced chained hash tables with open addressing and all handles preallocated. • 3182beeffc 2022-11-21 Warnings when `estimated_entry_charge` is off, because a sparse table costs TLB misses and a full one under-uses memory. • fe3405e80f 2023-09-01 AutoHCC Lookup does "a 'naive' pass that will almost always find entries quickly", falling back to the careful algorithm only when not found. • f6cb763409 2023-09-22 Growth had collapsed into linear search; a likely-empty-slot hint, linear probing bounded at 4, then a golden-ratio double hash (plus 1 per cycle to cover every slot) took growth-phase throughput from 25,832 to 748,400 ops/s. Full double hashing was rejected because losing locality hurt steady state. • 1f7a286413 2026-07-01 Fixed chain indexing in the naive Lookup fast path, which had caused full fallbacks.

### rocksdb-095 — Make the cache scan-resistant and give metadata priority
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Insert new entries into a low-priority segment and promote them only on a second hit, so one-pass scans can't flush the working set. Insert metadata that every read needs (index, filter) at high priority.
Why it is faster: A long scan touches each block once. Under plain LRU it evicts everything else, and the hot set must be re-read afterwards. Midpoint insertion confines scan blocks to the low-priority segment. Index and filter blocks are needed for every lookup into their file, so losing one costs far more than losing a data block. Reserving a high-priority share keeps them resident.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: SLRU or young/old segments in an in-process cache; protect small hot objects from large one-off downloads in a CDN. • Data / databases: InnoDB-style midpoint LRU; keep index pages hotter than data pages. • Native / systems: active/inactive page lists, as in the OS page cache.
Watch out: a high-priority pool that is too large starves data blocks; too small, and metadata gets evicted anyway. Workloads with genuine one-touch reuse gain nothing.
Seen in RocksDB: 72f8cc703c 2016-08-19 LRU mid-point insertion with a `high_pri_pool_ratio`; 4a16c32ece index and filter blocks inserted at high priority. • bc7e8d472e 2018-05-24 Entries that have been hit, or are high priority, go to the high-priority pool; new blocks enter at the midpoint; a readwhilescanning benchmark added to evaluate it. • 15fd3be07b 2019-06-27 Midpoint insertion and high-priority index/filter caching on by default. • c277aeb42c 2022-07-06 The CLOCK cache keeps the idea: new entries start at medium or high clock priority depending on cache priority.

### rocksdb-096 — Admit to expensive tiers only on the second touch
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: On first sight, record only a cheap placeholder. Do the expensive insert (compressing into a secondary tier, writing to flash) when the item is seen again, or when it is evicted with evidence that it was used.
Why it is faster: Most items seen once are never seen again. Paying compression, memory and flash writes for them is waste, and they push out items that would have been reused. A zero-size dummy is nearly free and lets the second event trigger the real work. Avoiding duplicate copies of the same block across tiers frees capacity for distinct data.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: CDN "cache on second hit"; a TinyLFU doorkeeper (Caffeine). • Native / systems: SSD cache admission that saves flash endurance. • Data / databases: promote pages to a faster tier only after repeated access.
Watch out: items that really are reused pay one extra miss before admission. Placeholders still use metadata slots and need their own eviction.
Seen in RocksDB: f241d082b6 2022-04-11 Prevent the same block being cached in both primary and secondary tiers: hit rate 96.2% to 99.9% with a 320 MB primary plus 400 MB secondary. • d490bfcdb6 2022-09-07 Compressed secondary cache: on first lookup a dummy goes into the primary; on first eviction only a 0-size dummy goes to the secondary; the real insert happens on the second event. • a1743e85be 2023-08-18 An admission policy that also admits blocks evicted with their hit bit set; 6cc7ad15b6 adds an allow-all policy for comparison.

### rocksdb-097 — Keep bulk and background reads out of the shared cache
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Background compaction, bulk ingestion, verification and large scans should not insert what they read into the cache that foreground requests rely on. Give them no-fill reads or their own scan-lifetime buffers.
Why it is faster: Read-once data has no value in a cache, but inserting it evicts the hot working set and causes misses for latency-sensitive readers afterwards. Skipping the insert also saves its CPU and locking. Caller-owned buffers give a scan memory that is released when the scan ends, without touching shared structures.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: admin exports and batch jobs that bypass the shared Redis/memcached layer; CDN prefetch jobs marked no-store. • Data / databases: `SQL_NO_CACHE` and bulk-load paths; ring buffers for sequential scans (Postgres). • Native / systems: backups using `posix_fadvise(DONTNEED)` or O_DIRECT.
Watch out: if the "background" data is about to become hot (freshly written output that readers will hit), bypassing the cache causes a burst of misses later.
Seen in RocksDB: f23fed19a1 2018-06-15 Compaction output verification deferred until all subcompactions finish, so it doesn't fill the table cache with entries nobody uses yet. • cc8ded6152 2022-08-01 Blobs read during compaction are no longer cached. • 787730c859 2024-10-16 `IngestExternalFileOptions::fill_cache=false` skips loading index, filter and data blocks during ingestion. • 82085868e2 2026-06-08 `read_scoped_block_buffer_provider`: large scans read or decompress into caller-owned, scan-lifetime memory and bypass the data-block cache, while index and filter caching stays.

### rocksdb-098 — Warm the cache with data you just wrote
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Freshly written data is often read soon and is already in memory, so insert it into the cache while writing instead of reading it back from storage later.
Why it is faster: Without warming, the first read of new data is a guaranteed miss and a storage read, which is expensive under direct I/O or on remote and tiered storage. Inserting at write time costs one cache insert of bytes already in hand. Output from background rewrites is less likely to be hot than fresh writes, so it goes in at a lower priority.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: write-through caching of a newly created object right after the DB write; pre-warm a CDN after deploy. • Data / databases: keep freshly written pages in the buffer pool instead of evicting them on write. • Native / systems: keep newly generated assets in an in-memory cache.
Watch out: write-heavy workloads whose data is rarely read will flush the useful cache with cold writes. Use lower priority or make it opt-in per data class.
Seen in RocksDB: 5ba1b6e549 2021-06-17 `prepopulate_block_cache` warms data blocks during flush; 8b2f60b668 adds index, filter and dictionary blocks, saving a read-back under direct I/O. • ec4ebeff30 2022-07-17 The same for the blob cache. • 1ed5052486 2026-03-10 `kFlushAndCompaction` also warms with compaction output, at a lower priority than flush output.

### rocksdb-099 — Put metadata and write buffers under the same memory budget as cached data
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Charge index and filter blocks, write buffers and transient read buffers to one shared cache budget, shared across instances, so total memory is bounded and the least valuable bytes are evicted, whatever their kind.
Why it is faster: Separate budgets each need headroom, and none can borrow idle memory from another, so total memory is either over-provisioned or unbounded. A single budget lets cold metadata compete with data on equal terms. Starting flushes before the hard cap is reached (at 7/8) avoids stalls. Charging at coarse granularity (arena blocks) keeps accounting overhead low. Huge, never-evictable items can thrash a shared cache.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: one memory budget for decoded images and metadata in a mobile app; shared cache across tenants on a node. • Data / databases: a unified memory manager for caches and query buffers (as in Spark). • Native / systems: charge transient I/O buffers against the same quota as the cache.
Watch out: metadata evicted from a shared cache must be re-read, adding I/O to lookups. Pin or prioritize the critical pieces. Accounting on every update can become a contention point.
Seen in RocksDB: d5503208cf 2012-07-11 One block cache shared across all DBs in a server. • 88ba331c1a 2013-11-12 Index and filter blocks go into the block cache (no regression measured); 2b205b35d8 temporarily disabled this for huge filters that thrashed the cache. • a14b7873ee 2014-12-02 One write-buffer budget shared across column families. • 95b0e89b5d 2017-06-02 `WriteBufferManager` tracks memory at arena-block granularity, triggers flush at 7/8 of the limit, and can charge memtable memory to the block cache; af1746751f avoids more flushes when half the data is already flushing. • 3fe0937180 2018-01-29 Blocks read with `fill_cache=false` are charged to the cache while in use; 13579e8c5a skips cache charging when no limit is set. • beb44ec3eb 2019-04-16 The write-buffer dummy entry shrank from 1 MB to 256 KB so it fits within a 512 KB shard. • 05cdd2d92b 2026-11-12 Readahead buffers charged to the block cache through an atomic fast path that takes the mutex only when the reservation must grow or shrink past a threshold; a mutex-only version lost 90% throughput at 64 threads.

### rocksdb-100 — Charge the memory you really use, overheads included
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Charge each cache entry with the allocator's usable size plus handle and key overhead, not the requested payload size, so the configured limit is the real limit.
Why it is faster: Allocators round up (a 4.5 KB block may occupy 8 KB), and each entry has a handle of tens of bytes. Charging only the payload lets real memory exceed the budget by up to 2x, which leads to swapping or OOM, or forces operators to set a smaller cache "just in case". Honest charging makes the budget trustworthy, so it can be set larger safely.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: byte-weighted caches (Guava `maximumWeight`) using retained size; include key size in Redis maxmemory estimates. • Native / systems: count object headers in off-heap cache budgets. • Embedded: account for allocator block rounding when sizing pools.
Watch out: `malloc_usable_size` must match the allocator that owns the memory, and calling it is not free, so compute it once at insert, outside locks.
Seen in RocksDB: 0a019d74a0 2015-06-26 Block-cache charge via `malloc_usable_size`: a MongoRocks scan with a 10 GB cache used 12 GB before and 10.5 GB after. 3d78eb66bb does the same for arena usage. • 29ffbb8a50 2018-06-29 Charge C++ object overhead. • 638d239507 2019-09-16 `kFullChargeCacheMetadata` charges the handle and key bytes (about 72 bytes, roughly 2% at 4 KB blocks). • 02db03af8d 2020-06-09 Keeps pinned L0 index/filter memory predictable.

### rocksdb-101 — Pin what is expensive to rebuild instead of letting it compete
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Some cached objects cost orders of magnitude more to rebuild than a normal miss, or are needed by every access. Pin them (or have their owner hold them directly) so they never compete in eviction.
Why it is faster: Eviction treats entries as equally costly, but they aren't. Evicting a stats collector whose rebuild scans the whole cache, or a top-level index needed by every lookup, causes huge stalls or a miss on the critical path. Pinning removes both the lookup (and its lock) and the risk. The cost is memory that can never be reclaimed under pressure.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: pin compiled query plans or regexes for hot routes; keep JIT output out of normal eviction. • Data / databases: pin B-tree roots and metadata pages in the buffer pool. • Native / systems: lock critical page-table or routing structures in memory.
Watch out: pinned memory can grow without bound as files or tenants are added, so charge it to a budget and limit what qualifies.
Seen in RocksDB: 9b51987521 2016-04-01 `pin_l0_filter_and_index_blocks_in_cache`: the table reader owns L0 filter and index blocks and skips the block cache and its lock. e70020e4f6 prefetches only L0 index/filter at open. • 80ade9ad83 2018-06-22 `pin_top_level_index_and_filter` keeps the top level of partitioned metadata pinned. • d5a46c40e5 2021-06-14 Pinned the cache-entry stats collector: when evicted, it forced a full cache scan (about 20 s over 100 GB) per column family; background scanning also capped at 0.2% of thread time. 2f93a3b809 fixed a `std::min`/`std::max` bug that defeated the result cache.

### rocksdb-102 — Keep only evictable entries on the eviction list, and work outside the lock
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Entries currently referenced cannot be freed, so keep them off the LRU list. Prepare new entries before taking the shard lock, and keep hash chains short.
Why it is faster: If in-use entries sit on the eviction list, eviction wastes time skipping them or, worse, drops them from the cache while they are still in use, so the next reader loads and decompresses the same block again. Separating "in cache" from "referenced" fixes both. Initializing entries before locking and keeping a low load factor shorten every critical section.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: image caches that never evict images currently on screen. • Data / databases: buffer pools with pin counts, where only unpinned pages are eviction candidates. • Native / systems: GPU texture caches; build cache entries before entering the lock.
Watch out: if most entries are pinned, the cache can exceed its capacity. Decide whether inserts fail, overshoot, or wait.
Seen in RocksDB: f8509653ba 2013-10-09 When full, free entries with refcount 1 (cache-only) before in-use ones. • e8d40c31b3 2013-12-11 Resize the hash table at 1.5 times the element count, use a vector instead of a list, and initialize entries before taking the lock (readrandom with 16 threads about 787k to 798k). • ee95cae9a4 2014-10-21 The LRU list holds only refcount-0 entries, with an "in cache" flag separate from the refcount.

### rocksdb-103 — Cap eviction effort in pathological states
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When most entries are pinned, an eviction sweep can scan the whole cache on every insert. Stop after a fixed amount of fruitless work and accept a small capacity overshoot.
Why it is faster: An unbounded search for a victim turns each insert into O(cache size) work precisely when the system is already under pressure. A cap makes worst-case CPU constant at the cost of a few percent more memory. The cap is a tunable trade-off between memory and CPU.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: a JS memo cache that stops evicting after N tries instead of blocking the event loop. • Native / systems: bounded GC slices; bounded free-list searches in allocators. • Data / databases: buffer-pool clock sweeps that give up and overshoot rather than spin.
Watch out: overshoot must itself be bounded, or a pinned-heavy workload grows memory without limit.
Seen in RocksDB: 88bc91f3cc 2023-12-14 `eviction_effort_cap` (default 30): with a pinned ratio of 1.7, AutoHCC went from 141,011 ops/s (more than 10x slower than LRU) to 1,981,092 ops/s, with RSS 1.08 to 1.13 GB; cap=1 gave 3.2M ops/s at about 2x memory.

### rocksdb-104 — Skip the cache layer for objects that are always resident
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: If a class of objects will be kept open anyway (unlimited open files), store direct pointers to them and skip the cache lookup, hash and lock on every access. Preload them at open.
Why it is faster: A cache lookup costs a hash, a shard lock and refcount traffic, which is pure overhead when nothing is ever evicted. A direct pointer removes all of it. Preloading at open also removes contention when many first accesses race. It gives up the memory bound the cache provided.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: pin hot DB connections or prepared statements; eagerly load singleton modules instead of registry lookups. • Embedded: static tables referenced directly instead of through a lookup service. • Data / databases: keep handles for all open segments in an array indexed by segment id.
Watch out: memory and file descriptors now grow with the data set. This is safe only when the "always open" assumption really holds.
Seen in RocksDB: aa0ef6602d 2014-01-06 With `max_open_files = -1`, table readers are stored in file metadata and the table cache is bypassed; 6b2e7a2a01 extends it to non-L0 iteration. • 5fbf2ef42d 2014-02-12 Preload table handles at recovery to avoid table-cache contention. • f0dda35d7d 2018-12-28 Preload up to 16 table readers at open even when the open-file limit is set.

### rocksdb-105 — Remember misses and recent results, not only hits
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Cache the fact that something is absent, or the result of an expensive read, and keep a tiny victim cache of the most recent buffers, so repeated calls don't redo the same I/O.
Why it is faster: Without negative caching, every request for a missing item repeats the full failed search or read. Access patterns like binary search over a file revisit nearby offsets, so keeping the last couple of buffers removes duplicate syscalls. These caches are tiny and cheap. Their risk is staleness when the absent item appears later.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: DNS negative caching; caching 404s at a CDN; memoize a failed config lookup. • Native / systems: small victim caches in file readers; last-buffer reuse in HTTP range clients. • Embedded: a last-sector buffer in flash or FAT drivers.
Watch out: negative entries go stale when the item is created later, so tie them to an immutable object or give them a TTL.
Seen in RocksDB: dd9eb7a7d5 2014-04-29 Cache the first record read from each WAL file for `GetUpdatesSince`. • 570ba5aca8 2014-08-15 Don't retry reading an absent property block; 0fd985f427 stops reloading a missing filter on every Get. • 35c8c814e8 2014-09-26 The forward iterator caches immutable status: readseq 0.562 to 0.433 µs/op. • 9d0b8f19d9 2015-11-17 PlainTable reuses the index-probe buffer for the data read, so index and data don't double-read (checked with strace); 9a8e3f73ed keeps the two most recent read buffers.

### rocksdb-106 — Load each missing entry once, even under concurrent misses
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: On a miss, take a per-key striped lock and look up again before loading, so concurrent misses for the same key wait for one loader instead of all doing the same work.
Why it is faster: When a popular entry is missing, many threads miss at once and each does the expensive load: duplicate I/O, duplicate memory, and broken open-file limits. Double-checked loading under a striped lock collapses them into one. Striping keeps unrelated keys from waiting on each other. Opening a file once into the cache, rather than first reading a subset separately, applies the same idea.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Go `singleflight`; memoizing the in-flight Promise per key in Node; request coalescing at a CDN. • Data / databases: per-page latches in a buffer pool so a page is read once. • Native / systems: lazy-loading of shared libraries or assets with per-item locks.
Watch out: if the loader fails or hangs, every waiter is affected. Propagate errors to waiters and avoid holding the stripe lock across unrelated work.
Seen in RocksDB: 6ee66cf8f0 2020-04-21 `TableCache::FindTable` takes one of 128 striped mutexes keyed by file, re-runs the cache lookup, and only then opens the file. • 249eff0f30 2020-04-27 Added "add.redundant" statistics to measure the same race in the block cache. • 6e74afd550 2026-11-17 With unlimited open files, table properties are obtained by opening the table through the table cache (where the loader mutex coalesces openers) instead of a separate properties read.

### rocksdb-107 — Cache final results, and normalize keys so equivalent requests share an entry
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: For point lookups, cache the final key-to-value result above the block layer. When a request parameter cannot change the answer, drop it from the cache key so more requests hit.
Why it is faster: A block-cache hit still pays for the index lookup, block decode and in-block search. A result cache skips all of that, and holds only the bytes actually requested. Normalizing keys raises hit rates for free: a read at a snapshot newer than everything in a file sees the same data as a read with no snapshot, so both can share one entry.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: object caches in front of page caches (memcached in front of MySQL); HTTP caches that ignore irrelevant query params and normalize Vary headers. • Data / databases: result caches keyed by normalized query text and data version. • Native / systems: DNS answer caches in front of zone caches.
Watch out: normalizing a key wrongly serves the wrong answer, so prove the dropped parameter cannot affect the result. Result caches duplicate data that may also be in lower caches.
Seen in RocksDB: 782a1590f9 2015-06-23 Table-level `row_cache` for Get, aware of snapshots and merges. • 0655b58582 2017-07-17 Row-cache hits return pinned slices instead of copies. • 66b5613d0c 2019-07-22 The row cache uses the no-snapshot key when the read's snapshot is newer than the file's largest sequence number and no read callback is registered. • e10570331d 2019-08-28 Row-cache support for batched MultiGet.

### rocksdb-108 — Add a compressed in-memory tier between RAM and storage
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Keep a second cache tier of compressed blocks. A miss in the uncompressed tier then often costs a decompression from RAM instead of an I/O. Reuse the on-disk compressed bytes so nothing is recompressed, and issue lookups to slower tiers in parallel.
Why it is faster: Compressed blocks hold two to four times more data per byte of RAM, and decompressing from memory takes microseconds, versus hundreds of microseconds or more for an I/O. Inserting the already-compressed block from disk avoids paying compression CPU on insert. Latency-critical blocks (filters) stay uncompressed. Issuing all secondary-tier lookups of a batch before waiting overlaps their latency.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: zswap or zram in front of swap. • Web / backend: compressed object caches in mobile apps; a memory tier in front of an SSD cache tier. • Data / databases: compressed-in-memory columnar caches.
Watch out: decompression CPU on every tier-two hit adds up under hot workloads. Without admission control, the same block may sit in both tiers and waste capacity.
Seen in RocksDB: b4ad5e89ae 2013-09-01 `block_cache_compressed`, looked up after the uncompressed cache, with independent LRUs; 87d6eb2f6b drops OS page cache when the compressed cache replaces it; dc55903293 improves it. • 8ea0a2c1bd 2021-06-18 MultiGet issues all secondary-cache lookups, then waits for all (`WaitAll()`), with no regression without a secondary cache. • 64a1f7670f 2023-02-16 `do_not_compress_roles`: filter blocks not compressed by default. • 269478ee46 2023-09-21 A three-tier cache (primary, compressed secondary, NVM) inserts the compressed on-disk block directly, avoiding "the overhead of recompressing the block". 204fcff751 turned secondary-cache logic into a wrapper with stack-allocatable async lookup handles.

### rocksdb-109 — Erase entries whose backing object is gone
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When the source of cached data is deleted, actively remove its entries (in the background, with tunable effort) instead of waiting for them to age out.
Why it is faster: Dead entries can never be hit again but still take capacity until evicted. Caches with long memory, such as counting CLOCK caches, keep them longest. Erasing them frees room for live data and raises the hit rate. Doing it speculatively and off the critical path keeps the cost away from foreground requests.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: purge CDN or object-cache keys when content is deleted; invalidate compiled-template caches when a file is removed. • Native / systems: drop page-cache pages of deleted temp files. • Data / databases: evict buffer-pool pages of dropped tables eagerly.
Watch out: finding all entries of an object can be costly without an index by owner. Keep the effort bounded and off the foreground path.
Seen in RocksDB: b34cef57b7 2024-06-07 `uncache_aggressiveness` erases blocks of obsolete files: block-cache hit rate up to about 1 point higher (AutoHCC 0.336 to 0.345). • 21eb82ebec 2024-06-11 Disabled it at shutdown for safety.

### rocksdb-110 — Use fixed-size keys that are themselves well-mixed hashes
Lens: caching · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Derive a fixed-length (for example 16-byte) cache key from stable identifiers, and make it a reversible hash of the logical key, so the entry needs no separate hash field and no variable-length key compare.
Why it is faster: Variable-length keys need storage, length checks and memcmp. Fixed 16-byte keys compare in two word compares. If the key is already a well-mixed bijective hash, its bits serve directly as the hash, so the stored hash field disappears and the whole handle fits in one cache line. Deriving keys from file identifiers known before the file is read lets keys be computed without I/O.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: key HTTP or object caches by a 128-bit digest instead of the full URL string. • Data / databases: content-addressed blob stores; page caches keyed by (file id, offset) packed into 16 bytes. • Native / systems: fixed-size keys in lock-free tables, where variable-length keys are impractical.
Watch out: a non-bijective (lossy) hash risks collisions that serve wrong data. Identifiers must be unique across restarts and copies of files.
Seen in RocksDB: 0050a73a4f 2021-12-16 Stable, fixed-length 16-byte block-cache keys with no filesystem dependency: readrandom +2.8% (121,924 to 125,385 ops/s). • 415200d792 2022-06-10 An experimental fast LRU cache that assumes 16-byte keys. • 86a1e3e0e7 2022-08-12 Cache keys derived from SST unique IDs, so they are known before the file is read. • 7555243bcf 2022-10-18 The clock-cache handle drops its hash field "by making the fixed-size key itself a hash, using a 128-bit bijective (lossless) hash", keeping the handle within one cache line.

### rocksdb-111 — Let the compiler see the concrete type: devirtualize and monomorphize hot calls
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: On hot paths, replace dynamic dispatch (virtual calls, interfaces, function pointers, runtime type switches) with calls the compiler can resolve statically, so it can inline and specialize them.
Why it is faster: An indirect call costs a load, a possible branch mispredict and, most importantly, an optimization barrier: the callee cannot be inlined, so constant propagation and register allocation stop at the call. Sealing leaf classes (`final`), holding a pointer to the concrete type, templating on the implementation, or putting a cheap non-virtual check in front of the virtual call removes the barrier. This pays off on functions called per key or per step. On a function called once per request it changes nothing and adds code size.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: keep JS hot functions monomorphic (same object shapes, one call target) so V8's inline caches and inliner engage; in Java/Kotlin/C#, mark classes `final`/`sealed` so the JIT can devirtualize. • Native / systems: use `std::sort` with a lambda instead of `qsort` with a function pointer; Rust generics (`impl Trait`) instead of `dyn Trait` in inner loops. • Embedded: replace driver vtables on per-byte paths with compile-time selected functions; keep the table only for configuration-time dispatch. • Data / databases: specialize the comparator or codec per column type once per query instead of dispatching per row.
Watch out: templates multiply code size and compile time, and too many specializations can thrash the instruction cache. `final` only helps when the call site sees the concrete static type. RocksDB applied `final` to `InternalKeyComparator` only in release (NDEBUG) builds, because tests subclass it.
Seen in RocksDB: d20b8cfaa1 2014-08-29 Cuckoo table: hash function pointer replaced with an inlined hash (together with power-of-two buckets), about 50% faster lookups. • 64b6452e0c 2017-09-11 `InternalKeyComparator` marked `final`; `MergingIterator` and heap comparators hold `const InternalKeyComparator*` instead of `Comparator*`. Same day 2dd22e5449 2017-09-11 made `DBIter` final so `ArenaWrappedDBIter::key()/value()` inline; ddfd2525d2 2018-05-09 did the same for `BlockIter`. • b555ed30a4 2018-02-12 A purpose-built `BlockBasedTableIterator`/`LevelIterator` replaced the generic two-level iterator and references the final `BlockIter` directly, saving a virtual call and an allocation per data block. • a661c0d208 2019-02-26 `ReadCallback::IsVisible` became an inline wrapper that settles most calls (`seq==0`, below `min_uncommitted_`, above the snapshot) and calls the virtual `IsVisibleFullCheck` only otherwise. 14b3f683a1 2019-04-02 statically initialized the WriteUnprepared callback (+0.26% seekrandom). d6862b3f51 2019-04-18 made `ReadRangeDelAggregator` final with an inline empty check before `ShouldDeleteImpl`. • dd29ad4223 2020-07-07 `BlockIter` holds concrete `UserComparatorWrapper`/`InternalKeyComparator` types instead of `Comparator*`; 643c863b72 2020-07-20 then moved them to the point of use to shrink the iterator. • 7555243bcf 2022-10-18 Sharded cache templated on the shard type (static polymorphism): HyperClockCache readrandom 614,126 to 645,349 ops/s (~5%), LRU ~0.1%. • 9f7801c5f1 2023-01-11 Block cache access unified on static polymorphism: a template parameter replaced a mix of template and runtime `BlockType` switch, removing branches and casts. • 41e5eb1a4d 2026-03-31 Trie index: `TrieIndexIterator`/`TrieIndexBuilder` marked `final`, and a repeated virtual `IndexValue` result cached in the wrapper. • d3a82f28ce 2026-08-19 Compaction output partitioning compares a cached "next boundary prefix" inline instead of a per-key key copy plus virtual `SstPartitioner` call: instructions -2.6%, compaction wall time -2.4%.

### rocksdb-112 — Inline the tiny hot path, push the cold path out of line
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Make small, frequently called functions inlinable (visible in headers, or force-inlined), and move rare branches into separate non-inlined functions so the inlined fast path stays small.
Why it is faster: For a function of a few instructions (varint decode, key compare, hash mix), call and return plus argument passing can cost as much as the body. Inlining also lets the compiler optimize across the boundary. The catch is size: inlining a large body bloats the caller and the instruction cache, and compilers refuse to inline functions that contain big rare branches. Splitting the rare branch into an out-of-line function keeps the hot part small enough to inline everywhere.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: keep JS hot functions small and move error handling and rare cases into separate functions so V8 stays within its inlining budget. • Native / systems: `__attribute__((cold, noinline))` on error handlers; `#[inline]` on tiny cross-crate Rust accessors; header-only codecs. • Embedded: `always_inline` on CRC or bit-manipulation helpers in ISR paths, with the slow resync path out of line.
Watch out: force-inlining large functions grows code and can slow everything through instruction-cache misses. Measure binary size with throughput. With LTO the compiler can inline across files on its own, which reduces the need for header placement.
Seen in RocksDB: 071fb0d77b 2013-11-19 and bb19b530ca 2014-01-23 Small coding functions (10 lines or fewer) moved inline: fillseq +4.6%, overwrite +9%, binary less than 1% larger. • cd535c2280 2014-01-14 Bloom `MayContainHash` inlined and constants made `const`: 9.127 s to 7.891 s for 2^30 probes; it had been 5-7% of production CPU. • 885b1c682e 2017-09-14 `RangeDelAggregator::ShouldDelete` split into an inline `rep_ == nullptr` check and an out-of-line `ShouldDeleteImpl`; `IterKey::EnlargeBufferIfNeeded` slow path moved to `EnlargeBuffer` (~1% readseq). edcbb36944 2017-09-14 moved `MergingIterator::Next`'s direction switch into a separate function. • d382ae7de6 2018-03-23 `InternalKeyComparator::Compare`/`CompareKeySeq` moved into the header as `inline`: fillseq 274,866 to 295,979 ops/s. • 01cfea6637 2019-04-18 `FindNextUserEntryInternal` inlined into `DBIter::Next`; 7a73adda9c 2019-04-19 added `inline` so `DBIter::Next` inlines into `ArenaWrappedDBIter`. • ba81144d8d 2026-07-29 Hash mix helpers (`XXPH_mult64to128`, `XXPH3_mul128_fold64`, `XXPH3_avalanche`) marked force-inline on the NPHash64 path. • db114777b1 2026-09-14 `Slice::difference_offset` written inline in the public header on purpose, because an out-of-line helper cost measurably more without LTO.

### rocksdb-113 — Pass callbacks as template or generic parameters, not type-erased objects
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When a hot function takes a callback, make the callback's type a compile-time parameter instead of wrapping it in a type-erased container such as `std::function`.
Why it is faster: A type-erased callable hides the target behind an indirect call (no inlining), and when its captures exceed the small-buffer size it heap-allocates on construction. A template parameter lets the compiler inline the lambda body into the loop and keeps the captures on the stack. The cost is more instantiated code.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `template <class F> void ForEach(F&& f)` instead of `std::function`; `impl Fn` instead of `Box<dyn Fn>` in Rust. • Web / backend: hoist closures out of hot JS loops so a new context is not allocated per iteration. • Embedded: capture-free callbacks or function-pointer-plus-context pairs instead of heap-allocated closures.
Watch out: a callable stored for later or run on another thread must own its captures. RocksDB's reverted stack functor shows that removing an allocation can create a use-after-scope bug.

### rocksdb-114 — Test the common case first, before the general machinery
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Put a cheap check for the overwhelmingly common case (one element, same object, no work needed) in front of the general algorithm, and order conditions so the cheapest or most selective runs first.
Why it is faster: General code pays for generality on every call: set inserts, serialization, merges over one input. A pointer-identity or size check costs a compare and a well-predicted branch. It only pays when the fast case really dominates; otherwise it adds a branch to every call.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `===` before deep-equal in memoization; skip the middleware chain when only one handler is registered; collapse `Promise.all([x])`. • Data / databases: skip the merge step when a query touches one shard; skip the sort when input is already one run. • Native / systems: interned strings compared by pointer before content; single-listener fast path in event emitters.
Watch out: the fast path must give exactly the same result as the slow path, including error states. Profile first: a "fast path" for a case that is not common just adds a branch.
Seen in RocksDB: d9bca1e14c 2016-01-11 `IteratorWrapper` destroys the current iterator directly unless it is pinned, rather than always inserting it into a `std::set`: newiterator ~1.0 to ~0.75 us/op. • fc9d4071f0 2022-01-21 Unchanged `prefix_extractor` detected by pointer identity against a saved instance before a full serialized comparison: seekrandom 4,833 to 6,704 ops/s, fixing a regression. 37ec9d0c12 2022-01-27 precomputes the ID at construction so `SliceTransform::AsString` is ~10x faster. • 749b849a34 2022-09-21 With one memtable and no SST files, `DBIter` wraps the memtable iterator directly instead of a merging iterator with one child: seek+30 nexts 2.41M to 3.01M ops/s. • 5139ff5c29 2025-02-21 `MaybeAutoRefresh` evaluates `status().ok()` only after the cheap `sv_number_ != cur_sv_number` test.

### rocksdb-115 — Hoist loop-invariant work out of the inner loop
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Anything that does not change across iterations (objects, derived values, status checks, constant inputs) should be computed once per container, batch or object lifetime, not once per element.
Why it is faster: Compilers hoist simple invariants, but not object construction with side effects, virtual calls or values read through pointers they cannot prove unchanged. Doing it by hand removes constructors, branches and loads from every iteration. Storing a derived value costs a field and requires it to really be invariant.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: create `Intl.Collator`, regexes and formatters once and reuse them in sorts and loops; reuse prepared statements across a batch. • Native / systems: hoist a composite object's `isValid()` check out of the loop and check once after a batch (like `ferror`). • Embedded: precompute loop bounds and header-adjusted lengths in parser state once per frame.
Watch out: a cached derived field goes stale silently if the inputs can change. Hoisting a status check means errors are found later, so make sure that is acceptable.

### rocksdb-116 — Decode once; compare in encoded form without materializing objects
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Parse a search key a single time and pass the decoded form into every comparison, and compare stored records directly in their encoded bytes instead of building temporary objects.
Why it is faster: A binary search or skiplist descent does O(log n) comparisons. If each one re-parses a length prefix or builds an owning key object (with allocation and destructor), the parsing dominates. Decoding once and comparing a parsed struct against a raw slice removes that per-comparison cost. Whether it works depends on the encoding being comparable in place.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Schwartzian transform: precompute sort keys (parsed dates, lowercased strings) once instead of in the comparator. • Data / databases: compare normalized binary index keys with `memcmp`; read FlatBuffers or Cap'n Proto fields in place instead of deserializing. • Native / systems: parse protocol headers once per packet and pass the parsed struct to every stage.
Watch out: two comparison paths (encoded and parsed) must agree exactly on ordering, or searches give wrong answers. Test both against each other.

### rocksdb-117 — One lookup, no hidden copies: container and parameter hygiene
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Hash or search a key once and reuse the result, move heavy values instead of copying them, and pass read-only parameters by reference.
Why it is faster: `find()` followed by `at()` hashes and probes twice. A by-value `std::string` or options struct parameter, or a `for (auto x : v)` loop, silently allocates and copies. Each is small, but they sit in hot loops and under locks. Static analysis finds them in bulk.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `map.get` once instead of `has` then `get`; `computeIfAbsent` in Java; avoid spreading objects inside reducers and loops. • Native / systems: Rust `entry()` API; enable clang-tidy `performance-*` or clippy `needless_pass_by_value` in CI. • Data / databases: pass row batches by reference through operator pipelines.
Watch out: each instance is tiny, so only fix them in bulk with a linter or where a profile shows them. A reference parameter to an object that may be freed during the call is a correctness hazard.

### rocksdb-118 — Remove unpredictable branches and needless control dependencies
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: On hot paths, prefer straight-line code (conditional moves, unconditional updates, branch-free masks) to data-dependent branches, mark the expected direction of the branches that remain, and separate "decide" from "act" so the CPU can overlap them.
Why it is faster: A mispredicted branch costs 10-20 cycles of discarded work. Incrementing a counter unconditionally is cheaper than testing whether it is needed. `std::min` can become a `cmov`. When later work does not depend on a condition, splitting the computation removes a control dependency, so out-of-order execution can run both. Branch hints only change code layout and static prediction; they do not help if the branch is truly random.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: branch-free clamps and selects in codecs; `if (UNLIKELY(c == '\\'))` in parsers; linear search instead of binary search for arrays of a few dozen elements. • Data / databases: vectorized engines compute selection masks separately from the action (predicate, then compact). • Embedded: unconditional counter updates in packet loops; fixed-time branch-free code also helps constant-time cryptography. • Web / backend: in JS hot loops, compute values with arithmetic or lookup tables instead of chains of `if`s on random data.
Watch out: branchless code always does both sides' work, so it loses when the branch is well predicted and one side is expensive. Wrong `LIKELY` hints make things worse. Check generated assembly; compilers may turn your branchless code back into a branch, or vice versa.
Seen in RocksDB: c178da053b 2018-02-16 `WriteBatchInternal::Iterate` and `MemTableInserter` wrap `s.ok()` in `LIKELY` and rare cases in `UNLIKELY`. • 01cfea6637 2019-04-18 `DBIter::Next` always increments `local_stats_.next_count_` instead of branching on `statistics_ != nullptr`, and drops a redundant `Valid()` check. • f059c7d9b9 2019-11-13 The scalar cache-local Bloom probe was slower on "outside" queries because of a "nastily unpredictable" early-exit branch; the AVX2 version tests all probes branch-free (see SIMD probing). • b234a3f569 2021-10-19 `BlockBuilder::AddWithLastKey` takes the previous key from the caller and uses `std::min` instead of `buffer_.empty() ? ... : ...`: fillseq +2.9 to 5.4%. • f63428bcc7 2024-08-14 `MaybeCutAFilterBlock` split into `DecideCutAFilterBlock` and `CutAFilterBlock` "for more instruction-level parallelism by reducing unnecessary control dependencies"; with dedup moved into the bits builders, SST build +0.36% to +1.8%. • ce0fff9f41 2026-02-26 The trie index scans sparse nodes with 16 or fewer labels linearly, which is cheaper than a binary search with mispredicted branches.

### rocksdb-119 — Strength-reduce division and modulo
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Replace integer division and modulo on hot paths with shifts and masks (power-of-two sizes), multiply-shift range reduction (any size), or running sums of a precomputed reciprocal.
Why it is faster: A 64-bit integer divide takes tens of cycles and does not pipeline well; a shift, mask or multiply takes one to three. Power-of-two sizes give `x & (n-1)` but force sizes to double. "Fastrange" `(uint64(h) * n) >> 32` maps a 32-bit hash into [0, n) for any n with one multiply, using the hash's high bits. A per-insert `n / ratio` can become an accumulated `+= 1/ratio`.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: shard or bucket selection in load balancers and consistent-hash rings with multiply-shift; `(Math.random() * n) | 0` is the float analogue. • Embedded: power-of-two ring buffers indexed with a mask; avoid software division on MCUs without a hardware divider. • Native / systems: hash-table bucket index for non-power-of-two tables; running averages updated with a precomputed reciprocal.
Watch out: fastrange uses the high bits of the hash, so the hash must be well mixed there; masking uses the low bits, which weak hashes leave poorly mixed. Accumulated floating-point reciprocals drift, which is fine for estimates but not for exact counts.
Seen in RocksDB: 462796697c 2014-06-02 Blocked Bloom uses cache-line-sized blocks so divides become shifts; d20b8cfaa1 2014-08-29 power-of-two cuckoo buckets turn mod into mask. • a1a546a634 2018-07-30 `FullFilterBitsReader` derived the cache-line size per probe with `/` and `%`; it now stores `log2_cache_line_size_` once and uses shift and mask. • 6d37fdb365 2018-08-20 The data-block hash index's `EstimateSize()`, called on every `Add`, divided `num_keys / util_ratio_`; it now accumulates `estimated_num_buckets_ += bucket_per_key_`. • b55b2f45d0 2019-09-05 "Uses fastrange instead of %" in DynamicBloom; ca7ccbe2ea 2019-10-24 added `fastrange64`; f059c7d9b9 2019-11-13 picks the Bloom cache line with `fastrange32`; 08552b19d3 2020-09-28 generalized it to `FastRange32/64/Generic`. • 925d8252e5 2023-05-01 jemalloc arena selection uses a thread-local PRNG plus `FastRange32` (no division, no shared counter); 17bc27741f 2023-05-24 striped mutex arrays map hash to stripe with `FastRangeGeneric` instead of modulus.

### rocksdb-120 — Use hardware checksum instructions, dispatched once, with independent streams
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Compute checksums with the CPU's dedicated instructions, choose the implementation once at startup with runtime feature detection, and break the instruction's latency chain by checksumming several independent segments in parallel and combining them.
Why it is faster: SSE4.2 and ARMv8 `crc32` instructions process 8 bytes per instruction, many times faster than a table loop. Each has a latency of a few cycles but a throughput of one per cycle, so a single dependent chain leaves the unit idle; three interleaved streams combined with carry-less multiply (`pclmulqdq`/`pmull`) keep it busy. Runtime detection keeps one portable binary, but the dispatch must be resolved once, not on every call, and it must actually succeed.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: use ISA-L or `crc32c` libraries with hardware paths; resolve dispatch with `ifunc` or a static function pointer. • Embedded: use the MCU's CRC peripheral or ARM CRC instructions for flash and packet integrity. • Web / backend: pick checksum libraries for Kafka, gRPC or object storage clients that use hardware CRC on both x86 and ARM servers. • Data / databases: page and WAL checksums are often several percent of read CPU; check which implementation your build actually runs.
Watch out: feature detection can fail silently, leaving a slow fallback in production for years. Log or export which implementation was selected, and test on every target architecture.

### rocksdb-121 — Pick the fastest hash that meets the need; keep in-memory and persisted hashes separate
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Use a modern non-cryptographic hash (xxHash/XXH3 class) wherever integrity or distribution, not security, is the requirement, and put in-memory-only hashing behind one indirection so it can be upgraded without breaking stored data.
Why it is faster: Hash and checksum throughput varies several-fold between functions. A better-mixed hash also distributes keys more evenly across shards, which lowers lock contention. Hashes written to disk are frozen by the format, but hashes used only in memory (cache shards, hash tables) can change freely if they are kept apart.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: xxHash or wyhash for in-process maps and dedup, but a stable, versioned hash for persisted sharding keys. • Native / systems: XXH3 or BLAKE3 for build-system content hashes instead of MD5/SHA-1 where security is not needed. • Data / databases: choose page checksums by measured throughput on your target CPUs, and record the algorithm per file so it can change later.
Watch out: non-cryptographic hashes are vulnerable to deliberate collisions; seed them per process if inputs come from untrusted users. Persisted hashes need a format version.

The trailing compression-type byte is folded in separately to avoid the slower streaming API. 27c9705ac4 2022-10-21 made it the default (microbench +23 to 30%).

### rocksdb-122 — Never compute a checksum twice: combine, hand off, or skip
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Pass checksums along with the data between layers, derive the checksum of concatenated data mathematically instead of re-reading it, and do not compute checksums the caller did not ask for.
Why it is faster: CRC is linear: `crc(A||B)` follows from `crc(A)`, `crc(B)` and `len(B)` in O(log n) work without touching the bytes. When a producer already checksummed a buffer, the writer layer only needs to combine, not re-hash, which also extends end-to-end protection. Internal reads that ignore a caller's "skip verification" flag pay for checks nobody wanted.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: combine per-part CRCs for multipart object uploads; reuse a request body's hash as its ETag. • Native / systems: zlib `crc32_combine` for parallel compression; pass NIC checksum offload results up the stack. • Data / databases: propagate "skip validation" options through every internal read layer, not just the top one.
Watch out: a handed-off checksum must cover exactly the bytes being written; verify the copy, or a corruption between layers goes unnoticed. Skipping verification is a deliberate trade of safety for CPU.
Seen in RocksDB: ecccc63179 2021-06-16 `Crc32cCombine` added. a904c62d28 2021-06-25 `WritableFileWriter::Append` accepts a caller-supplied crc32c for WAL and MANIFEST data and combines it instead of recomputing. • 0df7ed563b 2026-08-31 The block builder hands its raw CRC32C to `WritableFileWriter::Append()` for the filesystem handoff checksum. • b45738622a 2023-04-04 User `ReadOptions` now reach index and filter block reads: with `verify_checksums=false`, block checksum computations fell from 384,353 to 22, and fully cached readrandom rose from 274,848 to 277,904 ops/s.

### rocksdb-123 — Remove hidden atomic operations: borrow instead of sharing ownership, use the weakest memory order
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: On hot paths, pass raw pointers or references to objects whose lifetime is already guaranteed instead of copying reference-counted handles, store owned helpers by value, and give each atomic the weakest memory ordering that is correct.
Why it is faster: Copying a `shared_ptr`/`Arc` is an atomic increment plus a later decrement; under concurrency that cache line bounces between cores. Sequentially consistent atomics add fences that relaxed ones do not need, and implicit conversions on `std::atomic` silently use the strongest order. For pure statistics, a relaxed load plus store is cheaper still than a read-modify-write, at the cost of rare lost updates.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: borrow `&T` instead of cloning `Arc<T>` in Rust; take `const T&` or `T*` rather than `shared_ptr` by value in C++. • Web / backend: metrics counters with relaxed atomics; avoid Swift ARC traffic by passing references to long-lived singletons. • Embedded: on multi-core MCUs, use relaxed atomics for counters and reserve barriers for real publication points.
Watch out: a raw pointer is only safe if something else really keeps the object alive for the whole use. Relaxed ordering is wrong for anything that publishes data to another thread. Reserve it for counters and flags whose readers tolerate staleness.
