### rocksdb-124 — Fuse chatty per-step interface calls into one
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When a layered abstraction makes several virtual calls per step (advance, check validity, fetch key), offer one call that does all of them and returns the result, and cache that result in a thin wrapper.
Why it is faster: In a deep iterator stack, each layer multiplies the number of indirect calls per element. One combined call per layer cuts dispatch by a constant factor and lets the callee return values it already has in registers.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: the JS iterator protocol's `next()` returning `{value, done}` instead of separate `hasNext()`/`next()`. • Data / databases: Volcano-style engines moving to `next(batch)`; cursor APIs returning row plus status in one call. • Native / systems: plugin interfaces with a combined "read and advance" entry point.
Watch out: every implementation must provide the fused call, or a default that falls back to the separate calls quietly brings the cost back, as it did in RocksDB.
Seen in RocksDB: 72c8533f2c 2019-04-23 `DBIter` uses `IteratorWrapper`, whose `Next()` calls `iter_->NextAndGetResult(&key_)` and caches validity and key: about 1% readseq. • 692f6a3138 2020-07-29 `NextAndGetResult` implemented for the memtable iterator and `LevelIterator` to undo a regression where they fell back to separate calls.

### rocksdb-125 — Skip resetting state that was never touched
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Track whether reusable state was actually written, and reset only what was used, so cleanup cost follows actual use instead of capacity.
Why it is faster: Reusing objects avoids allocation, but clearing every field or slot on each reuse turns into its own fixed cost, often proportional to capacity. A dirty flag, or resetting slots just before they are written, removes it on the common path.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: reuse pooled response objects without zeroing their full capacity; skip virtual-DOM work for untouched subtrees. • Embedded: clear only the used prefix of a DMA or frame buffer. • Native / systems: dirty bits on render state in game engines; arenas reset by moving a pointer, not by zeroing.
Watch out: stale data left in untouched slots must never be readable. Bounds have to follow what was written, and security-sensitive buffers still need explicit clearing.

### rocksdb-126 — Use the primitive that does the least work
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When you already know the length or format, call the narrow primitive (`memcpy`, `strtoull`, a compile-time-sized overload) instead of the general one that rediscovers it.
Why it is faster: `strncpy` tests every byte for NUL and zero-pads; `memcpy` is a vectorized block copy. `sscanf` parses its format string and runs `strlen` on every call. A `const std::string&` parameter makes a heap string out of every literal passed to it, while a template on `char[N]` knows the length at compile time.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `std::string_view`/`&str`/`ReadOnlySpan<char>` parameters; `std::from_chars` instead of stream parsing. • Web / backend: hand-rolled integer parsing in log parsers; avoid `String.format` in hot logging paths. • Embedded: `memcpy` with known lengths for header copies; avoid `printf`-family parsing in loops.
Watch out: the narrow primitive drops the safety checks the general one did (NUL termination, input validation). Make sure the known length really is known.
Seen in RocksDB: caa1fd0e0e 2015-01-04 Backup metadata parsing uses `strtoul`/`strtoull` instead of `sscanf`. • 678ba5e41c 2021-10-15 `SyncPoint::Process` takes a non-owning slice, with a `char[N]` template for literals: a load-and-iterate test went from 32 min 17 s to 1 min 14 s, and live heap objects from 5.1M to 105K. • 96e7ef0269 2026-09-21 `Status::CopyState` uses `memcpy` instead of `strncpy` for a known-length message.

### rocksdb-127 — Make thread-local access a plain load
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Access per-thread state through the compiler's native, constant-initialized thread-local storage rather than through library wrappers or thread-locals that need a lazy-init call.
Why it is faster: A generic thread-local wrapper does a lookup and lazy initialization on every access. In C++, a `thread_local` with a non-trivial destructor accessed from another translation unit goes through a compiler-generated init function (often via the PLT) on every use. A `constinit` thread-local pointer, set on first use, reduces each access to a load and a null check. For per-operation counters touched millions of times per second, this is a visible share of CPU.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: per-thread stats, allocator caches and RNGs as `constinit` or trivially constructible `thread_local`s; check the disassembly for `__tls_init` calls. • Web / backend: in JVM services, avoid `ThreadLocal.get()` in the innermost loop; read it once per request into a local. • Embedded: per-core state in RTOSes via a register-based core ID rather than a lookup table call.
Watch out: native TLS does not run destructors the way wrappers can, so per-thread resources must be cleaned up some other way. With coroutines, the thread can change between suspension points.
Seen in RocksDB: 0025a36409 2017-06-26 `perf_context`/`iostats_context` reverted from `ThreadLocalPtr` to `__thread` because of a regression. • ee305ac1c3 2026-10-21 `PERF_COUNTER_ADD` reads a `constinit thread_local PerfContext*`: microbenchmark 2.10 to 0.84 ns per update; TLS-init calls in block.cc.o and memtable.cc.o fell from 40 and 96 to 0. These accessors had been about 0.3-0.6% of fleet CPU.

### rocksdb-128 — Let the build help: link-time optimization, ISA flags and per-function targets
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Turn on whole-program optimization, and make faster instruction sets available to hot functions without giving up a portable binary.
Why it is faster: Without LTO, the compiler cannot inline across translation units, so small functions in .cc files stay calls. Building for a baseline ISA disables SSE4.2/AVX2 paths unless they are enabled per function (`target` attributes) or by explicit flags. Removing unused language features (RTTI) shrinks binaries.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `-flto`/ThinLTO and PGO for services; Rust `lto = "fat"`, `codegen-units = 1`; function multiversioning for x86-64-v3. • Embedded: `-flto` with `-Os` often makes firmware both smaller and faster; build without RTTI and exceptions. • Web / backend: build WASM modules with SIMD enabled and a scalar fallback.
Watch out: forcing an ISA flag globally crashes on older CPUs; prefer per-function targets plus runtime dispatch. LTO lengthens builds and can change which code is inlined, so benchmark both ways.
Seen in RocksDB: ba3c58cab6 2017-09-07 `__attribute__((target("sse4.2")))` on the CRC function, so it is compiled with SSE4.2 without global `-msse4.2`. • 21696ba502 2017-07-28 `dynamic_cast` replaced with explicit virtual accessors so users can build with `-fno-rtti`. • f059c7d9b9 2019-11-13 AVX2 added to the `USE_SSE=1` build; a09c3cf13e 2020-09-03 options to force AVX/AVX2. • c0c33a4854 2020-07-28 `USE_LTO=1` build option. Later benchmark numbers were often quoted with -O3/LTO (for example 6cdb7af9f8 2022-11-28).

### rocksdb-129 — Check what portable intrinsics compile to on each ISA
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: "Portable" spin hints and prefetch builtins map to different instructions with different strengths per architecture; verify and tune them per target.
Why it is faster: On arm64, the `yield` hint barely delays a spinning core, so a spin loop hammers the contended cache line, while `isb` behaves more like x86 `pause`. `__builtin_prefetch(p, 0, 1)` requests low temporal locality, which arm64 maps to a prefetch into a far cache level rather than L1. Code tuned on x86 can behave quite differently on ARM servers.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: audit `cpu_relax()`-style macros and prefetch locality hints in spinlocks and allocators when porting to ARM. • Web / backend: JVM `Thread.onSpinWait()` and Go/.NET runtimes made the same per-arch choice; prefer the runtime's spin primitive over a hand-rolled one. • Embedded: check `WFE`/`WFI`/`YIELD` semantics on the specific Cortex core before using them in spin-waits.
Watch out: the best choice differs between microarchitectures of the same ISA. Benchmark on the actual production hardware.
Seen in RocksDB: 63748c2204 2021-03-26 arm64 spin hint changed from `wfe` to `yield`; 2e5a323dbd 2022-06-15 then to `isb`: about 2x on 64-thread fillrandom on Graviton3. • b550fc0b09 2022-06-14 arm64 `PREFETCH` uses locality 3 (L1): skiplist fillrandom +10 to 15% on Graviton3 and Apple M1.

### rocksdb-130 — Test many candidates per instruction instead of branching per candidate
Lens: simd · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When a query checks several independent positions (filter probe bits, hash-table slots), compute and test them all at once with vector instructions and combine the results, instead of a loop that exits early on the first failure.
Why it is faster: An early-exit loop is fast only if its branch is predictable. For a membership filter, whether a probe fails is random, so mispredictions dominate. Eight 32-bit lanes can derive eight probe positions with one multiply and test all bits with one compare, at the same cost whether the answer is yes or no. It works best when the data for all candidates is in one cache line, so the gathers are cheap register shuffles rather than memory loads.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: client-side dedup or blocklist filters in WASM SIMD; SwissTable-style maps (absl, hashbrown) in services. • Data / databases: split-block Bloom filters in Parquet and query engines; SIMD probe of hash-join buckets. • Native / systems: packet classifiers testing several rule fields per vector compare.
Watch out: needs a scalar fallback and runtime or build-time ISA selection; the results must be bit-identical across paths if the structure is persisted. Hardware gathers are often slower than they look, so measure shuffles against gathers.
Seen in RocksDB: f059c7d9b9 2019-11-13 `FastLocalBloomImpl`: all probes of a key stay in one 64-byte line. AVX2 `_mm256_mullo_epi32` with golden-ratio powers makes 8 probe hashes, `_mm256_permutevar8x32_epi32` plus blend fetch the words (a gather was "a little slow"), and `_mm256_testc_si256` tests all bits. Hot-data query time 63 to 26 ns, cold 188 to 150 ns. The scalar version had suffered from a "nastily unpredictable branch" on negative queries. • efd035164b 2022-04-13 With folly enabled, the largest CPU users of `std::unordered_map` switch to F14FastMap, whose buckets are probed with SIMD tag matching, behind a compile-time `UnorderedMap` alias.

### rocksdb-131 — Work a machine word at a time (SWAR, popcount, parity)
Lens: simd · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Treat a 64-bit integer as eight bytes or 64 flags: compare, test and count many elements per ordinary integer instruction, with `ctz`, `popcount` and parity finishing the job.
Why it is faster: A byte loop runs one compare and one branch per byte. Loading 8 bytes, XOR-ing and counting trailing zeros finds the first difference in one step per 8 bytes, about 8x fewer iterations and branches. Bit sets packed in words let one load and one mask test several bits. Popcount gives rank over 64 bits in one instruction. No vector ISA is needed, so it is portable.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: longest-common-prefix and string diffing via a `BigUint64Array` view; bitset permission checks. • Data / databases: bit-sliced or bitmap indexes, rank/select structures, word-at-a-time key comparison in B-tree pages. • Embedded: bit-plane lookup tables and flag words tested with one mask; `__builtin_popcount` maps to one instruction on most cores.
Watch out: endianness changes which end of the word holds the first byte; RocksDB restricted its fast path to little-endian. Use `memcpy` for unaligned loads to avoid undefined behaviour, and handle the tail.
Seen in RocksDB: b55b2f45d0 2019-09-05 DynamicBloom sets and tests two bits per 64-bit word (`1<<(h&63) | 1<<((h>>6)&63)`) and derives further probes by rotating the hash: add latency 65.6 to 44.4, for about 1.12x higher FP rate. • 746909ceda 2020-11-03 Ribbon filter's interleaved solution layout computes each result bit with AND plus parity over a few contiguous words: outside-query time 117.8 to 42.3 ns. • ce0fff9f41 2026-02-26 Succinct trie index: rank via popcount with a lookup table sampled every 256 bits. 41e5eb1a4d 2026-03-31 moved `NextSetBit`/`PrevSetBit` into the header and unrolled the `Rank1` popcount loop, the hottest function. • db114777b1 2026-09-14 `Slice::difference_offset` compares 8 bytes at a time via `memcpy` into `uint64_t` and finds the byte with `__builtin_ctzll(x ^ y) >> 3`, little-endian only, with a byte loop for the tail.

### rocksdb-132 — Batch independent lookups and group them by locality
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Accept many lookups as one request, sort or group them by the container they fall in (file, block, partition), and do each container's fixed work once per group instead of once per key.
Why it is faster: Per-lookup overhead (finding the file, searching an index, loading a filter, function calls) is shared across all keys in the same container, and touching a container once keeps it in cache for its neighbours. A batch also exposes independence that later stages can exploit with prefetching and parallel I/O. Batching pays when keys have locality or the per-container work is large; with tiny batches it adds sorting and bookkeeping for little gain.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: DataLoader-style batching that groups ids by shard or table before querying; batched N-API or FFI calls instead of per-item crossings. • Data / databases: sort a batch of B-tree probes so neighbouring keys reuse the same leaf; index nested-loop joins on sorted probe keys. • Embedded: group table lookups by flash page so each page is read once. • Native / systems: batch syscalls (`readv`, io_uring) and hash-table lookups per request.
Watch out: batching adds latency for the first key while waiting for the rest, and results must be returned in the caller's order. Keep per-key state in fixed-size stack arrays to avoid allocating per batch.
Seen in RocksDB: fbd2dafc9f 2014-09-25 An early read-only `CompactedDBImpl::MultiGet` with prefetching: 3M to 4.9M lookups/s. • fefd4b98c5 2019-04-11 New batched `MultiGet`: keys grouped per SST file, and `BlockBasedTableReader::MultiGet` and `FullFilterBlockReader::KeysMayMatch` run once per batch; per-key state in stack arrays (batch 32). 10-25% lower latency with locality, for example stride 16, batch 32: 2.753 to 2.135 us/op. • 63ace8ef0e 2019-06-10 Reuse the data-block iterator when the next key is in the same block: 326,122 to 332,945 ops/s. bae6f58696 2020-04-28 touch each filter partition once per batch: 597,517 to 752,963 ops/s, about 25%, even for random keys. • e10570331d 2019-08-28 row cache supported in batched MultiGet; 6c7b1a0cc7 2019-11-12 batching extended across column families. • bd16531142 2026-08-25 One top-level filter-partition iterator per batch instead of one per key. • d9a441113e 2024-03-12 The Java `multiGet()` now calls the batched C++ MultiGet instead of looping single gets across JNI, picking up its gains ("consistent improvement" in JMH).

### rocksdb-133 — Software-pipeline batched probes: prefetch all, then probe
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: For a batch of independent memory-bound lookups, run two loops: the first computes every address and issues a prefetch, the second does the actual probes, so all cache misses are in flight together.
Why it is faster: Probing one key at a time pays a full miss per key, back to back, because the CPU's out-of-order window is too small to reach the next key's load. Prefetching every key's line first lets the memory system serve up to about ten misses in parallel; by the time the second loop runs, most lines are in cache. The gain grows with miss rate and with the number of lines a probe touches. On data already in cache it costs a few extra instructions per key.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: hash-join probe phases that prefetch a group of buckets, then compare (as in DuckDB or Velox); batched index lookups. • Native / systems: packet-classification tables in DPDK processing a burst of packets; BFS frontier expansion. • Web / backend: in WASM or native addons, batch hash-map lookups over arrays of keys instead of one call per key.
Watch out: the batch must be large enough to cover latency but small enough that prefetched lines are not evicted before use (tens of keys, not thousands). Prefetches to wrong addresses waste bandwidth silently.
Seen in RocksDB: c83b085770 2014-06-12 Prefetch Bloom filter data for L0 files. • fefd4b98c5 2019-04-11 `FullFilterBitsReader::MayMatch(num_keys, ...)` computes hashes and calls `FilterPrepare`, which prefetches both ends of each key's cache line, for every key before calling `HashMayMatch`. • 4c49e38f15 2019-10-10 Same two-loop structure for the memtable `DynamicBloom::MayContain(num_keys, ...)`, plus `MemTable::MultiGet`: 1-5%. • e4f1e64c30 2021-02-10 Prefetching for batched Ribbon filter queries, which often span more than one line: batched queries ~222 to ~97 ns/key; cold single queries ~333 to ~226 ns.

### rocksdb-134 — Prefetch addresses you know before you need them
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When the address of an upcoming access is computable early and the pattern is irregular, issue a software prefetch as soon as the address is known, then do other work before using it.
Why it is faster: Hardware prefetchers follow sequential and strided streams but cannot guess hashed or data-dependent addresses. A prefetch instruction starts the miss early and does not block, so the latency overlaps with computation. It only helps if there is enough independent work between the prefetch and the use, and if the address is right.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: prefetch B-tree child nodes once the separator search picks them; prefetch hash buckets while hashing the next key. • Native / systems: prefetch the next block in checksum, compression or copy loops over large buffers. • Embedded: on cores with cache and a prefetch instruction (Cortex-A, some Cortex-M7), prefetch lookup-table entries computed from input.
Watch out: prefetching data that is already cached, or too early (evicted before use), costs instructions and bandwidth for nothing. Verify the addresses in a test; a bad prefetch never fails visibly.
Seen in RocksDB: 90d8355075 2017-02-28 `DynamicBloom::Prefetch` had prefetched `data_[b]` (a bit index) instead of `data_[b/8]`; a wrong address makes the prefetch useless. • 25403c2265 2018-06-28 Unaligned filter data means a probe's span can straddle two lines; two prefetches (start and end of the span) before the probe loop "reduced filter lookup overhead by 19.4%". • f059c7d9b9 2019-11-13 Cache lines are also prefetched while building the filter. • a378a4c2ac 2019-09-12 ARMv8 CRC32C prefetches the following block: 811.9 to 1,648.5 MB/s on 500 MB inputs. • 746909ceda 2020-11-03 Ribbon construction prefetches coefficient and result rows (`Prefetch(Index i)`). • b550fc0b09 2022-06-14 On arm64, the prefetch locality hint was raised to 3 so lines land in L1: skiplist fillrandom +10 to 15% (see per-ISA intrinsics).

### rocksdb-135 — Bound each lookup to one cache line
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Design probe sequences and records so a typical lookup touches a single cache line: all probes of a key in one 64-byte block, a whole handle within one line, short linear probing before jumping elsewhere.
Why it is faster: A lookup's cost is roughly the number of distinct lines it misses on. A classic Bloom filter's k probes hit k random lines; confining them to one block makes it one miss, with a small false-positive penalty that a few extra bits recover. Keeping a cache handle within one line means one miss reads both its key and state. Linear probing reuses the line just loaded, while double hashing jumps to a new line each step.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: split-block Bloom filters; bucketized cuckoo tables with 4-8 slots per line; B-tree nodes sized to lines or pages. • Web / backend: open-addressing caches with small inline entries rather than pointer-chained buckets. • Embedded: lay out lookup tables so one query's entries share a line or a flash read unit.
Watch out: line-local designs slightly raise false-positive rates or clustering; tune with real data. The structure must be allocated line-aligned, or a "one-line" probe straddles two (RocksDB had to prefetch both ends because filter data was unaligned).
Seen in RocksDB: 0d755fff14 2014-03-28 "Cache friendly blocked bloomfilter" (`bloom_locality`); 462796697c 2014-06-02 blocks equal to `CACHE_LINE_SIZE`, with byte rotation after each probe for a better FP rate. • f059c7d9b9 2019-11-13 `FastLocalBloomImpl` puts all probes in one 64-byte line, so a query costs one miss. • 7555243bcf 2022-10-18 Making the fixed-size cache key itself a 128-bit bijective hash freed space so `ClockHandle` stays within one cache line. • f6cb763409 2023-09-22 AutoHCC probes linearly for at most 4 slots before a golden-ratio double hash; full double hashing was rejected because losing locality hurt steady state. Growth-phase throughput went from 25,832 to 748,400 ops/s.

### rocksdb-136 — Prefetch the next hop in pointer chasing
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: While examining a linked node, prefetch the node it will move to next, so the following hop's miss overlaps with the current comparison.
Why it is faster: Linked structures (skiplists, trees, lists) serialize misses: the next address is unknown until the current node arrives. Prefetching `next->next` as soon as `next` is known starts the following miss one step early, hiding part of it behind the key comparison. Placing the key in the same allocation as the node also means the line fetched for the link already holds the key.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: skiplists and linked lists in in-memory stores; kernel list walks; tree descent in allocators. • Data / databases: prefetch the next leaf during B+-tree leaf-chain scans. • Web / backend: in WASM or native graph traversals, prefetch neighbour records while processing the current one.
Watch out: prefetching a null or freed pointer is harmless on most CPUs, but dereferencing it to compute the address is not. The win shrinks when nodes are already cached, as in small or hot structures.
Seen in RocksDB: c90d446ee7 2014-04-04 Hash-linked-list memtable nodes store the key immediately after the node, which helps prefetch. • 75f7f42d41 2017-10-04 `InlineSkipList` issues `PREFETCH(next->Next(level))` in `FindGreaterOrEqual`, `FindLessThan`, `EstimateCount` and `FindSpliceForLevel` (also prefetching level-1 when recomputing splices): fillrandom 314,804 to 360,087 ops/s.

### rocksdb-137 — Raise TLB reach with huge pages for large random-access structures
Lens: cpu-microopt · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Back large, randomly probed in-memory structures (filters, hash indexes, arenas) with 2 MB pages, and fall back to normal pages if they are unavailable.
Why it is faster: Every memory access needs a virtual-to-physical translation. With 4 KB pages, a few thousand TLB entries cover only megabytes, so random probes into a gigabyte table also miss the TLB and pay a page-table walk on top of the cache miss. 2 MB pages cover 512 times more memory per entry. A table sized far above what it uses has the same problem: sparse touches spread over many pages.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: JVM `-XX:+UseLargePages` or transparent huge pages for large heaps and in-process caches. • Data / databases: huge pages for buffer pools, hash tables and ML embedding tables; `MADV_HUGEPAGE` on large anonymous mappings. • Native / systems: DPDK hugepage mbuf pools; size hash tables near their real occupancy.
Watch out: reserved huge pages must be configured by the operator and are not swappable; allocation can fail, so a fallback is required. Transparent huge pages can cause latency spikes from compaction and inflate memory use for sparse data.
Seen in RocksDB: 7dafa3a1d7 2014-04-25 Huge-page TLB option for the dynamic Bloom filter, plain-table index and hash linked list. • 1410180167 2014-11-21 The arena allocates from huge pages and falls back to malloc. • e5b5f12b81 2016-07-26 `memtable_huge_page_size` extends huge pages from the memtable Bloom filter to the memtable arena. • 3182beeffc 2022-11-21 HyperClockCache warns when its table is sized wrongly, noting that a table that is too sparse costs TLB misses.

### rocksdb-138 — Submit every independent read before waiting on any of them
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When one request needs several reads that don't depend on each other, issue them all at once (vectored, async or thread-pooled) and then wait. Latency becomes about one round trip instead of N.
Why it is faster: Serial reads add up their latencies, and the device sits idle at queue depth 1. Fast SSDs, NVMe and remote storage reach full throughput only with many requests outstanding. Submitting in a batch also pays per-call overhead (syscall, boundary crossing, lookup) once instead of per item. The cost is more memory for in-flight buffers and more complex error handling, since some requests can fail or come back partial.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: `Promise.all` fan-out to storage or microservices; DataLoader-style batching instead of N+1 queries; pipelined Redis commands. • Native / systems: io_uring or libaio submission batches; `preadv`; batched N-API/JNI/FFI calls instead of per-item calls. • Embedded: queue several DMA descriptors before kicking the controller, instead of one transfer at a time. • Data / databases: issue reads for all shards or partitions of a scatter-gather query before merging.
Watch out: fan-out multiplies memory and device load. Cap the batch size, and keep a synchronous fallback for backends that don't support async or vectored I/O.
Seen in RocksDB: cee1e8a080 2015-08-11 Table files are opened in parallel on DB open and recovery. Reading footers and indexes is latency-bound I/O, so a thread pool overlaps it. 1518b733eb 2016-04-07 set `max_file_opening_threads` to 16 by default. • 0153e14569 2019-06-05 Added `Env::MultiRead()`. 7259e28d91 2019-06-30 had batched MultiGet read all the data blocks it needs through that one call. • e3a82bb934 2019-12-07 `PosixRandomAccessFile::MultiRead` on io_uring, with a thread-local ring, `io_uring_prep_readv`, `io_uring_submit_and_wait` and handling of partial results. 4fc216649d 2020-03-20 and 91b7553293 2020-05-14 extended it to direct I/O. • 8ea0a2c1bd 2021-06-18 Secondary-cache lookups in MultiGet return future-like handles, and a single `WaitAll()` overlaps the latency of N misses. No regression without a secondary cache (1.55M to 1.56M ops/s). • 2db6a4a1d6 2022-05-20 Parallel seek: a merging iterator's Seek first submits async reads for every level (`Status::TryAgain` means "submitted"), then waits and builds the heap. It falls back to synchronous reads on error. • 5b5b011cdd 2023-09-18 In the two-phase async Seek, the block-cache lookup result from phase 1 is carried into phase 2 instead of probing again. • d9a441113e 2024-03-12 The same reasoning applied at a language boundary: JNI `multiGet()` now calls the batched C++ MultiGet instead of looping over single calls, with a "consistent improvement" across JMH benchmarks.

### rocksdb-139 — Suspend instead of block: keep many I/Os in flight per thread behind a synchronous-looking API
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Turn each blocking read into a suspension point (coroutine, async task) backed by an async I/O engine. One thread can then drive dozens of outstanding lookups without needing a thread per request.
Why it is faster: A blocked thread wastes its slot for the whole I/O latency, and adding threads costs memory and context switches. Suspension lets the same thread start the next lookup and resume the first one when its data arrives. For I/O-bound work, throughput then grows with queue depth. For CPU-bound work (everything cached), the extra frame and scheduler overhead is pure cost.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: async DB drivers in Node, Python asyncio and Rust Tokio. Await inline when already on the right executor (.NET synchronous continuations) instead of re-queuing. • Native / systems: io_uring-based file servers; C++20 coroutines over an event loop. • Data / databases: batched key-value lookups that keep NVMe queue depth well above 1.
Watch out: when data is mostly cached, coroutine frames and scheduling make things slower. Gate the feature, or use it only on paths likely to miss. Large buffers embedded in frames inflate every frame.
Seen in RocksDB: 8465cccde2 2022-03-10 POSIX `ReadAsync`/`Poll` on io_uring. c0e0f30667 2022-06-13 added `AbortIO` via io_uring cancel. • 57997ddaaf 2022-05-19 Coroutine MultiGet across the files of a level: I/O-bound remote storage went from 1,202 to 3,137 ops/s (about 2.6x). CPU-bound workloads used 6-15% more CPU. • bf4532eb5c 2022-08-04 Table-reader MultiGet split into a filter stage and a lookup stage, so filter probes decide early which keys need I/O. 35cdd3e71e 2022-08-19 extended the coroutines across levels, and 7b11d48444 2022-09-14 made this the default. • a4645e864d 2026-07-27 Coroutine Get and MultiGet (`SubmitReadAsync`): with direct I/O and a 50% cache hit rate, MultiGet went from 88K ops/s (sync) to 340K (10 jobs per thread). 88752298ff 2026-08-10 native `CoroDB` removed the callback/Baton adapter overhead. d0e6a4ca27 2026-11-03 added a dedicated READ thread pool. 87d442749a 2026-10-29 covered direct-I/O files, which had been read synchronously. • 4c38c45ac1 2026-10-27 `CoroDB::CoGet` awaits the child task inline when it is already running on the target executor, which saves two queue hops per call.

### rocksdb-140 — Sort pending reads by offset and merge neighbours into fewer, larger I/Os
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Collect the reads you know you will need, sort them by position, and merge ranges that touch or nearly touch (up to a gap threshold). Then slice the shared buffer.
Why it is faster: Each I/O has a fixed cost (syscall, device command, RPC for remote storage) that dwarfs the per-byte cost of a few extra KB. Merging adjacent ranges removes that fixed cost, and reading a small gap is often cheaper than issuing two requests. Sorting is what makes the merging possible. The trade-offs: bytes read in gaps are wasted, a merged buffer can force a copy, and memory has to be bounded for long scan plans.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: merge HTTP Range requests to object storage; plan Parquet/ORC column-chunk reads as a few coalesced GETs. • Native / systems: elevator-style request sorting in a block layer; merge adjacent page reads in a buffer pool. • Embedded: coalesce sector runs in an SD-card or NAND driver. • Data / databases: read a record's header and body (or value and checksum) in one pread.
Watch out: a gap threshold that is too large reads useless bytes and pins memory. Merging can also force a copy out of the shared buffer, which may cost more than the saved syscall for small, cached data.
Seen in RocksDB: 30a017feca 2018-01-05 BlobDB reads a blob value and its CRC in one read instead of two: 234 to 261 MB/s, p50 5.98 to 5.16 us, p100 787 to 190 us. • cddd637997 2019-12-16 `RetrieveMultipleBlocks` appends a block to the previous request when `prev_end == offset`. It does this only when no extra copy is forced, i.e. compressed blocks that get decompressed into fresh memory anyway. • b512f4bc76 2021-09-17 Blob reads for one MultiGet are batched through `MultiRead`. b92cef2d1d 2021-09-24 sorts them by offset because the reader merges only sorted requests. • 6aef1a05d6 2023-02-17 Before a scan that will touch every index partition, all partitions are loaded with one large read. Reads under 1 KB during compaction fell from 1119 to 242 (strace). • 618f660eab 2025-08-18 `io_coalesce_threshold` merges reads separated by small gaps. 16 KiB cut the read count from 7.68M to 0.91M. • 2b28885c80 2026-01-07 A centralized IODispatcher collects a scan plan's block reads, sorts and merges them, and submits them asynchronously. feffb67303 2026-02-02 replaced the iterator prefetch logic with it. 8f9cb1a708 2026-02-06 added a memory limit (+12% on 1 MiB scans). b6582c84bc 2026-10-28 added rolling prefetch windows. 2620c85638 2025-09-15 made MultiScan I/O async (48 to 54 ops/s I/O-bound). 023fbb074a 2026-06-01 skips the defensive sort when the ranges are already known to be sorted. • 8e1569b351 2026-08-07 Blob reads fetch only the requested byte range. 34d5074bfa 2026-09-16 coalesces lazy column resolutions across keys, so N reads become one MultiRead per file.

### rocksdb-141 — Detect sequential access and read ahead in growing windows
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When reads turn out to be sequential, replace many small reads with large prefetches into a buffer you own. Start small, double the window while the pattern holds, and turn it off again for random access.
Why it is faster: Scans that read one 4-16 KB block at a time pay per-request latency thousands of times. One large read amortizes that cost and lets the device stream. Growing the window exponentially means point lookups (one or two reads) never pay for a large prefetch, while long scans quickly reach the cap. Owning the buffer is necessary under direct I/O or remote file systems, where there is no OS readahead. The cost is wasted bandwidth and memory when a "sequential" pattern stops early.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: S3/GCS readers that widen range requests only after detecting sequential access; media players buffering ahead. • Native / systems: a userspace FS (FUSE) or object-store client implementing its own readahead heuristic; `BufferedInputStream` sized to the pattern. • Embedded: flash readers that prefetch the next pages only once a linear walk is detected. • Data / databases: buffer-pool read-ahead triggered by sequential page access, carried across extents.
Watch out: readahead on random re-seeks wastes I/O and memory, so require evidence of sequential access first. Keep a cap so concurrent scans can't exhaust memory.
Seen in RocksDB: 5f4166c90e 2015-08-26 `ReadaheadRandomAccessFile` and `compaction_readahead_size` turn compaction's block-sized preads into a few large reads, even with OS readahead disabled. 9130873a13 2015-08-20 gave compaction inputs their own table reader. 24a24f013d 2016-05-04 exposed readahead for iterators. • 9d0b8f19d9 2015-11-17 The plain-table reader reuses the index-probe buffer for the data read, removing a duplicate pread (checked with strace). 9a8e3f73ed 2015-12-23 keeps the two most recent read buffers. • d938226af4 2018-01-25 After more than 2 reads to the same file, readahead doubles from 8 KB to 256 KB: seekrandom with 10000 nexts went from 29 to 114 ops/s (about 3.9x). 7103559f49 2018-06-21 did the same for direct I/O with `FilePrefetchBuffer`, without re-reading overlapping chunks: 26 to 117 ops/s. a71e467381 2018-06-23 added readahead for BlobDB GC. • 3548e4220d 2019-04-26 User readahead goes through `FilePrefetchBuffer` instead of new table readers: 28-30% faster buffered, 50-67% faster direct. 24b118ad98 2019-06-19 unified compaction and user readahead. 22028aa9ab 2019-06-21 made compaction honour the exact configured size. 0f4d90e6e4 2019-07-16, c129c75fb7 2019-07-19 and e1c468d16f 2019-08-16 added readahead to sequential files, log reading and `VerifyChecksum()`. c2485f2d81 2020-08-27 uses an internal prefetch buffer when the FS reports no readahead support. • a0e0feca62 2021-04-28 Prefetch only when reads are actually contiguous (tracked by previous offset and length). This fixed large readahead on MultiGet re-seeks. • 17ce1ca48b 2021-11-10 `adaptive_readahead` carries the grown window across files of a level instead of restarting small, and shrinks it by 8 KB on cache hits. 0c7f455f85 2022-04-15 and 4cd16d65ae 2022-09-01 made the initial size and trigger count configurable. 8353ae8b27 2022-06-15 doesn't prefetch until `num_file_reads` is above 2, which helps short scans.

### rocksdb-142 — Double-buffer sequential reads so I/O overlaps consumption
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: While the consumer drains buffer A, an async read fills buffer B. Start the next async read as soon as a buffer empties.
Why it is faster: Plain readahead still stalls the consumer every time the buffer runs dry. Ping-pong buffers hide the I/O latency behind the time spent processing the previous chunk, so a scan runs at max(compute, I/O) instead of compute plus I/O. Issuing the refill the moment a buffer frees up, not after the next copy, removes the remaining bubble.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: streaming CSV/Parquet readers with a background read thread; chunked downloads that fetch chunk N+1 while parsing N. • Embedded: DMA ping-pong buffers for audio, sensors or display. • Native / systems: video and audio ring buffers; tape-style backup readers.
Watch out: it doubles buffer memory per stream. Mixing synchronous and asynchronous fills on the same buffer is an easy source of correctness and performance bugs.
Seen in RocksDB: 49a10feb21 2022-03-21 `FilePrefetchBuffer` asynchronously prefetches readahead_size/2 into a second buffer. 63e68a4e77 2022-04-11 applied this to explicit readahead_size too. • 03fc43976d 2022-09-12 Issues `ReadAsync` on the first buffer as soon as it empties, and splits the readahead across both buffers on seek. • d1aca4a5ae 2022-11-11 and bec4264813 2022-12-21 fixed regressions, including one from mixing sync and async reads in the same prefetch buffer.

### rocksdb-143 — Never read past what the query can use: clip prefetch and skip blocks using bounds and cache state
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Use the caller's limits (upper bound, prefix, declared scan ranges) and what is already cached to shrink every read to the bytes that will actually be consumed and are actually missing.
Why it is faster: Readahead and block-by-block iteration are blind: they fetch the next block even when the scan is about to stop, and they re-read blocks that are already in memory. Index separator keys and first-key metadata can show that a block lies beyond the bound before it is read. A cache probe per upcoming block costs nanoseconds, while a wasted read costs microseconds.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: size HTTP range requests to the visible viewport; stop paginated prefetch at the LIMIT boundary. • Native / systems: map or tile viewers fetching only missing tiles; video players that stop buffering at a clip boundary. • Data / databases: zone maps and min/max pruning in Parquet; partition pruning from declared predicates; partial CDN fills for only the missing byte ranges.
Watch out: the trimming logic has to be exactly as correct as the bounds semantics. An off-by-one excludes data. Per-block cache probes are cheap but not free on fully cached workloads.
Seen in RocksDB: a30a696034 2017-05-05 `KeyReachedUpperBound` compares the index key with `iterate_upper_bound` so the next data block is never read. 92ee3350e0 2018-06-19 keeps the block iterator after going out of bounds, so repeated seeks into the same block don't re-fetch it. • 7891af8b53 2017-10-17 `ReadOptions::table_filter` skips whole SSTs based on table properties (an incremental backup went from "hours to seconds"). 408205a36b 2018-06-26 uses the prefix bloom when `[key, upper_bound)` shares a prefix. • f65a0379f0 2023-08-18 `auto_readahead_size` trims readahead to the upper bound, and e7c6259447 2023-12-14 made it the default. 58fc9d61b7 2024-10-17 also trims by prefix under `prefix_same_as_start`, at about -0.4% CPU. • 3d67b5e8e5 2023-09-22 Cache-aware readahead: before prefetching, walk the index for upcoming blocks, look each one up in the block cache, and trim cached blocks from the end of the range. c77b50a4fd 2023-12-06 trims both ends for async I/O and issues a zero-length read when everything is cached. • fe68fbcd7f 2025-07-21, 7c5c37a1a4 2025-08-04, b9951ded37 2025-11-18, e18d41e08c 2026-06-10, e492562651 2026-05-29, 798373975c 2025-09-19 For declared multi-range scans: normalize the ranges, prune scan options per level, prepare all iterators and start their I/O up front (about 20% lower latency), create sub-iterators lazily only for overlapping files (multiscan 19.1% faster), prune the last block using its first key, and unpin blocks of skipped ranges.

### rocksdb-144 — Tell the kernel how you will access a file, through the mechanism that actually serves the reads
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Declare random or sequential access, drop pages you won't reuse, and call OS readahead explicitly for known-sequential reads. Use the right API for the access method (`fadvise` for read(), `madvise` for mmap), sized within device limits.
Why it is faster: Default kernel readahead suits sequential streaming. Under random point reads it pulls in pages nobody will use, which wastes bandwidth and evicts the hot set. Correct hints make reads exactly as large as needed. Explicit `readahead()` lets the page cache do the prefetching without copying into user buffers. Hints that never reach the mechanism, or exceed what the device accepts, are silently ignored.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `MADV_RANDOM` for mmapped indexes or model weights; `posix_fadvise(SEQUENTIAL)` for log replay; `DONTNEED` after backups or log rotation. • Web / backend: stream large files through `sendfile` with sequential hints so they don't flush the page cache used by hot assets. • Embedded: match prefetch and DMA burst size to the controller's maximum transfer size. • Data / databases: different hints for the point-read path and the bulk-scan path on the same file.
Watch out: hints are advisory and platform-specific. Verify with strace or iostat that I/O sizes actually changed.
Seen in RocksDB: 4028ae7d31 2012-09-13 Readahead pages are not cached. 93f4952089 2012-09-13 switches off FS readahead for random reads. ad96563b79 2013-03-14 made buffered I/O, readahead and mmap configurable. • ab8d2f6ab2 2013-05-17 Changes the fadvise hint on an already-open table depending on whether it is used for Get or for compaction. • 87d6eb2f6b 2013-09-20 Env API to drop OS page cache for files whose blocks a compressed block cache already holds. d58ef52ba6 2016-12-29 SstFileWriter's `fadvise_trigger` drops written pages. • 602c13a964 2017-01-18 Removed fadvise calls on direct reads (a regression fix, since they are meaningless there). • 80afa77660 2022-06-10 then 69a32eecab 2022-06-15 For mmapped files the hint must be `madvise()`. Random advice was confirmed to produce smaller I/O sizes. • 629605d645 2023-07-21 Compaction reads call FS `readahead()` explicitly. fce04587b8 2023-09-26 and 719f5511f6 2023-09-26 fall back to internal prefetching only when the FS lacks support. 05daa12332 2023-08-30 set default compaction readahead to 2 MB. f69a5fe8ee 2024-11-18 caps readahead by the device's `max_sectors_kb`, because the kernel had been silently ignoring larger requests.

### rocksdb-145 — Fetch a file's metadata region in one read, sized exactly
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When opening a file means reading several metadata structures from a known region (footer, indexes, properties), read that whole region at once. Record its exact size at write time instead of guessing.
Why it is faster: Opening a file naively costs a chain of dependent small reads: footer, then meta-index, then each block it points to. One read that covers them all collapses the chain into a single round trip, which matters most on remote storage. Guessing low causes extra reads, and guessing high wastes bandwidth. A size recorded by the writer, or learned from recent opens, gets both right.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: read a ZIP central directory or a Parquet footer with one range request, and store the footer length so the guess is exact. • Native / systems: MP4 streaming with the moov atom size known up front; container formats that store index size in a fixed header. • Data / databases: cache block or row-group offsets at open so later misses need a single I/O.
Watch out: keep a compatibility fallback for files written without the recorded size, and make sure the fallback buffer really covers what follows.
Seen in RocksDB: 76f286cc82 2015-09-02 The filter block's location is resolved once at `Open()`, so a filter cache miss costs 1 I/O instead of 2 (meta block plus filter). • 6e8d6f429d 2017-04-14 Reads ahead backwards from the SST end for compaction. 666a005f9b 2017-08-11 prefetches the last 512 KB under direct I/O into a buffer that later reads consult first. • b010116d82 2018-01-10 Reuses the meta-index to read the compression dictionary, saving two disk reads at open. • b5613227a9 2018-07-19 Prefetches 4 KB instead of 512 KB when index and filter won't be read. 8425c8bd4d 2018-07-20 `TailPrefetchStats` remembers the last 32 effective sizes and picks the size with under 10% wasted read. • 4a4b8a1344 2020-05-12 sst_dump forces a 512 KB tail readahead so footer, metaindex and properties come from one I/O. • 8f763bdeab 2023-05-08 The writer records `tail_start_offset` in the table properties and manifest, and readers prefetch exactly the tail. 3093d98c78 2023-06-06 fixed an upgrade regression where a smaller fallback buffer caused extra partition reads at open (open-read p50 593 us versus 3.4 us).

### rocksdb-146 — Write back incrementally behind the writer instead of one giant sync at the end
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Ask the OS to start writing dirty data every N bytes, a little behind the current write offset, and cap how much unsynced data can pile up. The final sync is then cheap and the device queue stays smooth.
Why it is faster: A large file written to the page cache and then fsynced once becomes a burst of I/O. The burst blocks the writer, floods the device queue and spikes read latency for everyone else. Trickling write-back with `sync_file_range` spreads the work out. Lagging behind the head (about 1 MB) avoids flushing pages that are about to change again, and avoids filesystems that block writers on neighbouring pages.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: log shippers calling `fdatasync` every N MB; checkpoint spreading (Postgres `checkpoint_completion_target`). • Web / backend: flush network writers per message, not per chunk; stream uploads with periodic flushes instead of buffering whole files. • Embedded: dirty-page writeback thresholds on flash so a power-loss sync is short.
Watch out: `sync_file_range` doesn't make data durable. You still need the final fsync. Syncing too close to the write head can block writers on some filesystems.
Seen in RocksDB: 778e179046 2013-06-04 Incremental `sync_file_range` during compaction: 10-20% higher per-thread compaction throughput, and device await dropped from 3-10 ms to 1-3 ms. 3cc1af2062 2013-06-13 added the `bytes_per_sync` option. 5d0074c471 2014-08-12 set it to 1 MB when the rate limiter is on. • 4a855c0799 2015-05-18 A separate `wal_bytes_per_sync`, because foreground `sync_file_range` on the WAL could block writes. • 3dbf4ba220 2015-07-20 Syncs only up to end minus 1 MB, 4 KB-aligned (`kBytesNotSyncRange`), avoiding re-syncs of hot pages and XFS neighbour flushes. • 8272a6de57 2019-04-22 `strict_bytes_per_sync` waits on earlier ranges (`WAIT_BEFORE|WRITE`) to cap outstanding dirty data. • b2274da0e5 2019-05-21 The log writer flushes once per logical record instead of once per physical fragment.

### rocksdb-147 — Throttle and deprioritize background I/O to protect foreground latency
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Put bulk maintenance I/O (compaction, flush, deletion, backup) behind a rate limiter and a lower I/O priority, and keep it from queue-jumping user reads in device caches.
Why it is faster: Background work is throughput-oriented and doesn't care about latency. Left alone, it saturates the device and pushes user-read tail latency up by orders of magnitude. A token bucket smooths its bandwidth, OS I/O priority lets the scheduler favour foreground requests, and write-through keeps large writes from sitting in the device cache in front of reads. Total throughput barely changes while p99 improves sharply.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: throttle backup, replication and reindex streams; separate I/O budgets for batch jobs. • Native / systems: `ionice`/cgroups io.weight for maintenance daemons; rate-limited blob GC deletes. • Embedded: schedule flash garbage collection in idle windows with a bandwidth budget.
Watch out: throttle too hard and background debt piles up until writes stall. The limit needs to adapt to demand.
Seen in RocksDB: 1036537c94 2013-08-05 Soft and hard rate limits. 5ef1ba7ff5 2014-07-08 and 534357ca3a 2014-07-08 added a generic rate limiter integrated into flush and compaction. • 58c49466d2 2014-08-13 Background threads get lower I/O priority (ioprio). • 1026e794a3 2017-10-04 The rate limiter auto-tunes based on how often it drains. • b5c99cc908 2017-12-21 `FILE_FLAG_WRITE_THROUGH` for compaction output on Windows: P99.9 56.9 to 35.6 ms (-37%), and in a multi-tenant test P99.9 86.2 to 50.4 ms and P99 8.4 to 3.4 ms. • dae3b5545c 2019-03-28 WAL deletions go through the rate-limited DeleteScheduler, so TRIM bursts don't land all at once.

### rocksdb-148 — Bypass the OS page cache when you cache yourself or read data only once
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Use direct I/O (or explicit eviction) for data that the application caches on its own, and for read-once bulk scans. That avoids double caching and keeps background scans from evicting the hot working set.
Why it is faster: With buffered I/O, a block can live twice in RAM, once in the page cache and once in the application's cache. Sequential background reads also churn through the page cache and evict pages user reads depend on. O_DIRECT removes both problems, and the memory goes to the cache that knows the access pattern. The cost: aligned buffers, no free OS readahead, and per-request buffer management.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: O_DIRECT for engines with their own buffer pool; direct reads for backup, ETL and compaction scans. • Native / systems: video transcoders and streaming servers reading inputs once; `posix_fadvise(DONTNEED)` when O_DIRECT isn't possible. • Web / backend: analytics scans that use per-request arenas instead of populating a shared LRU.
Watch out: without your own readahead and buffering, direct I/O can be much slower. Small-hot-set workloads may prefer buffered reads. Alignment requirements spread through every buffer.
Seen in RocksDB: 36300fbbe3 2016-02-01 Windows unbuffered reads use per-request aligned allocations instead of a lock-protected per-file buffer (found from XPerf lock waits). • f89caa127b 2016-04-21 Direct I/O reads in Env. 972f96b3fb 2016-12-22 direct I/O writes. • 7affaee1c4 2026-06-09 `use_direct_io_for_compaction_reads`, with an ephemeral table-reader bypass so already-open buffered handles aren't reused: +22.3% throughput, P99 -28.5% and P99.99 -57.3% with a large hot set. • 82085868e2 2026-06-08 Caller-provided, scan-scoped aligned buffers: large scans decompress into caller memory and bypass the shared data-block cache, while index and filter caching stay.

### rocksdb-149 — Read straight into the final destination, or let the storage layer lend its buffer
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Skip bounce buffers whenever alignment or format already allows it. If the I/O layer has the bytes in a buffer it owns, return a view of that buffer instead of copying into caller scratch.
Why it is faster: A memcpy per block is pure memory-bandwidth overhead, and it shows up as several percent of CPU on read-heavy workloads. Direct I/O often reads into an aligned internal buffer and then copies. When the caller's buffer, offset and length are already aligned, or the data needs no transformation, the copy can go. Remote or distributed FS clients already hold the data in their own buffers.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: io_uring provided buffers; DMA into page-aligned user buffers; mmap-backed slices from an object-store client. • Web / backend: Node `Buffer.subarray` instead of `Buffer.from`; skip `Buffer.concat` when there is only one chunk. • Embedded: hand the DMA buffer to the parser instead of copying into a protocol buffer.
Watch out: a borrowed buffer ties the caller's lifetime to the I/O layer's. Release it promptly and never keep views past the owner's cleanup.
Seen in RocksDB: e78715c29a 2018-02-07 `ReadBlockFromFile` skips the staging stack buffer when the table properties say the file is uncompressed. • 0a0151fb99 2020-03-06 Direct-I/O `Read` returns a slice into its internal aligned buffer instead of memcpy'ing to scratch. 40497a875a 2020-04-24 `BlockFetcher` decompresses straight from `direct_io_buf_`, saving one memcpy per compressed block. • 749b179c04 2023-07-14 If offset, length and scratch are already aligned, read directly into them. `__memmove_evex_unaligned_erms` in perf fell from 8.73% to 2.29%. ed5b6c0d99 2023-09-11 stops aligning non-direct reads, which had been reading extra bytes. • fbd2f563bb 2023-06-23 `FSSupportedOps` capability flags let the FS provide its own buffer in MultiRead. b9cb7b9644 2024-01-29 used it for point lookups and non-prefetch reads. 26b480609c 2024-11-21 extended it to synchronous prefetching to "avoid an extra memcpy", and d386385e0b 2024-12-09 temporarily disabled that for compaction prefetches.

### rocksdb-150 — Preallocate and reuse file space so syncs don't update metadata
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Reserve a file's expected size up front (and trim the unused tail at close), or recycle already-written files. Each data sync then avoids a block allocation and an inode size update.
Why it is faster: Appending to a file changes its size and allocates extents, so every fdatasync must also journal metadata. Preallocation and reuse turn syncs into pure data writes, which can be 2-3x faster, and they reduce fragmentation. Speculative preallocation by the filesystem can waste gigabytes, so state the expected size explicitly and trim at close.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: preallocated WAL or journal segments (Postgres WAL recycling, SQLite `SQLITE_FCNTL_CHUNK_SIZE`). • Native / systems: preallocate video recording files; reuse Kafka-style segment files. • Embedded: circular flash logs with sequence-tagged records instead of create/delete.
Watch out: a recycled file contains old data. Records need a generation tag so readers don't accept stale tails. Over-preallocation wastes space unless it is trimmed.
Seen in RocksDB: 3dafdfb2c4 2013-01-15 fallocate for SST and log files: XFS had been speculatively preallocating 1 GB, and LOG went from 4 MB to 128 KB. 1ba5abca97 2013-03-13 followed up. • f26cb0f093 2014-03-17 fallocate without `KEEP_SIZE` makes fdatasync up to 2-3x faster, and native fallocate is preferred over `posix_fallocate`, which writes zeros. ad9a39c9b4 2014-03-26 preallocates the MANIFEST. • 26ac5603f4 2014-03-06 ftruncates unused space at Close. 44f0ff31c2 2014-10-29 uses `FALLOC_FL_PUNCH_HOLE` when ftruncate doesn't free it. 64ae6e9eb9 2014-03-28 stopped preallocating log files. • 666376150c 2015-10-01 Recycles WAL files: "the blocks are already allocated and fdatasync does not need to update the inode after each write" (543c12ab06 2015-10-07). a7b2bedfb0 2015-10-19 adds a log number to each record so stale tail records from a previous use are detected. • 7291a3f813 2018-01-22 fallocates compaction output to min(target size, total input), capped at 1 GB. 9272e12f19 2017-03-17 avoids a double ftruncate. • a3b0f187ff 2026-10-16 Skips WAL truncation on open when the FS never preallocates.

### rocksdb-151 — Use metadata you already have instead of asking the filesystem again
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Take sizes, existence and properties from your own manifest or from the producer that just wrote the file, list each directory once, and cache device facts. Don't issue per-file stat, open or metadata RPCs.
Why it is faster: Metadata syscalls look cheap but are serialized and often remote. Thousands of files times a stat or open each becomes seconds at DB open, and much worse on distributed storage. The application usually already recorded these facts when it wrote the file, so re-asking is pure waste. Opening files lazily for a focused job also avoids failing on unrelated files.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: avoid N+1 HEAD requests to object storage; ship content-length and ETag with uploads; lazy module loading. • Native / systems: build tools caching stat results (watchman/Bazel-style); `readdir` with `d_type` instead of stat per entry; cache device geometry in drivers. • Data / databases: data-lake manifests with file stats (Iceberg); serverless workers fetching only their partitions.
Watch out: trusting metadata means trusting its consistency. Keep a verification path (or a paranoid mode) for when the filesystem and the manifest disagree.

### rocksdb-152 — Skip writes that carry no new information
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Before appending, syncing or creating anything persistent, check whether it changes state. Empty files, unchanged configuration and redundant metadata records should cost nothing.
Why it is faster: Every persistent write costs I/O, often a sync, and on remote storage a slow RPC. Logs and metadata also grow and slow down recovery. A cheap "did anything change?" check before the write removes the whole cost, and the effect is largest on open, close and configuration paths that run frequently in automated fleets.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: idempotent configuration pushes that diff before applying; Kubernetes-style controllers that no-op unchanged specs; skip uploading empty telemetry batches. • Native / systems: skip fsync when nothing is dirty; don't rotate empty log files. • Data / databases: avoid zero-row partitions; skip checkpoint records when nothing changed since the last one.
Watch out: the "unchanged" check must be correct. Skipping a needed metadata record can break recovery. Test crash-recovery paths after adding such skips.
Seen in RocksDB: ccf0fcd5c2 2011-06-22 Removed the MANIFEST write on memtable/log switch: +10-20% fill throughput on ext4. • 4a27a2f193 2014-09-15 Doesn't sync the MANIFEST when data sync is disabled. 011241bb99 2014-09-05 Flush on an empty memtable returns immediately. e6acb874cd 2014-04-15 Doesn't roll empty logs. • c1b375e96a 2017-06-29 Compaction doesn't generate empty SST files. • e48ccc28f4 2025-01-02 Omits empty file-checksum entries. c9ce4a3d6b 2025-02-10 SetOptions skips the MANIFEST write. d375bbbbd0 2026-11-02 SetOptions returns early when no value changes: no new Version, SuperVersion or OPTIONS file. • 02a2b3501d 2026-05-07 `optimize_manifest_for_recovery` skips MANIFEST updates on a clean reopen. 60b34f30c4 2026-05-07, c7f7b1b2ae 2026-07-15 (redundant `DeleteWalsBefore`) and fe6406f7b2 2026-08-31 followed up. 62f05627be 2026-06-01 gives foreground MANIFEST batches 25% headroom so file rotation lands on background batches.

### rocksdb-153 — Pay for durability syscalls only where the filesystem needs them, and keep bookkeeping off the sync path
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Fsync is the most expensive call on the write path. Share one sync across files that need it, skip syncs the filesystem already guarantees, and keep tracking work out of the loop around each sync.
Why it is faster: A sync costs a device flush, often milliseconds. Directory fsyncs after create or rename are redundant on some filesystems (btrfs syncs the file's metadata with it), and one directory sync can cover many files. Any extra work bolted onto each sync multiplies across sync-heavy workloads.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: per-filesystem sync policies (as SQLite does); one directory fsync after a batch of renames. • Native / systems: skip fsync on tmpfs in tests or CI; keep audit writes off the fsync path in journaling systems. • Web / backend: update segment metadata only when a segment rolls, not on every append.
Watch out: filesystem-specific shortcuts need reliable detection and an override, because getting durability wrong loses data silently.
Seen in RocksDB: c5a64cffd2 2019-02-12 Atomic flush fsyncs a shared directory once instead of once per column family. • 29102641dd 2021-11-03 Skips the directory fsync on btrfs (`FsyncAfterFileRename`), with `IOOptions.force_dir_fsync` for deletes and multi-file renames. • b283f041f5 2022-07-12 Stopped tracking sync state of the live WAL: fillsync had fallen from about 1287 to 640 ops/s with that tracking, and the fix brought it back to about 1223.

### rocksdb-154 — Prove a read is unnecessary from metadata, and skip it
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Before reading data, check whether an index, a content-addressed name or a stored checksum already answers the question.
Why it is faster: The cheapest I/O is the one you never issue. Indexes can show a key is absent before you fetch auxiliary blocks. Names derived from content or session ids show two files are identical without hashing them. A separator choice that puts seeks in the right block avoids loading a neighbour.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: ETag/If-None-Match; HEAD-free uploads with known hashes. • Native / systems: rsync-style content hashes; dedup by content address in backup tools. • Data / databases: covering indexes; skip dictionary or codec loads for blocks you won't decode.
Watch out: metadata-based skipping is only as safe as the identity scheme. Names must uniquely identify content.
Seen in RocksDB: df38c1ce66 2019-04-22 Index shortening defaults to separators only, because shortened successor keys made seeks land in the previous block and load an unnecessary block. • 66942e8158 2020-06-01 MultiGet doesn't read the uncompression dictionary when the index shows the key is absent. • b923dc720b 2020-07-21 BackupEngine computes table checksums once, using DB session ids in file names. 9d8eb77c4d 2020-09-21 incremental backups no longer read table files that are already in the shared backup directory.

### rocksdb-155 — Shape I/O to the device's physical units: no straddling reads, full-stripe writes
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Pad logical blocks so a random read never crosses a page or storage unit boundary, and fill write buffers completely before flushing so the storage receives aligned, full-size writes.
Why it is faster: A 4 KB block that crosses a 4 KB page boundary costs two device pages. A block that crosses a "super block" of distributed storage costs two fetches. On erasure-coded or striped storage, partial writes trigger read-modify-write while full-stripe writes don't. A little padding space, or a short wait to fill the buffer, buys fewer physical operations.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: RAID full-stripe writes; sector-aligned records for direct I/O. • Embedded: write in NAND page multiples; align records to erase blocks. • Web / backend: S3 multipart uploads with exact part sizes; chunk alignment for range reads.
Watch out: padding costs space and conflicts with compression, which makes block sizes variable. Measure the space overhead ratio.
Seen in RocksDB: 0175d58c3c 2017-06-13 `WritableFileWriter::Append` doubles its aligned buffer up to the maximum before resorting to a flush, instead of flushing small direct-I/O writes early. • f9f4d40f93 2018-03-26 `block_align` pads data blocks so they don't straddle 4 KB pages (needs a power-of-two block size and no compression). • 742741b175 2025-10-01 `super_block_alignment_size` pads SSTs so no block splits across a storage super block, with no fillseq regression (202 versus 204 MB/s). • a1be80c5c2 2024-12-03 Tops up the write buffer with a partial append before flushing, so an erasure-coded distributed FS gets full-stripe writes.

### rocksdb-156 — Choose mmap or pread by concurrency and storage speed, by measuring
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Memory-mapped reads avoid syscalls and copies for a single thread, but page faults and address-space lock contention can cap throughput under many threads on fast storage.
Why it is faster: mmap turns a read into a memory access, so there is no syscall when the data is resident. A miss, though, is a page fault taken under the process's mmap lock, which serializes many threads and hides queue depth from the device. pread costs a syscall and a copy but scales across threads. Which one wins depends on residency, thread count and device speed.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: LMDB-style mmap for read-mostly, RAM-resident data versus pread for large, I/O-bound data. • Native / systems: memory-mapped asset packs in games (resident) versus streamed reads for large media. • Web / backend: Node streams versus mmap modules for large files under concurrency.
Watch out: benchmarks with a warm page cache flatter mmap. Test at production thread counts with a cold cache.
Seen in RocksDB: 9013f13b15 2012-03-15 Enabled mmap reads on 64-bit "to speed-up reads". • 33323f2111 2012-09-14 Removed mmap for random reads: a 24-core server couldn't exceed 10k IOPS at 32+ threads on storage capable of 100k+. • 7fdd5f5b33 2013-03-28 A non-mmap WAL with no regression (185k versus 180k ops/s fillseq). • b14c1f995b 2014-03-27 mmap reads and writes became an option again for RAM-resident formats.

### rocksdb-157 — Prepare the next file in the background so the switch is instant
Lens: io · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When the foreground will predictably need a new resource (next log file), create and open it ahead of time on another thread, and keep a synchronous fallback.
Why it is faster: File creation on remote storage takes a slow metadata round trip, and doing it on the write path stalls writers. Precreating moves that latency off the critical path entirely.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: warm connection pools; pre-forked workers. • Native / systems: pre-opened log segments (Kafka-style); pre-allocated next buffer in double-buffered loggers.
Watch out: clean up unused precreated resources on shutdown, and handle the race where the foreground arrives before the background finishes.
Seen in RocksDB: 21723bbbef 2026-05-15 `async_wal_precreate`: `SwitchMemtable()` takes a prepared WAL and falls back to synchronous creation if none is ready.

### rocksdb-158 — Hold the lock only to snapshot or publish state; do I/O and heavy work outside it
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Under a shared mutex, take references or swap pointers and nothing else. File I/O, sorting, allocation, object construction and expensive calls happen before acquiring or after releasing.
Why it is faster: Every microsecond inside a global lock is serialized for all threads, and one slow operation in there (a file open, a MANIFEST write) turns into a latency spike for everyone. Moving work out shortens hold times, so contention drops super-linearly and tail latency collapses. The trade-off is careful reasoning about what the lock protects: prepared state can go stale, so work outside the lock is often protected by finer reservations (file numbers, pending-output markers) and published with a short re-lock.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: build the response or serialize JSON before taking a cache lock; never make HTTP or DB calls inside a transaction or critical section; don't `await` network I/O while holding an async mutex. • Native / systems: allocate before `spin_lock`, free after unlock; compute derived structures off-lock and publish with a pointer swap. • Embedded: keep ISR-shared critical sections to a few loads and stores; prepare buffers before disabling interrupts. • Data / databases: do catalog I/O before taking the metadata latch; install results with a short re-lock.
Watch out: state read outside the lock may change before you publish. Validate on re-acquire, or reserve what you need (ids, markers) while holding the lock.
Seen in RocksDB: e3584f9c28 2011-08-22 Get no longer holds the DB lock while searching memtables. 7263023651 2011-09-01 no mutex while writing the log or MANIFEST: average latency 42 to 24 us, p99.99 32000 to 130 us, max 48000 to 7000 us. • 95dda37858 2012-10-31 File-size sorting moved outside the mutex, and only the first few files per level are sorted (1500 to 1700 writes/s). 5e9f3a9aa7 2013-09-17 the vector-memtable sort moved out of the DB lock ("upwards of 200 MB/sec"). ecd8db0200 2013-03-25 general minimization of mutex code. • beeb74be6f 2013-11-01 MANIFEST I/O outside the lock. 299f5c76bb 2013-11-22 creates the new WAL file and allocates the memtable outside the mutex. e8ab1934d9 2013-12-12 `NewInternalIterator` does only a Ref under the lock. ea0198fe9a 2014-04-03 `log::Writer` construction ("can take hundreds of ms") outside the mutex. ca250d71a1 2014-10-15 and 7661e5a76e 2014-12-16 file copies for trivial moves outside the mutex. • d8c8f08c12 2015-06-08 GetSnapshot and ReleaseSnapshot allocate and free outside the DB mutex. 7731d51c82 2015-01-06 removed a spinlock from the write path by doing column-family create/drop in the write thread. • c78a87cd71 2022-05-24 LRU handles store `total_charge` at insert, so `malloc_usable_size()` is no longer called inside the cache mutex (22ff8c5af7 2022-10-27 made that call valid). • 0cc0543893 2022-02-07 The file-location hash is built in `PrepareForVersionAppend` without the DB mutex. df5dc73bec 2021-07-16 block-cache stat scans no longer hold the DB mutex (P100 write latency had been 5 s under a slow scan). 9d77bf8f7b 2022-08-05 fragments range tombstones "without holding mutex". 4207872fc3 2022-06-20 removed a duplicated consistency check in release builds to cut DB mutex time. •
