write queue and moved a log warning outside the mutex. • 0df5500d35 2026-11-17 FIFO temperature-change file copies (open on a colder tier, copy every byte, fsync), which could stall the DB for seconds, run unlocked. 6e74afd550 2026-11-17 table opens for properties are allowed only for callers not holding the DB mutex.

### rocksdb-159 — Defer frees, destruction and logging until after unlock, or hand them to a background thread
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Under the lock, collect what must be freed, deleted or logged into a local list or buffer. Act on it after releasing the lock, or on a background thread so user threads never pay.
Why it is faster: Destructors, frees of large structures, file deletion and log formatting can each take milliseconds. They happen at the end of operations, often still inside the lock. Deferring them turns hold time into "unlink a pointer" and moves the expensive part to a point where nobody is waiting. It is the same idea as RCU's deferred free.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: async log appenders; defer `close()` and `unlink()` of large temp files to a worker. • Native / systems: RCU `call_rcu`; free lists drained after unlock; destructor work queued to a reaper thread. • Embedded: log into a ring buffer from critical code and drain it from a low-priority task.
Watch out: deferred work needs a bounded queue and has to be drained at shutdown. Deferred deletion of files that are still referenced must keep the reference counting correct.
Seen in RocksDB: 4c107587ed 2012-10-21 Obsolete files are deleted outside the mutex. fa46ddb41f 2013-10-07 `Unref` is split into a cheap part (inside the lock) and an expensive part (outside). 27bbef1180 2013-11-25 frees obsolete memtables outside the mutex. 8ce5492623 2014-04-25 deletes the SuperVersion and logs outside the mutex. • 444cf88a56 2013-11-07 and 19f5463d3f 2013-12-10 Info-log flushes moved out of the lock and off foreground threads. ecb1ffa2a8 2014-03-04 compaction-picker logs go to a `LogBuffer` that is flushed after unlock. 2abb592688 2015-05-22 no logging under the mutex during recovery. bea5831bff 2022-05-23 three more info-log calls moved into a LogBuffer. 9da88a8321 2018-10-30 removed info logging inside the DB mutex in `EnableFileDeletions`. • fd3dbef22b 2015-03-30 Old log files whose destructors call `ftruncate` are cleaned up on background threads. 120bc4715b 2019-04-01 `avoid_unnecessary_blocking_io` moves file deletions from iterator and CF-handle destruction to background threads. • d12691b86f 2017-07-27 `TableCache::EraseHandle`, about 86% of post-compaction DB-mutex hold time (found with LTTng tracing), moved from `~Version` to `PurgeObsoleteFiles`, outside the mutex.

### rocksdb-160 — Group commit: one leader performs the serialized step for all waiting writers
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When many threads each need an expensive serialized operation (log append plus fsync), let the first arrival become leader, combine everyone queued behind it into one operation, then wake the followers.
Why it is faster: N fsyncs become 1, so throughput scales with the number of writers instead of being capped at 1/fsync-latency. Followers should join without touching the global lock, and the leader should hold the lock only to collect pointers. Bigger groups mean fewer IOPS, so incompatible requests should be set aside rather than ending the group.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: WAL group commit (Postgres, MySQL); batched MANIFEST or journal records. • Web / backend: coalesce concurrent small writes into one DB transaction; batch Kafka producer sends per partition. • Embedded: NIC TX batching; flash-page write combining in a log. • Native / systems: logging frameworks batching appends from many threads into one write.
Watch out: a single writer pays a little extra coordination, and huge groups can add latency for early arrivals. Cap the group size, and make sure one failing member can't poison the others.
Seen in RocksDB: d79762e273 2012-03-08 Leader/follower `Write` with `BuildBatchGroup` and per-writer condvars: "drastically speeds up multi-threaded synchronous write workloads". 1ca0584345 2012-10-19 also commits MANIFEST updates from concurrent compactions as a group. • 7d9f21cf23 2014-01-14 The leader collects only pointers under the lock and does the memcpy and malloc after releasing it. • b7198c3afe 2015-08-05 Writers join the next group through a lock-free list and wait on a per-writer primitive, with no DB mutex: +33% at 8 threads (CPU-bound, no WAL), about 2% even single-threaded. • 6ce42dd075 2015-11-06 Merging the group's batches into one WriteBatch happens only in the WAL step and is skipped when the WAL is off (about 5%). • 6f71d3b68b 2016-02-05 Writes that carry callbacks (optimistic transactions) also join groups: run all callbacks, then write only the survivors. • 5aaef91d4a 2016-07-05 Several flush results are committed in one `LogAndApply`, one record and one sync. • 1fa5dff7d1 2024-02-27 Incompatible writers (sync versus non-sync, WAL versus no-WAL) move to a side list that is grafted back later, so the leader "can take as more commits as possible" to cut IOPS.

### rocksdb-161 — Shard contended state, and choose the shard without creating new contention
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Split a structure protected by one lock into independent shards selected by hash, current CPU or a thread-local random number. Size the shard count to the core count and capacity.
Why it is faster: Independent shards multiply available concurrency. Shard selection itself must not contend: a shared round-robin counter bounces a cache line, and modulo costs a division, so use the hash's top bits, `sched_getcpu`, or a thread-local PRNG with multiply-shift range reduction. Too few shards keep contention, and too many tiny shards hurt eviction quality or waste memory. Defaults have to be revisited as core counts grow.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: sharded in-process caches in Go or Java (ConcurrentHashMap segments, bigcache); sharded rate-limiter buckets. • Native / systems: per-CPU free lists and slab caches; multiple allocator arenas; striped lock tables. • Embedded: per-core queues on multicore MCUs instead of one locked queue. • Data / databases: partitioned lock managers; sharded buffer-pool hash tables.
Watch out: operations spanning shards (global size, eviction fairness) get more expensive. A thread can migrate between CPUs mid-operation, so record which shard an element came from.
Seen in RocksDB: e3584f9c28 2011-08-22 Block and table caches sharded 16 ways by top hash bits (`hash >> (32 - kNumShardBits)`). a2a0e358cb 2012-05-16 configurable shard count. e5fe80e4e3 2012-08-29 sharding capped at 2^20. • 7d87f02799 2015-08-14 `ConcurrentArena`: cache-line-padded per-core shards picked by thread-local cpuid, with `try_lock` and a re-pick on contention. • 2d75cd40d3 2017-01-27 Default shard bits derived from capacity (each shard at least 512 KB, at most 6 bits). 1518b733eb 2016-04-07 default shard bits 6 for less lock contention. • 3c17930ede 2023-04-04 Default block cache 8 MB to 32 MB, which raises default shards from 16 to 64, citing mutex contention on machines with more than 100 threads. • 925d8252e5 2023-05-01 jemalloc nodump allocator spread across `num_arenas`, chosen with a thread-local `Random` plus `FastRange32`: MyRocks query latency -73% (37.1 to 10.2 s) for +3% RSS. • 17bc27741f 2023-05-24 `Striped<T>`: lock stripes in one contiguous, optionally cache-aligned array that instances can share (each OptimisticTransactionDB had allocated 1M mutexes individually), indexed with `FastRangeGeneric` instead of modulo. • e38f68d6ce 2026-10-23 `ShardedSnapshotList`: 16 cache-line-aligned shards picked by current CPU with round-robin fallback, and each element records its shard. At 16 workers, 0.586 to 2.956 Mops/s (+404%), futex wait -99.96%. 16 shards beat 32 by about 11.7%.

### rocksdb-162 — Give each independently written hot variable its own cache line
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Pad or align per-shard, per-core and heavily written fields (counters, mutexes) to cache-line boundaries so different cores don't invalidate each other's lines.
Why it is faster: Cores keep coherence per 64-byte line. Two atomics written by different threads on the same line ping-pong it between caches, costing roughly 100 cycles per write even though the threads share no data. A busy mutex next to read-mostly fields invalidates every reader on each lock and unlock. The price is memory per padded object, and over-alignment that allocators like `make_shared` may not honour.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Java `@Contended` and `LongAdder` cells; padded per-worker stats in Go. • Native / systems: `alignas(64)` per-thread data; Rust `crossbeam::CachePadded`; per-CPU structures in kernels. • Embedded: on multicore SoCs, separate DMA descriptors and flags written by different cores.
Watch out: padding inflates memory and can hurt locality for read-mostly data. Apply it where profiling shows coherence traffic. Some CPUs prefetch adjacent lines, so 128-byte separation is sometimes needed.
Seen in RocksDB: 4e91f27c3a 2014-01-23 A plain array of atomic tickers caused a large regression. fb84c49a36 2014-01-29 fixed it with `struct Ticker { atomic value; char padding[64-sizeof] }` in an aligned(64) array. • 16e0388205 2017-07-24 `LRUCacheShard` declared `ALIGN_AS(CACHE_LINE_SIZE)`, allocated via `cacheline_aligned_alloc`. e367774d19 2017-08-14 overloads `new[]` for the shard array. • 346d1069c3 2018-06-25 `StatisticsData` aligned to the cache line rather than size-padded. 7f850b889d 2018-06-27 removed over-alignment on `StatisticsImpl`, because `std::make_shared` doesn't honour it. • 9983eecdfb 2022-02-27 `CacheAlignedInstrumentedMutex` gives the DB mutex its own line after `mutex_` and `block_cache_tracer_` bounced between writers and readers. 3ae9c5309b 2022-04-05 dropped the explicit padding, since sizeof already includes alignment padding.

### rocksdb-163 — Publish an immutable, refcounted snapshot of shared state, and cache it per thread
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Bundle the pointers a reader needs into one immutable object. Readers grab it with one reference, and writers build a new one and swap it in. A per-thread cached copy validated by a version number removes even that lock from the read path.
Why it is faster: Without a snapshot, each read takes the global mutex to ref several components. With one, the read takes one atomic, and with a thread-local cache usually only an atomic swap and a version compare. Writers pay for building and publishing new snapshots and for scraping stale per-thread copies. Reads vastly outnumber writes, so the trade is lopsided in favour of reads.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: RCU-style config or routing tables swapped atomically in proxies and services; immutable state snapshots (Redux-like) read without locks. • Native / systems: kernel RCU; `std::atomic<std::shared_ptr>` config objects; per-thread cached epoch pointers. • Data / databases: MVCC catalog snapshots; versioned file-set objects in storage engines.
Watch out: old snapshots live until their last reader releases them, so long readers pin memory. Per-thread caches complicate teardown.
Seen in RocksDB: 1fdb3f7dc6 2013-12-20 SuperVersion, a refcounted bundle of memtable, immutable memtables and version: readrandom 771,301 to 1,219,803 ops/s (+58%). • b2795b799e 2014-02-25 `ThreadLocalPtr` storage. ad0c3747cb 2014-02-27 caches the SuperVersion per thread: Get does an atomic `Swap(nullptr)`, compares `version_number`, and takes the mutex only when stale. The installer `Scrape`s all thread-local copies.

### rocksdb-164 — Lock-free readers through immutable nodes and release/acquire publication
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: If published data is never modified or freed while readers may see it, readers need no locks. Writers initialize fully, then publish with a release store, and readers load with acquire.
Why it is faster: On x86, acquire loads and release stores compile to plain moves, so readers pay nothing beyond normal memory access and scale perfectly. The cost moves to the write side: a single writer or CAS, and memory reclaimed only when the whole structure dies (arena-backed) or through epochs. Fixed-size arrays of atomics give lock-free shared tables, with a rarely used overflow structure under a lock.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: append-only in-memory logs read by many consumers; read-mostly routing tries in proxies. • Native / systems: seqlock-protected snapshots; lock-free ring buffers for logging. • Embedded: single-producer lookup tables read from ISRs via atomic pointer publish. • Data / databases: commit-status tables in MVCC engines.
Watch out: memory reclamation is the hard part. Without arenas or epochs you get use-after-free. Get the memory orders wrong and you have bugs that only show up on weakly ordered CPUs such as ARM.
Seen in RocksDB: 85584d497e 2012-04-17 The inherited LevelDB skiplist: "reads progress without any internal locking". Nodes are immutable after linking and allocated from an arena freed only with the memtable. This is what let e3584f9c28 2011-08-22 search memtables without the lock. • fbfa3e7a43 2017-08-26 The snapshot list is split into a cached atomic array plus a normally empty vector. 09713a64b3 2017-09-13 WritePrepared lock-free `CommitCache`, where each entry is one atomic uint64. • fce6c892ab 2017-09-08 `max_evicted_seq_` advances in coarse steps to cut update frequency. b225de7e10 2018-04-02 a smallest-uncommitted shortcut avoids CommitCache lookups. 0999e9b79a 2018-03-26 enlarged the cache from 2^21 to 2^23 entries.

### rocksdb-165 — Pack an entry's whole concurrent state into one atomic word
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Put reference count, eviction metadata and state bits into a single 64-bit atomic. Then each common operation (lookup, release) is exactly one atomic add, and rare transitions are one CAS.
Why it is faster: Several separate atomics or a lock per operation cost several cache-line round trips and create windows that need extra synchronization. One word means one coherence transaction and atomic consistency of all fields for free. Encoding the refcount as acquire minus release counters even turns "hit" bookkeeping (a CLOCK bump) into a side effect of taking a reference. Tolerating rare benign races (duplicate entries, slight overshoot) is what makes the design wait-free.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: concurrent caches in JVM or Go services packing version and refcount in a 64-bit atomic; JS `SharedArrayBuffer` slots updated with `Atomics`. • Native / systems: tagged pointers and state machines in lock-free queues; GPU hash tables where only 64-bit atomics are cheap. • Embedded: RTOS object pools with refcount and state in one word for ISR-safe updates.
Watch out: bit budgets overflow (counters need wraparound handling), and the protocol is subtle. Exhaustive stress testing and explicit memory orders are mandatory.
Seen in RocksDB: 4cc37f59e5 2016-08-19 The first ClockCache packs in-cache bit, usage bit and refcount into one atomic, with a concurrent hash map for lookup. • efdb428edc 2022-07-15 lock-free Lookup and Release. 6a160e1fec 2022-07-25 a fully lock-free ClockCache. • 5724348689 2022-09-16 The revamped HyperClockCache word holds acquire and release counters (refcount = difference, CLOCK countdown = min(3, acquires)) plus state bits. Lookup went from about 4 atomic updates to 1, and Release from read plus update to one update. Insert claims an empty slot with an optimistic atomic OR, and spins and yields were removed. 8c0f5b1fcf 2022-11-17 marked it production-ready. • fe3405e80f 2023-09-01 AutoHyperClockCache shares the low-level protocol, is "essentially wait-free" (about 1 yield per second across 16 threads) and tolerates rare duplicates. 387cb4aae7 2026-01-05 added a `BitFields` atomic API for such low-lock code.

### rocksdb-166 — Use the weakest atomic operation that is still correct
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Counters need atomicity, not ordering, so use relaxed operations. Where a lost update is acceptable, use load plus store instead of read-modify-write. Before an RMW that may change nothing, load first and skip it.
Why it is faster: Sequentially consistent operations add fences, and locked RMW instructions (`fetch_add`, CAS loops) take the line exclusive and serialize. Relaxed loads and stores are plain moves. A check-before-`fetch_or` keeps the line in shared state when bits are already set, which stops concurrent writers from ping-ponging it. Implicit conversions can silently use seq_cst, so require explicit orders.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: relaxed metric counters in C++ and Rust; test-before-set on visited bitmaps in parallel BFS and on GC card tables. • Web / backend: approximate request counters (lazySet/`setRelease` in Java). • Embedded: avoid full barriers on weakly ordered MCUs for flags that only need atomicity.
Watch out: relaxed ordering gives no happens-before. Never use it for flags that publish other data. Lossy load+store is only fine for statistics.
Seen in RocksDB: df22e2fb71 2015-09-15 Statistics tickers use `fetch_add(memory_order_relaxed)`. 54f6b9e162 2016-03-11 histogram buckets, sum and count become relaxed atomics, removing a lock. • e9f91a5176 2017-06-29 `fetch_add` only for stats that are really updated concurrently, relaxed load and store elsewhere: 68,618 to 69,579 tps (69,200 with fetch_add everywhere). 5de98f2d50 2017-08-11 `HistogramStat::Add` replaced `fetch_add` and CAS min/max loops with relaxed load and store. • b55b2f45d0 2019-09-05 Concurrent bloom add: `if ((mask & ptr->load(relaxed)) != mask) ptr->fetch_or(mask, relaxed)`. • 65cde19f40 2023-11-08 A `std::atomic` wrapper that forces every access to name its memory order. It fixed places where implicit conversions had silently used seq_cst.

### rocksdb-167 — Cut shared refcount traffic: borrow, share one owner, or replicate per core
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Every `shared_ptr` copy or cache Ref/Release is an atomic on a shared line. Use raw borrows when an owner guarantees lifetime, one refcounted owner for N views, or per-core replicas of a pointer that everyone takes.
Why it is faster: Refcount increments from many threads on the same object serialize on one cache line, and per-item Ref/Release on a cache handle can also take a shard lock. Borrowing removes the atomics entirely. A single shared owner turns N lock-taking refs into N cheap relaxed increments, or none. Per-core replicas give each core its own counter, so readers never bounce lines.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: borrow `&T` instead of cloning `Arc<T>`; pass `const T&` or a raw pointer, not `shared_ptr` by value; Linux `percpu_ref`. • Web / backend: one refcounted network buffer with many views (Netty, Tokio `Bytes`); per-thread config handles in high-QPS servers. • Data / databases: pin a decompressed page once and share it among the rows returned from it.
Watch out: raw borrows need a documented owner that outlives every user. Per-core replicas multiply update cost and memory.
Seen in RocksDB: 3dff28cf9b 2021-03-15 `SystemClock*` instead of `std::shared_ptr<SystemClock>` in low-level routines: readrandom 735K to 872K ops/s. 9fdc9fbeea 2021-02-26 did the same in the perf step timer. • 9d0cae7104 2022-04-26 MultiGet takes one cache handle per block and wraps its release in a small relaxed-refcounted `SharedCleanablePtr`, instead of one Ref per merge operand (it avoids `std::shared_ptr`'s extra control-block allocation). 504fe4de80 2022-08-04 GetMergeOperands pins one SuperVersion/MergeContext bundle shared by all returned slices. • 7555243bcf 2022-10-18 Cache shards stopped each holding a `shared_ptr<SecondaryCache>` copy, removing refcount traffic. • f291eefb02 2022-09-13 A `CoreLocalArray` of `shared_ptr` to the cached tombstone list, so each core bumps its own counter. It beat an otherwise identical version without per-core replicas on multi-threaded reads. Rebuilds use double-checked locking. 9d77bf8f7b 2022-08-05 had noted that a shared_ptr per write "would slow down multi-thread read performance".

### rocksdb-168 — Try the cheap unsynchronized path first; take the lock only when the check fails
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Do the operation optimistically (atomic flag check, naive pass, lock-free grab), validate, and fall back to the careful or locked algorithm only on the rare miss. Bound the retries so progress is guaranteed.
Why it is faster: If the fast path succeeds 99% of the time, the average cost is close to the unsynchronized cost. An atomic "enabled" flag read before a mutex makes a disabled feature nearly free. A small filter can even rule out the lock for most callers. A last-try-takes-the-lock policy keeps the worst case bounded.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: optimistic concurrency in ORMs (version check, retry, then pessimistic lock); feature flags read atomically before any locking. • Native / systems: seqlock readers; double-checked initialization; RCU readers with a locked slow path. • Data / databases: optimistic B-tree latch coupling with restart on version change.
Watch out: a fast path that silently fails every time (a bug) is slower than no fast path. Monitor how often the fallback runs.
Seen in RocksDB: b9d6eccac1 2019-01-02 Lock-free MultiGet: grab SuperVersions per CF from thread-local caches, check that no memtable was sealed after the snapshot, retry up to 3 times, and lock the mutex on the last attempt. Batch-size-1 MultiGet reached 5.68M ops/s versus 5.98M for readrandom with 32 threads. • a720401877 2018-06-13 `SyncPoint`'s `enabled_` became an atomic bool checked before `mutex_`. Taking that mutex had been most of db_stress CPU. cda7923169 2021-05-27 a bloom filter lets most sync points skip the mutex entirely. • 48e7effa79 2019-03-25 `ReleaseSnapshot()` checks a global threshold before looping over every CF inside the DB mutex. • fe3405e80f 2023-09-01 AutoHCC Lookup does a naive pass that "will almost always find entries quickly", falling back to the careful algorithm only when not found. 1f7a286413 2026-07-01 fixed chain indexing in that fast path, which had been forcing the full fallback.

### rocksdb-169 — Let writers insert into a shared structure in parallel, or buffer privately and merge once
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Instead of serializing all inserts behind one writer, use a CAS-based structure with per-core allocation. If the structure can't be concurrent, have each writer fill a private buffer and merge it once per batch.
Why it is faster: A single-writer memtable caps writes at one core. Lock-free CAS inserts let all cores insert, as long as allocation also scales (per-core arena shards) and shared counters aren't touched per key. Thread-local buffering reduces contention from per entry to per batch. Both cost a little when there is no parallelism.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: lock-free ordered maps (Java `ConcurrentSkipListMap`); per-worker bump allocators. • Web / backend: per-thread log or metric buffers flushed to a shared sink. • Data / databases: per-thread insert buffers merged into a shared index; worker-local histograms merged periodically.
Watch out: CAS loops degrade under extreme contention on the same region. Private buffers delay visibility until the merge.
Seen in RocksDB: 7d87f02799 2015-08-14 `InsertConcurrently` on the inline skiplist with CAS on links and `max_height_`, a per-core `ConcurrentArena`, and statistics moved earlier so each write takes the DB mutex once instead of twice. Neutral without parallelism, always faster with more than one thread. 972e3ff295 2016-11-16 made it the default. • c8aafdba33 2025-06-18 Vector memtable concurrent writes: each writer appends to a thread-local buffer merged at the end of the batch. 5.05M ops/s at 1 thread to 21.5M at 8 (4.3x).

### rocksdb-170 — Pipeline the stages of a serialized write path
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Split one leader doing "log, then apply" for a whole group into separate queues per stage. The next group's log write then overlaps the previous group's apply, and phases the caller proves unnecessary are skipped.
Why it is faster: With a single queue, group N+1 waits for group N's memtable insert before starting its WAL write, so the two latencies add. Separate stage queues turn the path into a pipeline whose throughput is bounded by the slowest stage instead of the sum. Separating "last allocated sequence" from "last visible sequence" lets apply run in parallel with logging.
Applies elsewhere: Data / databases: separate log append from state-machine apply in Raft; group commit plus asynchronous apply. • Native / systems: split send and receive queues; CPU instruction-pipeline style staging in packet processors. • Web / backend: durable enqueue first, process in a separate stage.
Watch out: pipelining complicates visibility and recovery ordering. Readers must not see stage-2 results before stage 1 is durable.
Seen in RocksDB: e9e6e53247 2017-04-13 The last finished writer exits the group, and marking the log synced runs in parallel with memtable insertion. • 07bdcb91fe 2017-05-19 `PipelineWriteImpl` with a WAL queue and a memtable queue. • 499ebb3ab5 2017-06-24 Serial 2PC commits: the WAL buffer isn't flushed per write, and separate "last seq" versus "last seq for write" lets memtable writes run in parallel with the WAL (46k tps sysbench). • 17731a43a6 2017-11-01 The commit-time batch goes to the WAL only and is applied to the memtable before flush or recovery. a6d3c762df 2017-12-18 non-2PC WritePrepared commits in a single write.

### rocksdb-171 — Offer weaker ordering guarantees when the caller can accept them
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: If writers don't need to become visible in strict order, or the caller can guarantee there are no conflicts, let each writer proceed independently and skip the coordination phase.
Why it is faster: Strict ordering forces every group member to wait for the slowest, and makes the write path pay for conflict detection whether or not conflicts exist. Opting out removes the wait or the check entirely. The trade is a weaker guarantee (snapshot immutability, duplicate detection) that the application has to accept knowingly.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Kafka producers with `max.in.flight > 1`; eventual-consistency write paths for logs and metrics. • Data / databases: bulk-load modes that skip constraint or conflict checks the loader guarantees. • Native / systems: relaxed-ordering append queues where consumers sort later.
Watch out: make the weaker semantics opt-in and clearly documented. Silent reordering breaks read-your-writes assumptions.
Seen in RocksDB: 8feee28020 2018-02-06 and 8a04ee4fd1 2018-02-12 `skip_concurrency_control` and `skip_duplicate_key_check` hints for callers that guarantee no conflicts. • f383641a1d 2019-05-13 `unordered_write`: fillrandom with WAL went from 78.6 MB/s to 368.9 MB/s (4.7x), or 177.8 MB/s (2.2x) with WritePrepared transactions, which restore snapshot guarantees.

### rocksdb-172 — Accumulate shared updates privately and publish them in bulk
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Collect per-item deltas to shared counters or budgets in thread-private state, and publish once per batch or only when a threshold is crossed. Skip the publish entirely when the delta is zero or nobody reads the value.
Why it is faster: Per-item atomics on shared counters turn every concurrent writer into a cache-line fight. Batching divides the contention by the batch size. For budgets, an atomic counter plus a mutex taken only when the reservation must grow or shrink, with hysteresis to avoid flapping, keeps almost every update lock-free.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: per-request metric accumulators flushed at request end; token buckets with local credit. • Native / systems: per-P counters in Go; per-NIC-queue stats; GC allocation budgets reserved in chunks. • Data / databases: memory-quota accounting with chunked reservations.
Watch out: published values lag behind reality by up to one batch or threshold. Limits enforced from them can overshoot slightly.
Seen in RocksDB: 907f24d0e1 2016-07-07 Memtable counters accumulated in a thread-private `MemTablePostProcessInfo` and published once per write batch. The counters had been 5% of CPU, and writes rose 12% at 64 threads with batch 128. • e70ba4e40e 2016-07-08 Skips `num_deletes_.fetch_add` when the delta is 0. 6797e6ffac 2016-07-07 skips allocation accounting when no write-buffer limit is set: +10% parallel insert. • 05cdd2d92b 2026-11-12 `AtomicCacheReservationManager`: atomic usage plus a mutex only when the reservation crosses a threshold (release below 3/4, hold for about 1 s). The mutex version lost 90% of throughput at 64 threads (89K ops/s), while the atomic one is within noise (908K versus 913K). At 1 thread, 310K updates took the mutex only 21 times.

### rocksdb-173 — Match the waiting strategy to the expected wait: spin briefly, then block
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: For waits usually measured in microseconds, spin (or yield) for a bounded time before sleeping. Adapt based on whether spinning succeeds, use the right CPU spin hint for each architecture, and pick contention-friendly lock implementations.
Why it is faster: A futex sleep and wake costs several microseconds plus a context switch, which is more than many critical sections last. Spinning catches the release cheaply, but burns CPU if the wait is long, so bound it and measure it (count "slow" yields where another thread actually ran). Spin hints differ: on arm64, `yield` barely pauses while `isb` behaves like x86 `pause`. Under heavy contention, locks that let waiters spin on their own line and hand off the critical section beat generic mutexes.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: spin-then-park locks (parking_lot, WebKit WTF::Lock); per-arch `cpu_relax` in DPDK; MCS/CLH locks under contention. • Web / backend: JVM `Thread.onSpinWait`; Go and .NET runtime spin tuning on ARM servers. • Embedded: poll a peripheral briefly before switching to interrupt-driven waits.
Watch out: spinning on oversubscribed machines steals CPU from the lock holder. Always bound spinning and fall back to blocking.
Seen in RocksDB: d897d33bf1 2013-05-31 `use_adaptive_mutex` (`PTHREAD_MUTEX_ADAPTIVE_NP`): 8-thread CPU-bound random reads went from about 750k to 880k ops/s. • 7d87f02799 2015-08-14 WriteThread spins with yield up to `max_yield_usec`, treating yields longer than `slow_yield_usec` as slow and blocking after 3 of them. 972e3ff295 2016-11-16 made adaptive yield the default. 548b628054 2016-12-27 a compile-time switch to make every Mutex adaptive. • 63748c2204 2021-03-26 arm64 spin uses `yield` instead of `wfe`. 2e5a323dbd 2022-06-15 then switched to `isb`: about 2x on 64-thread fillrandom on Graviton3. • 1aac814578 2022-06-17 folly `DistributedMutex` in LRUCache: cache_bench +21% (1,584,062 to 1,919,135 ops/s), P99.9 683 to 60 us. • 5724348689 2022-09-16 The lock-free cache removed spins and yields altogether. 124dd30879 2025-07-23 removed a redundant yield in the point lock manager.

### rocksdb-174 — Wake only the waiters who can proceed, and skip signalling when nothing changed
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Broadcast only when state actually changed. Skip the unlock/relock dance when there is nobody to notify. Give each waitable item its own queue so a release wakes exactly the next waiter.
Why it is faster: A condvar broadcast on a shared stripe wakes every waiter, but only one can proceed. The rest reacquire the lock, find nothing, and sleep again (thundering herd). Spurious broadcasts also cause feedback loops of useless work. Per-key FIFO queues make a release O(1) wakeups.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: `notify_one` instead of `notify_all`; futex-per-object wait queues. • Web / backend: connection-pool waiter queues; avoid redundant `setState` or event emission when the value didn't change. • Data / databases: row-lock managers with per-row wait queues. • Embedded: raise IRQs or events only on state change.
Watch out: waking too few causes lost-wakeup hangs. Each waiter's condition must be re-checked under the lock.
Seen in RocksDB: 91ddd587cc 2014-06-02 `SignalAll` at the end of compaction only when work was done: "nothing to do" log lines dropped from 7435 to 372. • 25f70a5abb 2014-12-16 No unlock and relock when there are no event listeners. de9df6e818 2022-03-30 skipped the same unlock/relock when no listener is registered. • 1aca60c089 2025-09-08 `PerKeyPointLockManager`: each key gets its own FIFO waiter queue and condvar, so a release wakes only the next waiter. Deadlock detection is lazy (`deadlock_timeout_us`) to keep it cheap.

### rocksdb-175 — Fan out wakeups as a tree instead of one leader waking everyone serially
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: When one thread must release N parallel workers, have it wake about sqrt(N) helpers, each of which wakes its own stride of workers.
Why it is faster: Each wakeup is a syscall of a few microseconds. Serial wakeups by one leader delay the last worker by N times that. With a two-level tree the leader's serial work drops to about 2·sqrt(N), and later wakeups happen in parallel, which matters on machines with 100+ cores.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: tree barriers; waking many futures in an async runtime. • Web / backend: relay nodes for notifying many pub/sub subscribers. • Data / databases: releasing parallel query workers after a shared build phase.
Watch out: only worth it for large groups. RocksDB applies it only at 20 or more writers, because for small N the extra hop costs more than it saves.
Seen in RocksDB: 20777b96cb 2024-05-30 `SetMemWritersEachStride` and `STATE_PARALLEL_MEMTABLE_CALLER` for groups of 20 or more writers. On 160 vCPUs with concurrent memtable writes: fillseq 184%, fillrandom 182%, overwrite 179%, fillsync 136% of baseline.

### rocksdb-176 — Split a lock by role so unrelated paths stop contending
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: If different code paths touch different parts of the state, give each its own mutex (or route the work to the thread that owns that state). Take the global lock only for the rare events that span both.
Why it is faster: A DB-wide mutex makes writers, background jobs and readers of unrelated state wait on each other. Dedicated locks reduce the conflict rate to real conflicts. Holding a lock across a whole group of items, rather than per item, also amortizes acquisition.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: two-lock concurrent queues (head and tail locks); separate logging and state mutexes. • Web / backend: per-table instead of global locks in app-level caches; split a pool's free-list lock from its stats lock. • Data / databases: separate latches for the log buffer and the buffer pool.
Watch out: more locks means lock-ordering rules and deadlock risk. Document the order and the protected state for each lock.
Seen in RocksDB: c292dc8540 2019-06-10 WritePrepared: separate `prepared_mutex_` (RemovePrepared) and push/pop mutex (AddPrepared), acquired on the first callback of a write group and released on the last. • 1e9bf25f61 2022-07-21 WAL-related state is protected by a dedicated `log_write_mutex_`. The DB mutex is taken only on stall or buffer-full events.

### rocksdb-177 — Have state changes enqueue themselves instead of polling every object under the lock
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Recompute "is anything ready?" only when something changes, and keep ready items in a queue. The hot path then checks O(1) state instead of scanning all objects while holding the mutex.
Why it is faster: Scanning N column families on every write or schedule call makes the cost grow with N, and the scan usually runs under the global lock, so it serializes everyone. Event-driven queues make the common case constant time.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: dirty queues in UI frameworks instead of full re-render checks; job queues instead of polling tables. • Native / systems: epoll instead of polling all sockets; scheduler ready queues. • Embedded: event flags set by ISRs instead of polling every peripheral in the main loop.
Watch out: every state change must enqueue, or work is silently missed. Watch for duplicates in the queue.
Seen in RocksDB: a2bb7c3c33 2014-09-08 The write controller recomputes stall state only when a flush or compaction finishes, not by looping over every CF per write. • 3d9e6f7759 2014-09-10 A full memtable registers itself for flush, which removed `MakeRoomForWrite`. fdb6be4e24 2014-12-19 flush and compaction queues replace the per-CF loop under the mutex: fillrandom 37,105 to 57,456 ops/s, and 131,758 after the next bottleneck was removed. • d7a486668c 2014-12-10 GetSnapshot no longer scans all CFs.

### rocksdb-178 — Keep slow bulk work from blocking urgent work: separate pools, priorities and leases
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Run latency-critical background jobs (flushes) in their own pool, ahead of long jobs (compactions). Cap concurrency per resource class, cancel queued work that is no longer needed, and don't let one stuck candidate block a coordinator.
Why it is faster: In a shared FIFO pool, a short urgent job waits behind hours of bulk work (head-of-line blocking). If that job is a flush, writers stall. Separate pools and priorities bound the wait for urgent work. Per-class limits stop slow devices (HDD) from tying up threads. Leases with bypass keep one stalled worker from blocking everyone.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: bulkhead thread pools per endpoint class; separate Sidekiq/Celery queues for latency-sensitive and batch jobs; cancel queued work on shutdown or unmount. • Native / systems: I/O scheduler priority classes; GC concurrent marking threads at lower priority. • Data / databases: per-tenant or per-device concurrency limits on maintenance jobs; lease-based task assignment.
Watch out: starving low-priority work builds debt that eventually stalls the foreground. Priorities need limits, not absolutes.
Seen in RocksDB: 9e84834eb4 2012-09-19 Configurable background threads. 1ca0584345 2012-10-19 multi-threaded compaction. d0798f67f4 2013-03-20 schedules extra tasks so other threads find concurrent compactions. • 1565dab809 2013-09-12 LOW and HIGH pools. fa798e9e28 2013-09-13 flushes in a separate pool. 0b4ccf765c 2014-02-06 flushes always go HIGH. e48348d196 2014-04-07 with a single thread, flush runs inside compaction. • b2b3086524 2015-03-16 On close, pending flush and compaction tasks for the DB are unscheduled instead of waited for. • cc01985db0 2017-08-03 A BOTTOM pool for universal compactions involving the last level. 090c78a0d7 2018-04-24 lower CPU priority for background threads. • a8b9891f95 2018-12-13 `ConcurrentTaskLimiter` per CF group (HDD versus SSD): "30%+ HDD write throughput gain" and fewer stalls. • a38cd3a439 2026-10-07 Cross-DB flush selection runs attempts on a small executor with leases and moves on to the next candidate when a lease expires: with 1000 DBs, +61% throughput, -47.9% P99, -62.8% peak RSS. 9b4c5422f0 2026-10-07 consumes a completion generation recorded during dispatch, so a fast rejection isn't followed by a 10 ms sleep.

### rocksdb-179 — Share background threads across instances, create them lazily, and de-synchronize periodic work
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Use one global scheduler instead of threads per instance or per feature, start threads only when a feature is enabled, and give periodic tasks stable, spread-out phases so a fleet doesn't fire in lockstep.
Why it is faster: Hundreds of instances each with their own timer threads waste memory and scheduler time. Periodic jobs that all wake at the same instant contend on shared locks (allocator, I/O) and cause synchronized spikes. Staggered, well-spread phases turn spikes into a flat load.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: one cron per process instead of per tenant; jittered cache TTLs, certificate renewals and client retries. • Native / systems: shared timer wheels (Netty HashedWheelTimer); lazily started worker threads. • Embedded: one tick scheduler for all periodic tasks, with phase offsets.
Watch out: a shared scheduler is a shared point of failure. One slow task delays the others, so keep tasks short or hand them off.
Seen in RocksDB: ceeca7542d 2020-03-24 The DeleteScheduler thread is created only when a rate limit is set. • 9a83fd21e6 2020-07-17 Staggers the first `DumpMallocStats` across DBs, which removed jemalloc lock contention from the top of the profile. • 69760b4d05 2020-08-14 A global `StatsDumpScheduler` replaces 2 threads per DB. f7613e2a9e 2020-06-13 and ee50b8d499 2020-03-28 can lower background and backup thread CPU priority, down to idle. • e4a7136d3e 2026-08-26 Periodic compaction with a stable, well-spread preferred phase per instance. 7e0c4d8e11 2026-10-29 pulls work early by a bounded amount (`early_pull = offset*min(q, k*r/n)`) to spread catch-up evenly.

### rocksdb-180 — Pipeline CPU-heavy stages across threads with ordered output, but only when the work is big enough
Lens: concurrency · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Split a sequential job (build, compress, write) into stages joined by bounded queues. Keep output order with per-item slots, let idle threads help the bottleneck stage, and skip parallelism when per-item work is smaller than the hand-off cost.
Why it is faster: Compression or decompression often dominates the CPU time of building or scanning a file. Running it on N threads overlaps it with building and I/O. A rigid producer/worker/writer layout wastes CPU on hand-offs and idle waits. A work-stealing ring where any thread can do any pending stage cuts CPU and bounds memory. For very fast codecs the hand-off overhead exceeds the work, so a single thread is better.
Applies elsewhere: Native / systems: pigz-style parallel gzip; video encoders with ordered frame output; parallel image-tile encoding. • Data / databases: parallel Parquet page decoding during scans; ETL stages where idle threads help the slowest stage. • Web / backend: thresholds on `Promise.all` fan-out and parallel streams; skip thread pools for tiny batches.
Watch out: measure CPU time, not just wall time. Parallelism that doesn't shorten wall time is pure waste. Bound queue depth to cap memory.
Seen in RocksDB: 03a781a90c 2020-04-01 `CompressionOptions::parallel_threads`: a compress queue feeds a pool, a write queue of slots keeps block order, and a `block_rep_pool` reuses buffers. 1c78e4b235 2020-10-22 tidied it. • 53c39c2b01 2025-08-04 Cut allocations in `PartitionedIndexBuilder::AddIndexEntry`. e59bbd7241 2025-08-27 split index building into a parallelizable "prepare" and an ordered "finish", removing key stashing. 5c7162da27 2025-08-14 made decoupled partitioned filters the default to support this. • 29d9798ae8 2025-09-14 A bounded work-stealing ring with semaphores (the producer compresses when the ring is full, and workers prioritize writing): lz4 at 8 threads 176.71 to 129.06 cpu-s for similar ops/s; zstd 1.89M to 1.97M ops/s with CPU 214.87 to 171.56 cpu-s. • 01084e7e8e 2026-06-10 `GetRecommendedParallelThreads()` returns 1 for Snappy, LZ4 and negative-level ZSTD. LZ4 at 8 threads used 3.95 user-s versus 2.54 at 1, with no wall-time gain. • 8fa3ee8fdc 2026-11-03 Decompression of prefetched scan blocks fans out to a bounded worker pool, joined with an atomic counter and acquire/release state: about 2x on full table scans.

### rocksdb-181 — Profile to locate the real cost before optimizing, with the tool that fits the resource
Lens: observability · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Use CPU profiles and flame graphs for compute, tracing for lock hold time, strace for syscall and I/O counts, hardware counters for stalls and branch misses, and allocator stats for heap churn. Let the measurement pick the target.
Why it is faster: Intuition about hot spots is often wrong. The costs RocksDB removed were frequently invisible in code review: a destructor holding the mutex, a hidden memcpy, a TLS init call. Each resource needs its own lens. Throughput alone tells you that something is slow, and the right profiler tells you why. It costs setup time, and some tools perturb what they measure.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: continuous profiling (pprof, async-profiler, py-spy) in production; tracing spans around lock waits and DB calls. • Native / systems: `perf stat` for IPC and branch misses; `strace -c` and `bpftrace` for syscall counts; heap profilers for allocation churn. • Embedded: cycle counters and logic-analyzer GPIO toggles around suspected hot paths. • Data / databases: wait-event views; I/O size histograms from blktrace or iostat.
Watch out: profiles from synthetic benchmarks can mislead. Confirm with production profiles where possible, and watch for observer effects from heavy tracing.
Seen in RocksDB: 36300fbbe3 2016-02-01 XPerf lock waits led to per-request aligned buffers. d4540654e9 2015-04-29 CPU profiling showed GetApproximateSizes as a bottleneck, fixed with binary search over sorted levels. • 9d0b8f19d9 2015-11-17 strace confirmed that a duplicate pread was gone. 6aef1a05d6 2023-02-17 strace counted reads under 1 KB (1119 to 242). • 580fede347 2016-03-11 Frontend-stall cycles fell from about 79% to 31% after moving iterator stats to object-local counters. • d12691b86f 2017-07-27 LTTng tracing found that `TableCache::EraseHandle` took about 86% of post-compaction DB-mutex hold time. aed7abbcca 2018-05-17 esan cache-access profiling showed two InlineSkipList fields taking most accesses, so they were placed together. • 26293c89a6 2019-08-23 CRC loop unrolling was verified with branch-miss counts (739,426,504 to 1,128,572). • 749b179c04 2023-07-14 perf showed `__memmove_evex_unaligned_erms` at 8.73%, and 2.29% after the fix. bc0db33483 2023-07-13 `InternalKey::DecodeFrom` and its destructor showed up in a flame graph. b1b6f87fbe 2023-07-14 jemalloc stats counted about 578k 32-byte allocations removed. • ee305ac1c3 2026-10-21 Counting TLS-init calls in object files (40 and 96 down to 0) measured the overhead directly. These accessors were 0.3-0.6% of fleet CPU.

### rocksdb-182 — Count the mechanism, not just the outcome
Lens: observability · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Expose low-level counters such as key comparisons, checksum computations, I/O counts and redundant inserts, so an optimization can be verified by the work it removes, independent of throughput noise.
Why it is faster: Throughput moves by a few percent and is noisy. A comparison counter that drops by 25% is deterministic proof that the change worked, and it exposes regressions that throughput hides. Per-thread perf-context counters make this cheap enough to leave compiled in. Diagnostic warnings when a configuration estimate is wrong turn silent inefficiency into actionable signals.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: count DB queries, cache lookups and serializations per request in tests to catch N+1 regressions deterministically. • Native / systems: allocation and syscall counters per operation in microbenchmarks. • Data / databases: rows examined, pages read and comparisons per query exposed in EXPLAIN ANALYZE-style output. • Embedded: per-task counters of retries, DMA transfers and cache misses.
Watch out: counters on hot paths need to be thread-local and cheap, or they become the bottleneck. Fewer operations of a mechanism doesn't always mean faster, so also measure end to end.
Seen in RocksDB: d9dd2a1926 2013-08-12 A thread-local `PerfContext` for per-operation counters, with "no locking/atomic is needed". • 4a21b1402c 2016-12-01 Heap-root comparison caching verified by comparisons per full scan (2.0M to 1.0M). • 25d81e4577 2019-05-09 Comparisons per key in readseq went from 2.981 to 2.165, and to zero on a compacted DB. • e6be168aa5 2020-06-10 `user_key_comparison_count` 19,399,529 to 18,431,498. 82611ee25a 2020-07-09 28,881,965 to 27,823,245. • 249eff0f30 2020-04-27 `*.add.redundant` stats measure duplicate block-cache inserts from racing misses. • 3182beeffc 2022-11-21 Warnings when the cache's `estimated_entry_charge` is wrong: too sparse means TLB misses, too full means wasted memory. • b45738622a 2023-04-04 Block checksum compute count fell from 384,353 to 22 once user ReadOptions reached metadata reads.

### rocksdb-183 — Build workload-specific, low-noise benchmarks and add a workload whenever a new trade-off appears
Lens: observability · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Keep a configurable benchmark harness covering the workloads that matter (fill, random and sequential read, scan while writing). Add a new mode for each new trade-off, and use small focused harnesses for micro-level changes where full-system noise would hide the effect.
Why it is faster: An optimization that helps one access pattern often hurts another. Without a benchmark for the pattern being defended (scan resistance, codec setup cost), the trade-off can't be evaluated. Full-system benchmarks are too noisy for sub-percent changes. A focused harness reveals them, and occasionally shows that a "better" API is slower.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: load tests per traffic shape (read-heavy, write-heavy, scan); microbenchmarks (JMH, criterion, benchmark.js) for hot functions. • Native / systems: pin CPU frequency and isolate cores for low-noise runs; compare instruction counts, not just time. • Data / databases: YCSB-style mixes plus a custom mode for each new feature's adversarial case.
Watch out: benchmarks optimized in isolation can diverge from production. Re-validate with production workloads or traces.
Seen in RocksDB: 50c2dcb78f 2014-07-21 db_bench exposes `block_restart_interval` so format trade-offs can be measured. • bc7e8d472e 2018-05-24 The `readwhilescanning` benchmark was added alongside midpoint LRU insertion, to evaluate scan resistance. • 9d3afcf543 2025-10-02 A focused benchmark showed LZ4's basic compress API beating per-call streams, reused streams and extState, so RocksDB went back to the plain API. sst_dump `--recompress` serves as the low-noise harness, and e231c1826c 2026-09-15 revamped it. • a6af317476 2026-01-21 Fixed a `std::find` bug that selected the ZSTD digested-dictionary decompressor for LZ4 and used extra memory.

### rocksdb-184 — Treat performance regressions as bugs: detect, bisect and fix at the root
Lens: observability · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Compare every release and refactor against a baseline. When throughput drops, find the commit and fix the cause, often by restoring a fast path or a field layout, rather than accepting the slowdown.
Why it is faster: Performance erodes one innocuous change at a time: a larger struct, a lost fast path, an extra virtual call, an incompatible format. Each is small, and together they are large. Regression tracking preserves the gains from every other technique. The fixes are usually cheap once the cause is known.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: performance budgets in CI (bundle size, p95 latency, query counts) that fail the build; bisect with `git bisect run` plus a benchmark script. • Native / systems: nightly benchmark dashboards with alert thresholds; struct-size static assertions on hot types. • Data / databases: cross-version compatibility tests that check performance-critical metadata (indexes, filters) is still used.
Watch out: noisy benchmarks produce false alarms. Use repeated runs and confidence intervals, and track mechanism counters that are deterministic.

### rocksdb-185 — Make instrumentation cost nothing when nobody is listening
Lens: observability · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Check the enable flag or level before reading clocks, formatting messages or calling hooks. Read the clock once per event even when several consumers need it, keep timing out of critical sections, and compile out rarely used hooks.
Why it is faster: A clock read costs tens of nanoseconds and string formatting much more. Done per key on hot paths, these become several percent of CPU even when stats are disabled. Timing inside a mutex lengthens its hold time. A cheap flag check up front removes all of it in the disabled case, and per-level granularity lets operators buy only the detail they need.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: guard `log.debug(expensive())` with `isDebugEnabled` or lazy suppliers; sampled tracing spans (OpenTelemetry samplers). • Native / systems: compile-time-disabled tracepoints; one `clock_gettime` shared by log and metric. • Embedded: `#ifdef` trace macros; a single timestamp per event for all consumers. • Data / databases: tiered statistics levels (counters only, plus timers, plus mutex timing).
Watch out: the flag check itself must be cheap and contention-free (a relaxed atomic or constant). Compiled-out instrumentation isn't available when you need to debug production.
Seen in RocksDB: 289efe9922 2013-07-09 Stats updated only if needed. 6db52b525a 2013-07-29 removed redundant `NowMicros()` calls. 5575316350 2014-01-07 StopWatch doesn't read the clock when stats are disabled. • 0f7f3b8605 2014-10-30 Checks `InfoLogLevel` before doing logging work. • fb9811ee9b 2016-01-21 A perf level that skips mutex timing. d20915d52a 2016-01-25 mutex-duration stats off by default through a stats level. f5177c761f 2016-06-13 FullMerge stops calling `ElapsedNanos` when stats are disabled. • f92cd2feb4 2018-05-09 `ROCKSDB_DISABLE_STALL_NOTIFICATION` compiles stall-notification structures out of the write path. • 7ccb35f653 2018-05-17 `InstrumentedMutex` takes one timing for both perf_context and statistics.

### rocksdb-186 — Accumulate statistics locally per operation and publish once
Lens: observability · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Count into plain fields of a long-lived object or a per-request context, and publish to the shared statistics object at the end, every N items, or on destruction. Report only the fields that can change.
Why it is faster: A global `recordTick` per `Next()` or per cache hit means an atomic on shared memory in the innermost loop, and it can wreck the front end of the CPU pipeline. Plain local increments are register operations. Publishing once divides the shared cost by the number of items, and an unconditional local increment is cheaper than branching on whether stats are enabled.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: request-scoped metric objects emitted at request end; batched StatsD/analytics flushes. • Native / systems: per-cursor row counters; per-frame counters in game engines. • Data / databases: per-query execution stats aggregated at query end.
Watch out: long-lived objects delay publication, so stats lag. Flush on a time or count bound for long scans.
Seen in RocksDB: d98e56315d 2013-05-10 atomic tickers allowed 43eef52001 2013-08-26 to move stats counting out of the mutex in Get (about 5% throughput). • 722d80c374 2014-08-28 The compaction loop counts drops in locals and calls RecordTick every 1000 keys. • 580fede347 2016-03-11 `DBIter` accumulates `LocalStatistics` and publishes them in its destructor: readseq 8.4M to 28.6M ops/s, readreverse 5.7M to 14.1M. • 51c2ea0feb 2017-12-12 Block-cache tickers accumulated in `GetContext` and reported once from `Version::Get`: `recordTick` 4.22% to 0.51% of CPU. f95a5b2464 2018-07-20 `GetContextStats` with 17 fields replaces a loop over all 100+ tickers per Get. • 01cfea6637 2019-04-18 `DBIter::Next` always does `local_stats_.next_count_++` instead of branching on `statistics_ != nullptr`.

### rocksdb-187 — Keep counters per thread or per core and sum them on read
Lens: observability · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Writes go to an uncontended per-thread or per-core slot, and readers fold over all slots. Use native thread-local storage, with constant initialization, so each access is a plain load.
Why it is faster: Statistics are written millions of times per second and read rarely, so moving the cost to the read side is a good trade. Per-thread slots make writes contention-free, but aggregation grows with thread count. Per-core slots bound it at the core count while keeping writes mostly uncontended. Generic TLS wrappers add a lookup per access, and C++ `thread_local` objects with destructors add an init-guard call, so the access mechanism matters too.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Java `LongAdder`; sharded Prometheus counters in Go. • Native / systems: Linux `percpu_counter`; `thread_local` trivially constructible counters instead of `pthread_getspecific`. • Embedded: per-core counters on multicore MCUs summed by a monitor task.
Watch out: reads are approximate while writers are active. Merge a thread's counts on exit or they are lost. Per-thread designs scale aggregation with thread count, which per-core designs avoid.
Seen in RocksDB: 6584cec8f2 2016-08-22 `ThreadLocalPtr::Fold`. 7c95868378 2016-08-24 tickers in per-thread slots with a `merged_sum` for exited threads: `recordTick` fell from 7.59% to 1.46% of CPU in 64-thread readwhilewriting. a081f798b0 2016-08-25 relaxed the read consistency. 1613fa9490 2016-08-31 did the same for histograms. • cda5fde2d9 2017-05-10 `CoreLocalArray` extracted from ConcurrentArena. ac39d6bec5 2017-05-23 moved statistics from thread-local to core-local slots. be421b0b16 2017-05-10 and a620966969 2017-05-09 made the physical core id portable. • 0025a36409 2017-06-26 perf_context and iostats_context back on native `__thread` after `ThreadLocalPtr` regressed. c319792059 2017-09-15 references perf_context directly internally. • ee305ac1c3 2026-10-21 A `constinit thread_local PerfContext*` set on first use removes the TLS-init call: 2.10 to 0.84 ns per counter update.

### rocksdb-188 — Collect expensive statistics in the background, tolerate staleness, and never under the main lock
Lens: observability · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Metrics that need a scan (cache contents, memory usage) should be computed periodically or on a budget and served stale. Skip dumping what didn't change, and make histogram updates O(1).
Why it is faster: Synchronous scans for each query or dump can take seconds over large caches, and if they hold a global lock they stall writers. Stale-tolerant background collection caps the overhead at a fixed CPU budget. Linear bucket search in histograms turns every latency sample into an O(buckets) loop.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: Prometheus collectors that cache expensive gauges; rate-limited health checks. • Native / systems: HdrHistogram-style O(1) bucket indexing; background memory accounting threads. • Data / databases: sampled table statistics refreshed asynchronously instead of exact counts on demand.
Watch out: label stale values as stale. Alerting on them can lag real incidents.
Seen in RocksDB: 009034cf12 2013-01-29 Histogram bucket lookup through a precomputed value-to-bucket map instead of a linear search. 97b30dee5b 2021-10-14 replaced the redundant map with `lower_bound` over bucket values. • d5a46c40e5 2021-06-14 Pins the cache-entry stats collector, whose eviction forced a full block-cache scan (about 20 s per 100 GB) per CF, and caps background scanning at 0.2% of thread time. 2f93a3b809 2021-06-08 fixed a `std::min`/`std::max` bug that defeated its result cache. • df5dc73bec 2021-07-16 Block-cache stat scans no longer hold the DB mutex. • 33ceea9b76 2022-10-20 A "fast" block-cache stats property with "much lower overhead at the expense of returning stale values". d989300ad1 2022-10-31 skips periodic dumps for unchanged CFs for up to 8 periods.

### rocksdb-189 — Keep test and debug instrumentation cheap enough that tests stay representative
Lens: observability · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Test hooks, sync points and debug assertions on hot paths must cost almost nothing when inactive. Otherwise debug builds and stress tests become too slow to explore realistic loads, and timing-sensitive bugs disappear.
Why it is faster: A `sleep(0)` syscall or an O(N log N) re-sort in an assertion, run per operation, can make debug builds 100x slower. Tests then run small workloads that never reach the interesting states. A mutex in a test hook distorts concurrency, and heap-allocating strings at every hook call thrashes the allocator.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: cheap guards around dev-only invariant checks in Redux middleware or React dev mode; sampled consistency checks. • Native / systems: fault-injection hooks behind a relaxed-atomic flag; tiered assertion levels. • Embedded: trace hooks that compile to a single flag test in release builds.
Watch out: fewer or cheaper checks can hide bugs. Keep expensive invariants available at a higher debug level rather than deleting them.
Seen in RocksDB: 08be1803ee 2015-04-13 Skip `SleepForMicroseconds` when the test delay is 0: debug fillrandom went from 8.5K to 780K ops/s. d41a565a4a 2015-04-13 removed `Valid()` asserts that re-sorted the vector memtable on every operation. • 236756f2cf 2016-08-16 SyncPoint returns immediately when disabled. a720401877 2018-06-13 its enabled flag became an atomic checked before the mutex, which had been most of db_stress CPU. • 678ba5e41c 2021-10-15 SyncPoint takes non-owning slices and compile-time-length literals instead of `const std::string&`: a load-and-iterate test went from 32 min 17 s to 1 min 14 s, and live heap objects from 5.1M to 105K.

### rocksdb-190 — Revisit defaults with measurements: defaults are performance features
Lens: observability · Source: RocksDB
Principle: Most users never change defaults, so periodically re-benchmark them against current hardware and workloads, and promote options proven in production.
Why it is faster: A default chosen years earlier (a cache size from 2011, an old codec, off-by-default async I/O) quietly limits every deployment that didn't tune it. Changing one default delivers an optimization fleet-wide with no user effort. It needs evidence that the new value doesn't regress the common case.
Applies elsewhere: Web / backend: re-tune pool sizes, timeouts and compression defaults in frameworks and internal libraries as hardware changes. • Native / systems: revisit inherited buffer sizes, thread counts and codec choices each major release. • Data / databases: promote proven opt-in features to defaults with a documented migration note.
Watch out: changing defaults affects users who relied on the old behaviour (memory, compatibility). Announce changes and keep an opt-out.
